\displaystyle 2q^{\nu_{2}}J_{2\nu_{2}}^{\nu_{1}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\left(q^{2}+m_{1}^{2}-m_{2}^{2}\right)J_{2}^{\nu_{1}}-\left(i/4\pi\right)q^{\nu_{1}}\log\left(m_{2}^{2}/\lambda^{2}\right)

(5.38)

\displaystyle g_{\nu_{12}}J_{2}^{\nu_{12}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle i/4\pi+m_{1}^{2}J_{2}-\left(i/4\pi\right)\log\left(m_{2}^{2}/\lambda^{2}\right).

Using the results above, we lead to the expression:

\displaystyle q^{\mu_{1}}(T_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}^{AV})_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2q^{\nu_{2}}\varepsilon_{\nu_{1}\nu_{2}}\Delta_{2\mu_{2}}^{\nu_{1}}+\frac{i}{\pi}\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}\nu_{1}}q^{\nu_{1}}

(5.39)

\displaystyle+\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}\nu_{1}}q^{2}(2J_{2}^{\nu_{1}}+q^{\nu_{1}}J_{2})+\left(i/4\pi\right)\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}\nu}q^{\nu}\log\left(m_{1}^{2}/m_{2}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle+\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}\nu_{1}}\{2(m_{1}^{2}-m_{2}^{2})J_{2}^{\nu_{1}}+q^{\nu_{1}}[4m_{1}^{2}-\left(m_{1}-m_{2}\right)^{2}]J_{2}\}.

The identity
\varepsilon_{[\nu_{1}\nu_{2}}\Delta_{2\mu_{2}]}^{\nu_{1}}=0
allows adjusting indices and recognizing one-point
functions together with relation for finite vectors and scalar two-point
integrals of two masses

q^{2}\left(2J_{2}^{\nu}+q^{\nu}J_{2}\right)=-q^{\nu}\left(m_{1}^{2}-m_{2}^{2}\right)J_{2}-\left(i/4\pi\right)q^{\nu}\log\left(m_{1}^{2}/m_{2}^{2}\right).

(5.40)

Doing it some more algebraic operations, we produce the result for this
contraction,

\displaystyle q^{\mu_{1}}(T_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}^{AV})_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}}q^{\nu_{2}}\Delta_{2\nu_{2}}^{\nu_{1}}+\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\nu_{2}}q^{\nu_{2}}(2\Delta_{2\nu_{1}}^{\nu_{1}}+i/\pi)

(5.41)

\displaystyle+2\left(m_{1}+m_{2}\right)\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}\nu_{1}}[(m_{1}-m_{2})J_{2}^{\nu_{1}}+q^{\nu_{1}}m_{1}J_{2}].

Recalling the
PV
functions of two masses and one-point differences means

q^{\mu_{1}}(T_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}^{AV})_{1}=T_{\left(-\right)\mu_{2}}^{A}-(m_{1}+m_{2})T_{\mu_{2}}^{PV}+\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}\nu_{2}}q^{\nu_{2}}(2\Delta_{2\nu_{1}}^{\nu_{1}}+i/\pi).

(5.42)

The contraction with the second vertex in the same version starts with

\displaystyle q^{\mu_{2}}(T_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}^{AV})_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}}q^{\nu_{2}}\Delta_{2\nu_{2}}^{\nu_{1}}-2\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}}(2q^{\nu_{2}}J_{2\nu_{2}}^{\nu_{1}}+2q^{2}J_{2}^{\nu_{1}})

(5.43)

\displaystyle-\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}}q^{\nu}[q^{2}-\left(m_{1}-m_{2}\right)^{2}]J_{2}

\displaystyle-\left(i/4\pi\right)\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}q^{\mu_{2}}\left[\log\left(m_{1}^{2}/\lambda^{2}\right)+\log\left(m_{2}^{2}/\lambda^{2}\right)\right];

here, the reductions occur directly, see
q^{\nu_{2}}J_{2\nu_{2}}^{\nu_{1}}.
Using (
3.67
), we get

q^{\mu_{2}}(T_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}^{AV})_{1}=-2\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}}q^{\nu_{2}}\Delta_{2\nu_{2}}^{\nu_{1}}-2\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}}\left(m_{2}-m_{1}\right)\left[\left(m_{1}+m_{2}\right)J_{2}^{\nu_{1}}+m_{1}q^{\nu_{1}}J_{2}\right],

(5.44)

where all the elements of the RAGF can be identified in the final result,

q^{\mu_{2}}(T_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}^{AV})_{1}=T_{\left(-\right)\mu_{1}}^{A}+\left(m_{2}-m_{1}\right)T_{\mu_{1}}^{AS}.

(5.45)

Note that RAGF is automatically satisfied and does not have an additional
term as (
5.42
).

Version two:
To the second one apply the same considerations:
Starting with
q^{\mu_{1}},

\displaystyle q^{\mu_{1}}(T_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}^{AV})_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}\nu_{1}}q^{\mu_{1}}\Delta_{2\mu_{1}}^{\nu_{1}}-\left(i/4\pi\right)\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}\nu}q^{\nu}\left[\log\left(m_{1}^{2}/\lambda^{2}\right)+\log\left(m_{2}^{2}/\lambda^{2}\right)\right]

(5.46)

\displaystyle-4\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}\nu_{1}}(q^{\mu_{1}}J_{2\mu_{1}}^{\nu_{1}}+q^{2}J_{2}^{\nu_{1}})-\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}\nu}q^{\nu}[q^{2}-\left(m_{1}+m_{2}\right)^{2}]J_{2}.

Reducing the integrals in a direct way as
q^{\mu_{1}}J_{2\mu_{1}}^{\nu_{1}}
and recognizing the terms follows

q^{\mu_{1}}(T_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}^{AV})_{1}=T_{\left(-\right)\mu_{2}}^{A}-\left(m_{1}+m_{2}\right)T_{\mu_{2}}^{PV}.

(5.47)

The relation in the second vertex (vectorial) appears to have the same
behavior as the equation (
5.36
). The terms can not be identified
directly; see the equation below

\displaystyle q^{\mu_{2}}(T_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}^{AV})_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2q^{\nu_{2}}\varepsilon_{\nu_{1}\nu_{2}}\Delta_{2\mu_{1}}^{\nu_{1}}+4\varepsilon_{\nu_{1}\nu_{2}}(J_{2\mu_{1}}^{\nu_{1}}+q_{\mu_{1}}J_{2}^{\nu_{1}})+\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\nu}q^{\nu}[q^{2}-\left(m_{1}+m_{2}\right)^{2}]J_{2}

(5.48)

\displaystyle+\left(i/4\pi\right)\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\nu}q^{\nu}\left[\log\left(m_{1}^{2}/\lambda^{2}\right)+\log\left(m_{2}^{2}/\lambda^{2}\right)\right]

Again, we have to switch the indices of place what will amount to the
apperance of a conditioning factor in its RAGFs, namely,

q^{\mu_{2}}(T_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}^{AV})_{2}=T_{\left(-\right)\mu_{1}}^{A}+\left(m_{2}-m_{1}\right)T_{\mu_{1}}^{AS}+\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}}q^{\nu_{1}}(2\Delta_{2\nu_{2}}^{\nu_{2}}+i/\pi).

(5.49)

Equivalence
: To be complete, we must evaluate the difference
between the versions (
5.12
) and (
5.13
). Taking their full
expression and subtracting one from another

\displaystyle(T_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}^{AV})_{1}-(T_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}^{AV})_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2[\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}\nu_{1}}\Delta_{2\mu_{1}}^{\nu_{1}}-\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}}\Delta_{2\mu_{2}}^{\nu_{1}}]-2\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}[q^{2}-\left(m_{1}^{2}+m_{2}^{2}\right)]J_{2}

(5.50)

\displaystyle+4[\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}\nu_{1}}(J_{2\mu_{1}}^{\nu_{1}}+q_{\mu_{1}}J_{2}^{\nu_{1}})-\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}}(J_{2\mu_{2}}^{\nu_{1}}+q_{\mu_{2}}J_{2}^{\nu_{1}})]

\displaystyle-(i/2\pi)\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}[\log(m_{1}^{2}/\lambda^{2})+\log(m_{2}^{2}/\lambda^{2})];

thereby employing the Schouten identity in the second line above, we have

4\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}\nu_{1}}(J_{2\mu_{1}}^{\nu_{1}}+q_{\mu_{1}}J_{2}^{\nu_{1}})-4\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}}(J_{2\mu_{2}}^{\nu_{1}}+q_{\mu_{2}}J_{2}^{\nu_{1}})=4\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}\mu_{1}}(J_{2\nu_{1}}^{\nu_{1}}+q_{\nu_{1}}J_{2}^{\nu_{1}}).

With the help of reductions, it is relatively easy to show exactly

(T_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}^{AV})_{1}-(T_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}^{AV})_{2}=2[\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}\nu_{1}}\Delta_{2\mu_{1}}^{\nu_{1}}-\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}}\Delta_{2\mu_{2}}^{\nu_{1}}]+\left(i/\pi\right)\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}\mu_{1}}.

(5.51)

Apllying
\varepsilon_{[\mu_{2}\nu_{1}}\Delta_{2\mu_{1}]}^{\nu_{1}}=0
, this result naturally also may be expressed as

(T_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}^{AV})_{1}-(T_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}^{AV})_{2}=\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}\mu_{1}}(2\Delta_{2\nu_{1}}^{\nu_{1}}+i/\pi).

(5.52)

Another way to systematize the RAGFs that will be used in Chapter (
7
) is to notice that every time the index is contracted with the one
remaining in the even amplitude, the relation is satisfied. Therefore we can
use the above relation to exchange the versions when contracting with the
index in the vertex used to define the version

\displaystyle q^{\mu_{1}}(T_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}^{AV})_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q^{\mu_{1}}(T_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}^{AV})_{2}+\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}\mu_{1}}q^{\mu_{1}}\left(2\Delta_{2\nu}^{\nu}+i/\pi\right)

(5.53)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle T_{\left(-\right)\mu_{2}}^{A}-2\left(m_{1}+m_{2}\right)T_{\mu_{2}}^{PV}+\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}\nu}q^{\nu}\left(2\Delta_{2\alpha}^{\alpha}+i/\pi\right)

\displaystyle q^{\mu_{2}}(T_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}^{AV})_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q^{\mu_{2}}(T_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}^{AV})_{1}-\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}\mu_{1}}q^{\mu_{2}}(2\Delta_{2\alpha}^{\alpha}+i/\pi)

(5.54)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle T_{\left(-\right)\mu_{2}}^{A}-2(m_{1}-m_{2})T_{\mu_{2}}^{AS}+\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\nu}q^{\nu}(2\Delta_{2\alpha}^{\alpha}+i/\pi).

These features are notable in two dimensions. In four dimensions, we also
establish relations among versions (three of them). However, in that
scenario, the odd amplitudes do not collapse in a direct connection to even
ones. We have to check the RAGFs explicitly.

Even Amplitudes
: The relations to the even amplitudes are easy to
check,

\displaystyle q^{\mu_{1}}T_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}^{VV}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2q^{\mu_{1}}\Delta_{2\mu_{12}}+\left(i/4\pi\right)q_{\mu_{2}}\left[\log\left(m_{1}^{2}/\lambda^{2}\right)+\log\left(m_{2}^{2}/\lambda^{2}\right)\right]

(5.55)

\displaystyle+4\left(q^{\nu_{1}}J_{2\mu_{1}\nu_{1}}+q_{\mu_{2}}q^{\nu_{1}}J_{2\nu_{1}}\right)+q_{\mu_{2}}[q^{2}-\left(m_{1}-m_{2}\right)^{2}]J_{2}.

Using the same operations in
J_{2}
-integrals as applied to the odd
amplitudes follows

\displaystyle q^{\mu_{1}}T_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}^{VV}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle T_{\left(-\right)\mu_{2}}^{V}-2\left(m_{1}-m_{2}\right)\left[\left(m_{1}+m_{2}\right)J_{2\mu_{2}}+m_{1}q_{\mu_{2}}J_{2}\right]

(5.56)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle T_{\left(-\right)\mu_{2}}^{V}+\left(m_{2}-m_{1}\right)T_{\mu_{2}}^{SV}

\displaystyle q^{\mu_{2}}T_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}^{VV}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle T_{\left(-\right)\mu_{1}}^{V}+\left(m_{2}-m_{1}\right)T_{\mu_{1}}^{VS}.

(5.57)

For the
AA
-amplitude (
5.11
), the two relations follows by

\displaystyle q^{\mu_{1}}T_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}^{AA}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle T_{\left(-\right)\mu_{2}}^{V}-\left(m_{1}+m_{2}\right)T_{\mu_{2}}^{PA}

\displaystyle q^{\mu_{2}}T_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}^{AA}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle T_{\left(-\right)\mu_{2}}^{V}+\left(m_{2}+m_{1}\right)T_{\mu_{1}}^{AP}.

(5.58)

See
PA
in (
5.17
); we could have expressed only in term of one
since they differ by a sign.

The double-contraction for the even amplitudes (
5.30
) and (
5.33
)
is associated with finite one-rank amplitudes. By themselves their relations
are

\displaystyle q^{\mu_{1}}t_{\mu_{1}}^{VS}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle+\left(m_{2}-m_{1}\right)t^{SS}+[t^{S}\left(1\right)-t^{S}\left(2\right)]

(5.59)

\displaystyle q^{\mu_{1}}t_{\mu_{1}}^{AP}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\left(m_{2}+m_{1}\right)t^{PP}-[t^{S}\left(1\right)+t^{S}\left(2\right)].

(5.60)

The LHS is finite, but the RHS shows a log-divergent object
I_{\log}
.
Nonetheless, in our strategy, it is an exact and straightforward algebraic
step to verify them. Using as an example the following equation

q^{\mu_{1}}T_{\mu_{1}}^{VS}=2\left(m_{1}+m_{2}\right)q^{\mu_{1}}J_{2\mu_{1}}+2m_{1}q^{2}J_{2}.

(5.61)

Applying Eq. (
3.69
) in order to reduce the two-masses vector integral,
we have

q^{\mu_{1}}T_{\mu_{1}}^{VS}=-\left(m_{2}-m_{1}\right)[q^{2}-\left(m_{1}+m_{2}\right)^{2}]J_{2}+\left(i/4\pi\right)\left(m_{1}+m_{2}\right)\log\left(m_{2}^{2}/m_{1}^{2}\right).

(5.62)

The last term can be manipulated by the scale relation (
3.62
), viz.,

\left(i/4\pi\right)\log\left(m_{2}^{2}/m_{1}^{2}\right)=I_{\log}\left(m_{1}^{2}\right)-I_{\log}\left(m_{2}^{2}\right),

(5.63)

which through an organization of the terms produces the following expression

\displaystyle q^{\mu_{1}}T_{\mu_{1}}^{VS}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(m_{2}-m_{1}\right)\{[-q^{2}+\left(m_{1}+m_{2}\right)^{2}]J_{2}+\left[I_{\log}(m_{1}^{2})+I_{\log}(m_{2}^{2})\right]\}

(5.64)

\displaystyle+2m_{1}I_{\log}(m_{1}^{2})-2m_{2}I_{\log}\left(m_{2}^{2}\right).

We can rewrite the first term as the
SS
amplitude (
5.7
) and
organize the result

\displaystyle q^{\mu_{1}}T_{\mu_{1}}^{VS}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(m_{2}-m_{1}\right)T^{SS}

(5.65)

\displaystyle+2m_{1}[I_{\log}(\lambda^{2})-(i/4\pi)\log\left(m_{1}^{2}/\lambda^{2}\right)]

\displaystyle-2m_{2}[I_{\log}(\lambda^{2})-(i/4\pi)\log\left(m_{2}^{2}/\lambda^{2}\right)].

The scalar one-point function is given in (
5.20
). Hence we verify that
the two last lines correspond to the difference between them, representing
the satisfaction of its RAGF,

q^{\mu_{1}}T_{\mu_{1}}^{VS}=\left(m_{2}-m_{1}\right)T^{SS}+T^{S}\left(1\right)-T^{S}\left(2\right).

(5.66)

Note that in these case, the difference between scalar one-point functions
does not cancel and depends on the individual masses.

The
q^{\mu}T_{\mu}^{AP}
works under the same manipulations used in
VS
,
starting with

q^{\mu_{1}}T_{\mu_{1}}^{AP}=2\left(m_{2}-m_{1}\right)q^{\mu_{1}}J_{2\mu_{1}}-2m_{1}q^{2}J_{2}.

(5.67)

Through of the relation estabilish in (
3.69
), the equation above
results in

q^{\mu_{1}}T_{\mu_{1}}^{AP}=\left(m_{1}+m_{2}\right)[\left(m_{1}-m_{2}\right)^{2}-q^{2}]J_{2}+\left(i/4\pi\right)\left(m_{2}-m_{1}\right)\log\left(m_{2}^{2}/m_{1}^{2}\right).

(5.68)

Rewriten the first term by (
5.6
) and organize the result

\displaystyle q^{\mu_{1}}T_{\mu_{1}}^{AP}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\left(m_{1}+m_{2}\right)T^{PP}

(5.70)

\displaystyle-2m_{1}[I_{\log}(\lambda^{2})-\left(i/4\pi\right)\log\left(m_{1}^{2}/\lambda^{2}\right)]

\displaystyle-2m_{2}[I_{\log}(\lambda^{2})-\left(i/4\pi\right)\log\left(m_{2}^{2}/\lambda^{2}\right)].

The two last lines now appear as the sum of scalar one-point functions,
namely

q^{\mu_{1}}T_{\mu_{1}}^{AP}=-\left(m_{1}+m_{2}\right)T^{PP}-[T^{S}\left(1\right)+T^{S}\left(2\right)].

(5.71)

For equal masses, the term to one-point functions is proportional to the
masses’ sum.

As explored in the chapter for equal masses, it is possible to obtain
properties for the amplitudes by combining their general tensor structures
with their symmetry relations or Ward’s identities. These results are not
restricted to perturbative solutions and should remain valid even for exact
solutions. The
VS
function is constructed from a vector with an external
vector,
T_{\mu}^{VS}=q_{\mu}F_{1}(q^{2}),
where
F_{1}(q^{2})
is an
invariant function. This form allows us to state a low-energy limit for this
amplitude contracting the equation, viz,
q^{\mu}T_{\mu}^{VS}=q^{2}F_{1}(q^{2}).
Then,
\left.q^{\mu}T_{\mu}^{VS}\right|_{q^{2}=0}=0
, since
F_{1}(q^{2})
does not poles at
q^{2}=0.

In this way, to obtain an interpretation relation from RHS of relations (
5.66
) and (
5.71
), let us analyze the
SV
and
AP
-amplitudes in the
limit in kinematical point. We have that
\lim_{q^{2}\rightarrow 0}(q^{\mu_{1}}T_{\mu_{1}}^{VS})=0
is satisfied, since the
J_{2}=i/4\pi Z_{0}^{\left(-1\right)}
where the function
Z_{0}^{\left(-1\right)}
in
this point is given by

Z_{0}^{\left(-1\right)}\left(0,m_{2},m_{1}\right)=\frac{1}{\left(m_{1}^{2}-m_{2}^{2}\right)}\log\frac{m_{2}^{2}}{m_{1}^{2}}.

(5.72)

From Eq (
5.66
) and the explicit result (
5.7
), follows

\displaystyle\left.(m_{2}-m_{1})T^{SS}\right|_{q^{2}=0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2(m_{2}-m_{1})I_{\log}(\lambda^{2})+\left(i/2\pi\right)[m_{1}\log(m_{1}^{2}/\lambda^{2})-m_{2}\log(m_{2}^{2}/\lambda^{2})]

(5.73)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\left[T^{S}\left(1\right)-T^{S}\left(2\right)\right],

(5.74)

therefore
\left.q^{\mu_{1}}T_{\mu_{1}}^{VS}\right|_{q^{2}=0}=0.

The low-energy theorem for
T_{\mu_{1}}^{AP}
is also fulfilled because the
same operations leads us to

\left.\left(m_{1}+m_{2}\right)T^{PP}\right|_{q^{2}=0}=-\left[T^{S}\left(1\right)+T^{S}\left(2\right)\right].

(5.75)

We saw that the one-point functions were indispensable for satisfying the
deduced kinematical implication based on the tensor structure for amplitude
with one Lorentz index. That is the opposite of the situation for amplitudes
with two indices. The reason for the need for scalar one-point functions can
be understood by analyzing the canonical structure of WIs for multiple
masses. There, the meaning of these terms finds a justification.

5.2
Ward Identities: Two Masses

Here we will argue why the scalar one-point functions are part of WIs from
one-index two-point functions. We take free fields that generate our
amplitudes, of particular interest to our purposes, obeying the equal-time
anticommutation relation

\{\psi_{i}^{\alpha}\left(y\right),\psi_{j}^{\dagger\kappa}\left(x\right)\}=\delta_{ij}\delta^{\alpha\kappa}\delta\left(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}\right),

(5.76)

where
i
and
j
refer to different species of fermions (
\psi_{1}
and
\psi_{2}
), all other anticommutators are null. Fermionic densities,
defined as a set of bilinear in the fermions, are

J^{\Gamma_{i}}=\bar{\psi}_{2}\Gamma_{i}\psi_{1},\quad\text{and }\quad J^{\Gamma_{i}\dagger}=\bar{\psi}_{1}(\gamma_{0}\Gamma_{i}^{\dagger}\gamma_{0})\psi_{2},

(5.77)

where
\Gamma_{i}
belong to set of the vertices given by (
2.2
). Explicitly we have

V^{\mu}=\left(\bar{\psi}_{2}\gamma^{\mu}\psi_{1}\right),\quad A_{\ast}^{\mu}=\left(\bar{\psi}_{2}\gamma_{\ast}\gamma^{\mu}\psi_{1}\right),\quad S=\left(\bar{\psi}_{2}\psi_{1}\right),\quad P=\left(\bar{\psi}_{2}\gamma_{\ast}\psi_{1}\right).

The adjoints yield the same matrices
\gamma_{0}\Gamma_{i}^{\dagger}\gamma_{0}=\Gamma_{i}
with the exception of pseudo-scalar one
\gamma_{0}\gamma_{\ast}\gamma_{0}=-\gamma_{\ast}
. We adopted a different
notation here to avoid confusion with
J
-integrals. Two-point functions can
be seen in position space as

T^{\Gamma_{1}\Gamma_{2}}\left(x-y\right)=\mathrm{tr}\left[\Gamma_{1}S_{F}\left(x-y,m_{1}\right)\Gamma_{2}S_{F}\left(y-x,m_{2}\right)\right]=-\left\langle J^{\Gamma_{1}}\left(x\right)J^{\Gamma_{2}\dagger}\left(y\right)\right\rangle.

(5.78)

The minus sign occurs because Wick contraction yields
i
times our
propagator definition, and
\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle=\left\langle 0\left|\mathcal{T}\left\{\cdot\right\}\right|0\right\rangle

is an abbreviation for a time-ordered product. We recovered the letter for
the Feynman propagator to not mistake it for scalar density.

To clarify the WIs for two-point functions with one-index, we use Dirac
equations,

\gamma^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\psi_{1}=-im_{1}\psi_{1};\quad\left(\partial_{\mu}\bar{\psi}_{2}\right)\gamma^{\mu}=im_{2}\bar{\psi}_{2}.

(5.79)

Through them, we obtain that the vector and axial currents satisfy

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}V^{\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle+i\left(m_{2}-m_{1}\right)S\left(x\right)=i\left(m_{2}-m_{1}\right)\bar{\psi}_{2}\psi_{1}

(5.80)

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}A^{\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-i\left(m_{2}+m_{1}\right)P\left(x\right)=-i\left(m_{2}+m_{1}\right)\bar{\psi}_{2}\gamma_{\ast}\psi.

(5.81)

The next step is to notice that when we perform space-time derivatives in
the time ordering for densities carrying Lorentz indices, equal-time
commutators will appear; to them, we will use the identity

\left[AB,CD\right]=-AC\left\{B,D\right\}+A\left\{B,C\right\}D-C\left\{D,A\right\}B+\left\{C,A\right\}DB.

(5.82)

Necessary formal commutators arise to time components, but in general, we
will have

\displaystyle\left[J^{\Gamma_{1}}\left(x\right),J^{\Gamma_{2}\dagger}\left(y\right)\right]_{x^{0}=y^{0}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[\bar{\psi}_{2}\left(x\right)\Gamma^{1}\psi_{1}\left(x\right),\bar{\psi}_{1}\left(y\right)\Gamma^{2}\psi_{2}\left(y\right)\right]

(5.83)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[\bar{\psi}_{2}\left(x\right)\Gamma^{1}\gamma^{0}\Gamma^{2}\psi_{2}\left(y\right)-\bar{\psi}_{1}\left(y\right)\Gamma^{2}\gamma^{0}\Gamma^{1}\psi_{1}\left(x\right)\right]\delta^{2}\left(x-y\right).

The commutators necessary to point out the differences between symmetry
relations of two and one indices two-point functions (satisfied for
VS
and

AP
amplitudes) are

\displaystyle\left[V_{0}\left(x\right),V^{\nu\dagger}\left(y\right)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[\bar{\psi}_{2}\left(x\right)\gamma^{\nu}\psi_{2}\left(y\right)-\bar{\psi}_{1}\left(y\right)\gamma^{\nu}\psi_{1}\left(x\right)\right]\delta^{2}\left(x-y\right)

(5.84)

\displaystyle\left[A_{0}\left(x\right),V^{\nu\dagger}\left(y\right)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[\bar{\psi}_{2}\left(x\right)\gamma_{\ast}\gamma^{\nu}\psi_{2}\left(y\right)-\bar{\psi}_{1}\left(y\right)\gamma_{\ast}\gamma^{\nu}\psi_{1}\left(x\right)\right]\delta^{2}\left(x-y\right)

(5.85)

\displaystyle\left[V_{0}\left(x\right),S^{\dagger}\left(y\right)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[\bar{\psi}_{2}\left(x\right)\psi_{2}\left(y\right)-\bar{\psi}_{1}\left(y\right)\psi_{1}\left(x\right)\right]\delta^{2}\left(x-y\right)

(5.86)

\displaystyle\left[A_{0}\left(x\right),P^{\dagger}\left(y\right)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[-\bar{\psi}_{2}\left(x\right)\psi_{2}\left(y\right)-\bar{\psi}_{1}\left(y\right)\psi_{1}\left(x\right)\right]\delta^{2}\left(x-y\right),

(5.87)

all evaluated in
x_{0}=y_{0}
. Observe that densities in LHS carry two
distinct masses, and the RHS bilinears appear with only one mass, though the
two terms carry a distinct mass.

Taking the derivative of
VV
, using the motion’s equation to the currents,
and observing the commutator at equal times (
5.84
), we get the formal
result

\displaystyle\partial_{x}^{\mu}\left\langle V_{\mu}\left(x\right)V_{\nu}^{\dagger}\left(y\right)\right\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle i\left(m_{2}-m_{1}\right)\left\langle S\left(x\right)V_{\nu}^{\dagger}\left(y\right)\right\rangle

(5.88)

\displaystyle+\left[\left\langle\bar{\psi}_{2}\left(x\right)\gamma_{\nu}\psi_{2}\left(y\right)\right\rangle-\left\langle\bar{\psi}_{1}\left(y\right)\gamma_{\nu}\psi_{1}\left(x\right)\right\rangle\right]\delta^{2}\left(x-y\right),

where
\partial_{x}^{\mu}=\partial/\partial x_{\mu}
. The Ward identity
for equal masses came from cancellation in the last line since the terms
become equal, and we are ignoring Schwinger’s terms. As for two masses, it
arises from Lorentz symmetry that implies the vanishing of one-point vector
function individually, e.g.,
\left\langle 0\left|\bar{\psi}_{1}\left(x\right)\gamma^{\nu}\psi_{1}\left(y\right)\right|0\right\rangle=0
. It is understood by using the generator of translations in a vector
operator
O^{\mu}\left(x\right)
,

\left\langle 0\left|O^{\mu}\left(x\right)\right|0\right\rangle=\left\langle 0\left|e^{-iP\cdot x}O^{\mu}\left(0\right)e^{iP\cdot x}\right|0\right\rangle=\left\langle 0\left|O^{\mu}\left(0\right)\right|0\right\rangle=0.

(5.89)

Furthermore, because o Lorentz symmetry, such a constant vector must vanish.
Note that this constraint may not be valid perturbatively. Putting aside
that, the proposed WI is

\partial_{x}^{\mu}\left\langle V_{\mu}\left(x\right)V_{\nu}^{\dagger}\left(y\right)\right\rangle=i\left(m_{2}-m_{1}\right)\left\langle S\left(x\right)V_{\nu}^{\dagger}\left(y\right)\right\rangle.

(5.90)

In it, only the contribution of motion’s equations plays a part;
additionally, if the correlator involves one axial and one vector current,
the argument for vanishing the one-point amplitudes in (
5.85
) is the
same.

The situation is quite different for
VS
and
AP
functions
; symmetry constraints pass

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}^{x}\left\langle V^{\mu}\left(x\right)S^{\dagger}\left(y\right)\right\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle i\left(m_{2}-m_{1}\right)\left\langle S\left(x\right)S^{\dagger}\left(y\right)\right\rangle

(5.91)

\displaystyle+\left\langle\bar{\psi}_{2}\left(x\right)\psi_{2}\left(y\right)-\bar{\psi}_{1}\left(y\right)\psi_{1}\left(x\right)\right\rangle\delta^{2}\left(x-y\right),

where the commutator
\left[V_{0}\left(x\right),S^{\dagger}\left(y\right)\right]=(
5.86
)
generates one-point scalar functions that
formally cancel each other for equal masses, but in that case, the
VS
-amplitude is null. Nonetheless, in
AP
(or
PA
), they appear in a
non-canceling way

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}^{x}\left\langle A^{\mu}\left(x\right)P^{\dagger}\left(y\right)\right\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-i\left(m_{1}+m_{2}\right)\left\langle P\left(x\right)P^{\dagger}\left(y\right)\right\rangle

(5.92)

\displaystyle-\left\langle\bar{\psi}_{2}\left(x\right)\psi_{2}\left(y\right)+\bar{\psi}_{1}\left(y\right)\psi_{1}\left(x\right)\right\rangle\delta^{2}\left(x-y\right).

The commutator yields a sum, not a cancellation, for equal masses. So the
canonical commutator terms appear and may not be zero due to other symmetry
arguments.

As in the two masses scenario, the scalar one-point functions are not
removed from expression to Ward identities and are an integral part of them.
For one species of fermions, the commutator of vector (and axial) densities
being zero is a particular phenomenon; this term comes from canonical
algebra. Their eliminations are to be accounted for by additional arguments,
e.g., Lorentz invariance. Such statements are not present against scalar
densities that, in turn, guarantee a low-energy theorem to the
VS
and
AP

amplitudes.

To visualize consequences of this reasoning line and connect it with
calculated expression, let us remind that Wick contractions yield
i
times
our definition of the propagator,

\left\langle 0\left|T\psi^{\alpha}\left(x\right)\bar{\psi}^{\kappa}\left(y\right)\right|0\right\rangle=iS_{F}^{\alpha\kappa}\left(x-y,m_{i}\right).

(5.93)

Therefore, Fourier transforming the two-point functions (
5.78
),

\displaystyle T^{\Gamma_{1}\Gamma_{2}}\left(q\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\mathrm{d}^{2}ze^{-iq\cdot z}\left[T^{\Gamma_{1}\Gamma_{2}}\left(z\right)\right]=-\int\mathrm{d}^{2}ze^{-iq\cdot z}\left\langle J^{\Gamma_{1}}\left(x\right)J^{\Gamma_{2}\dagger}\left(y\right)\right\rangle

(5.94)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{2}}\mathrm{tr}\left[\Gamma_{1}S_{F}\left(k+k_{1},m_{1}\right)\Gamma_{2}S_{F}\left(k+k_{2},m_{2}\right)\right],

(5.95)

where
z=x-y
and
k_{2}-k_{1}=q
. In the case of double-vector
VV
(
5.10
) and
VS
, we may write the motion’s equation, and the commutation
relations furnish the formal equations

\displaystyle\partial_{z}^{\mu}T_{\mu\nu}^{VV}\left(z\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle i\left(m_{2}-m_{1}\right)T_{\nu}^{SV}\left(z\right)

(5.96)

\displaystyle+i\mathrm{tr}\left[\gamma_{\nu}S_{F}\left(z,m_{1}\right)\right]\delta^{2}\left(z\right)-i\mathrm{tr}\left[\gamma_{\nu}S_{F}\left(-z,m_{2}\right)\right]\delta^{2}\left(z\right),

\displaystyle\partial_{z}^{\mu}T_{\mu}^{VS}\left(z\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle i\left(m_{2}-m_{1}\right)T^{SS}\left(z\right)

(5.97)

\displaystyle+i\mathrm{tr}\left[S_{F}\left(z,m_{1}\right)\right]\delta^{2}\left(z\right)-i\mathrm{tr}\left[S_{F}\left(-z,m_{2}\right)\right]\delta^{2}\left(z\right),

whose Fourier transform returns an expression where we do not neglect any
term,

\displaystyle q^{\mu}T_{\mu\nu}^{VV}\left(k_{1},k_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(m_{2}-m_{1}\right)T_{\nu}^{SV}

(5.98)

\displaystyle+\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{2}}\left\{\mathrm{tr}\left[\gamma^{\nu}S_{F}\left(K_{1},m_{1}\right)\right]-\mathrm{tr}\left[\gamma^{\nu}S_{F}\left(K_{2},m_{2}\right)\right]\right\}

\displaystyle q^{\mu}T_{\mu}^{VS}\left(k_{1},k_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(m_{2}-m_{1}\right)T^{SS}

(5.99)

\displaystyle+\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{2}}\left\{\mathrm{tr}\left[S_{F}\left(K_{1},m_{1}\right)\right]-\mathrm{tr}\left[S_{F}\left(K_{2},m_{2}\right)\right]\right\}.

Recapitulating the facts, the parts from the time component of the
commutator of currents with vector and axial currents formally cancel for
one species of massive fermions. We got a WI whose contribution comes only
from motion equations. On the other hand, for two masses, formal Lorentz
invariance requires the vector and axial one-point functions to vanish as
well, and thus they are not part of the WI. Indeed using our strategy, we
saw in momentum space that they become pure surface-term that can be made
zero. Additionally, the anomalies of the odd amplitudes are related to the
impossibility of the formal/canonical WI being realized, which we establish
as a consequence of a Low energy implication from a finite function; see the
next section where that point is discussed and the relation with the
linearity of integration.

In contrast, the commutator of the time component of the currents with
scalar densities, or pseudo-scalar ones, giving rise to scalar one-point
functions, besides the term coming from the motion’s equations, is not
necessarily zero. The point is that when the masses are equal, that
difference of amplitudes vanishes in pairs for
SV
and sum for
AP
. They
do not cancel in any situation for distinct masses and can not be zero
because they are not a constant function of their mass parameters.

One way to see the difference between the two situations is to take into
account that for even dimension, there is a matrix such that
C\gamma_{\mu}C^{-1}=-\gamma_{\mu}^{T}
, the charge conjugation matrix. This matrix
implies a behavior to the vertexes, viz.,

C\left[1,\gamma_{\ast},\gamma_{\mu},\gamma_{\ast}\gamma_{\mu}\right]C^{-1}=[1,-\gamma_{\ast}^{T},-\gamma_{\mu}^{T},-\left(\gamma_{\ast}\gamma_{\mu}\right)^{T}].

(5.100)

It is direct to see that the propagator obeys
CS_{F}(K_{i},m_{i})C^{-1}=S_{F}^{T}(-K_{i},m_{i})
. Applying it to the
definition of one-point function, we have

t^{\Gamma_{1}}=\mathrm{tr}[\Gamma_{1}S_{F}\left(K_{i},m_{i}\right)]=\mathrm{tr}[C\Gamma_{1}C^{-1}CS_{F}\left(K_{i},m_{i}\right)C^{-1}].

(5.101)

Using the trace properties and as well the relation for matrices

\mathrm{tr}\left(B_{1}^{T}\cdots B_{n}^{T}\right)=\mathrm{tr}\left(B_{n}\cdots B_{1}\right)^{T}=\mathrm{tr}\left(B_{n}\cdots B_{1}\right),

(5.102)

we may write from general considerations established above

t^{\Gamma_{1}}=\mathrm{tr}[\left(C\Gamma_{1}C^{-1}\right)^{T}S_{F}\left(-K_{i},m_{i}\right)].

At this point, note that there is a sign change to the pseudo-scalar,
vector, and axial vertices. Then integrating the result above, we have

T^{\Gamma_{1}}=-\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{2}}\mathrm{tr}[\Gamma_{1}S_{F}\left(-k-k_{i},m_{i}\right)].

(5.103)

Reflecting on the integration variable and shifting, as the hypothesis, we
get

T^{\Gamma_{1}}\left(k_{i}\right)=-\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{2}}\mathrm{tr}[\Gamma_{1}S_{F}\left(K_{i},m_{i}\right)]=-T^{\Gamma_{1}}\left(k_{i}\right).

(5.104)

That implies that axial and vector one-point functions must vanish
identically, as
T^{P}=0
already in the trace level. As trivial as it may
appear, this is not a direct consequence of Feynman’s rules; the possibility
of shifting is coded in the intrinsic surface term present in the
amplitudes, which is why the
T^{V},

T^{A}
are only surface terms.
Nevertheless, it does not mean these parts in the amplitudes could not be
non-zero and violate WIs.

For instance, the scalar function may have surface terms in 4D, but it is
not obliged to be identically zero by translational invariance. In that
case, the above equation picks up a positive sign. Those
T^{S}\left(k_{i},m_{i}\right)
amplitudes show a masse dependence through a logarithm.
Since they are proportional to the basic divergent object, taking its
derivative,

\frac{\partial I_{\log}\left(m_{i}^{2}\right)}{\partial m_{i}^{2}}=-\frac{i}{4\pi}\frac{1}{m_{i}^{2}}.

(5.105)

The integration picks up an arbitrary constant
I_{\log}\left(m_{i}^{2}\right)=\left(i4\pi\right)^{-1}\log\left(m_{i}^{2}/\lambda_{0}^{2}\right)
that could help with cancellations; however, in
combinations, this is not possible, see

I_{\log}(m_{i}^{2})-I_{\log}(m_{j}^{2})=-\frac{i}{4\pi}\log\frac{m_{i}^{2}}{m_{j}^{2}}.

(5.106)

However, the scalar-one cancels each other for equal masses when they arise
from a commutator of vector currents. When the masses are unequal, there is
no reason for them to disappear in the perturbative expression. They are
integral parts of WI and necessary for their consistency. The low-energy
theorem derived for them requires that part to occur

q^{\mu}T_{\mu}^{VS}=q^{2}F\left(q^{2}\right)=0.

(5.107)

Next, in addition to the paper
[
41
]
, we will have to present the
construction of a low-energy theorem, ultimately responsible for violations
associated with the chiral anomaly in the odd amplitude where the vector
current as the axial are not classically conserved.

5.3
Low-Energy Theorem and RAGFs

As observed, WIs to
AV
-versions can not both simultaneously hold. Firstly,
vanishing the surface term eliminates the one-point functions; however, it
implies linearity breaking, and an additional constant can not get rid of by
any other choice. On the other hand, if the non-zero value corresponding to
the maintenance of RAGFs (linearity) is chosen, axial one-point functions
violate WIs in any case. In the scenario where the surface term could be
arbitrary through some device or interpretation, the violation does not give
up. To understand this state of affairs, we have resorted to an explanation
only utilizing properties that are immune to choices and do not privilege
one symmetry over another:
the kinematical behavior of
PV
function.

We return to the last claims of the Chapter (
4
), assuming the
general tensor for odd amplitudes (
4.5
). In 2D, the amplitude has
Feynman integrals of power counting zero, one of which is a tensor integral.
These types of integrals, in any dimension, indeed own surface terms,
notwithstanding the coefficient of them only depending on the difference of
routings; they are intrinsic to Feynman diagrams, not only when the power
counting is linear. These features must be considered when stating general
theorems about kinematical properties and their relations to the symmetry
content of amplitudes coming from Feynman’s rules. In 4D, we will have a
more complex scenario: the surface terms appear with ambiguous combinations
of routing sums, see Sections (
6.2
) and (
6.3
).

Only external momenta imply that preserving divergent content intact follows
an expression to general tensor structure that accounts for the presence of
surface terms because, in the last instance, they contribute a coefficient
proportional to the metric,

F_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}=\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}F_{1}+\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\nu}q^{\nu}q_{\mu_{2}}F_{2}+\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}\nu}q^{\nu}q_{\mu_{1}}F_{3}.

(5.108)

The path often trailed to study symmetry violations is to perform
contractions and use some symmetry constraints to derive implications over
others. Nonetheless, we shall derive a device that prescinds from the choice
of some,
a priori
, selected symmetry. Performing contractions and
identifying two invariant functions constructed with form factors
F_{i}
,
viz.,

\displaystyle q^{\mu_{1}}F_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle:\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}\nu}q^{\nu}V_{1}\left(q^{2}\right)

(5.109)

\displaystyle q^{\mu_{2}}F_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle:\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\nu}q^{\nu}V_{2}\left(q^{2}\right).

(5.110)

We got two equations that are strict and intrinsic consequences of tensor
properties. If we sum them,
F_{1}
drops, and an independent equation
emerges

V_{1}\left(q^{2}\right)+V_{2}\left(q^{2}\right)=q^{2}\left(F_{3}+F_{2}\right).

(5.111)

For
F_{2}
and
F_{3}\,
sufficiently regular in the point
q^{2}=0
this
equation becomes

V_{1}\left(0\right)+V_{2}\left(0\right)=0.

(5.112)

From it, being aware of its generality, we establish some computational-free
conclusions. First, suppose the general tensor is chosen to correspond with
the axial-vector amplitude and function of two masses, i.e.,
F_{\mu\nu}=T_{\mu\nu}^{AV}
. In that case, we may inquire about expected amplitudes
related to the hypothesis of WIs.

The systematization of 2pt, 1st-rank amplitude arising from contraction
q^{\mu_{i}}
starts with

(q^{\mu_{i}}T_{\mu_{12}}^{\Gamma_{12}})^{\mathrm{2pt}}=\varepsilon_{\mu_{k}\nu}q^{\nu}\Omega_{i}^{\left(\mathrm{2pt}\right)},\ \quad\left\{i,k\right\}=\left\{1,3\right\},\text{ }k\not=i.

(5.113)

That is a form to compare standard identifications with consequences of
tensor structure in the LHS. It denotes the 2pt functions (finite) coming
from the
i
-th contraction. They can be zero to some contractions, e.g.,
vector contraction for equal masses. Particularly,

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\mu_{i}\nu}q^{\nu}\Omega^{PV}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\left(m_{1}+m_{2}\right)T_{\mu_{i}}^{PV}

(5.114)

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\mu_{i}\nu}q^{\nu}\Omega^{AS}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle+\left(m_{2}-m_{1}\right)T_{\mu_{i}}^{AS},

(5.115)

given by (
5.14
), (
5.15
). The vector and scalar integrals (
3.63
)-(
3.64
) enable to write

\displaystyle\Omega^{AS}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{i}{2\pi}[\left(m_{2}^{2}-m_{1}^{2}\right)Z_{1}^{\left(-1\right)}-\left(m_{2}-m_{1}\right)m_{1}Z_{0}^{\left(-1\right)}]

(5.116)

\displaystyle\Omega^{PV}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{i}{2\pi}[\left(m_{2}^{2}-m_{1}^{2}\right)Z_{1}^{\left(-1\right)}+\left(m_{1}+m_{2}\right)m_{1}Z_{0}^{\left(-1\right)}].

(5.117)

Summing them, we have from combination (
3.31
), a result independent
of masses,

\left(\Omega^{AS}+\Omega^{PV}\right)\left(0\right)=-\frac{i}{\pi}\left.[\left(m_{1}^{2}-m_{2}^{2}\right)Z_{1}^{\left(-1\right)}-m_{1}^{2}Z_{0}^{\left(-1\right)}]\right|_{q^{2}=0}=-\frac{i}{\pi}.

(5.118)

A moment of reflection shows that anomalous amplitudes share this
combination. As it is incompatible with the low-energy theorem, we derived a
general parity-odd second-rank tensor of mass dimension zero. That is an
inviolable property if it is free of kinematical singularities. We have
anomalies in the vertices, which themselves can be arbitrary,

V_{1}\left(0\right)+V_{2}\left(0\right)=0\not=-\frac{i}{\pi}=\left(\Omega^{AS}+\Omega^{PV}\right)\left(0\right).

(5.119)

Hence, we at least can write
V_{i}\left(q^{2}\right)=\Omega_{i}\left(q^{2}\right)+\mathcal{A}_{i}
, where the additional parameter will be
constrained by the equation above

\mathcal{A}_{1}+\mathcal{A}_{2}=\frac{i}{\pi}.

(5.120)

That represents the restriction of arbitrary anomalies in the axial and
vector vertices. This kinematical implication has an important consequence
over the RAGFs as well.

5.3.1
RAGFs: Linearity and Low-Energy Implications

The surface terms appear in explicit computations and are the only type of
non-finite structures for the 2nd-rank amplitudes. Also, we have observed
that they conditioned the RAGFs. Nonetheless, we needed to establish in the
absolute how they do it. Besides the exciting fact that versions one and two
are the only independent possibilities, the answer to how this appears to be
so must be constructed. Therefore, we explicit this intrinsic part of
perturbative amplitudes; first, we split the general representation in

F_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}=F_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}^{\Delta}+\hat{F}_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}},

(5.121)

where
\hat{F}_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}
encodes the finite parts. The term
F_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}^{\Delta}
stands for the most general combination of
surface terms, given by the equation

F_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}^{\Delta}=a\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\nu}\Delta_{2\mu_{2}}^{\nu}+b\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}\nu}\Delta_{2\mu_{1}}^{\nu}+c\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}\Delta_{2\nu}^{\nu}.

Since there is a linear relation in such tensor due to the vanishing of
3rd-rank complete antisymmetric tensor in
2D
,
\varepsilon_{[\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}\Delta_{2\nu]}^{\nu}=0
, we have a redefinition
a_{1}=\left(a+c\right)
and
a_{2}=\left(b-c\right)
of the
coefficients. Henceforth, the general structure assumes the form

\displaystyle F_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle a_{1}\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\nu}\Delta_{2\mu_{2}}^{\nu}+a_{2}\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}\nu}\Delta_{2\mu_{1}}^{\nu}

(5.122)

\displaystyle+\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}\hat{F}_{1}+\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\nu}q^{\nu}q_{\mu_{2}}\hat{F}_{2}+\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}\nu}q^{\nu}q_{\mu_{1}}\hat{F}_{3}.

The equation that represents the satisfaction of RAGFs can be systematized
through

q^{\mu_{i}}T_{\mu_{12}}^{\Gamma_{12}}=T_{\left(-\right)\mu_{k}}^{A}+\varepsilon_{\mu_{k}\nu}\Omega_{i}.

(5.123)

Remember the notation for the one-point differences (
5.18
). The
condition of linearity of integration is embodied in the following equations
when performing the contractions,

\displaystyle q^{\mu_{1}}F_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle a_{1}q^{\mu_{1}}\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\nu}\Delta_{2\mu_{2}}^{\nu}+a_{2}\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}\nu}q^{\mu_{1}}\Delta_{2\mu_{1}}^{\nu}+\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}\nu}q^{\nu}(q^{2}\hat{F}_{3}-\hat{F}_{1})

(5.124)

\displaystyle q^{\mu_{2}}F_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle a_{1}\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\nu}q^{\mu_{2}}\Delta_{2\mu_{2}}^{\nu}+a_{2}q^{\mu_{2}}\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}\nu}\Delta_{2\mu_{1}}^{\nu}+\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\nu}q^{\nu}(q^{2}\hat{F}_{2}+\hat{F}_{1}).

(5.125)

We rearrange their indices and recognize the one-point functions

\displaystyle q^{\mu_{1}}F_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\left(a_{1}+a_{2}\right)T_{\left(-\right)\mu_{2}}^{A}+\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}\nu}q^{\nu}(q^{2}\hat{F}_{3}-\hat{F}_{1}-a_{1}\Delta_{2\alpha}^{\alpha})

(5.126)

\displaystyle q^{\mu_{2}}F_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\left(a_{1}+a_{2}\right)T_{\left(-\right)\mu_{1}}^{A}+\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\nu}q^{\nu}(q^{2}\hat{F}_{2}+\hat{F}_{1}-a_{2}\Delta_{2\alpha}^{\alpha}).

(5.127)

The RAGFs require for the first terms
a_{1}+a_{2}=-2,
and the other part
must comply with the 2pt functions,
\Omega^{PV}
and
\Omega^{AS}
, which
means

\displaystyle\Omega^{PV}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q^{2}\hat{F}_{3}-\hat{F}_{1}-a_{1}\Delta_{2\alpha}^{\alpha}

(5.128)

\displaystyle\Omega^{AS}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q^{2}\hat{F}_{2}+\hat{F}_{1}-a_{2}\Delta_{2\alpha}^{\alpha}.

(5.129)

Eliminating
\hat{F}_{1}
and considering the first condition
a_{1}+a_{2}=-2
, we obtain

2\Delta_{2\alpha}^{\alpha}=\Omega^{PV}+\Omega^{AS}-q^{2}\hat{F}_{2}-q^{2}\hat{F}_{3}.

(5.130)

In the point
q^{2}=0
follows the low-energy implication of the finite
amplitudes over the integration linearity (RAGFs)

2\Delta_{2\alpha}^{\alpha}=\Omega^{PV}\left(0\right)+\Omega^{AS}\left(0\right)=-\frac{i}{\pi}.

(5.131)

Consequences:
The coefficients
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
may be
arbitrary, but once one is selected to satisfy one RAGF in automatic form,
the other must be zero. This unique solution signifies that most RAGFs found
without conditions are achieved by the basic versions we have defined. This
fact is independent of explicit computations through the traces of four
Dirac matrices and continues to happen in four dimensions. Another
consequence is that the satisfaction of all RAGFs is conditioned through
kinematical features of finite functions that require a non-zero and
specific amount value to the surface terms, implying that shifts in the
integration variable and linearity of integration are incompatible. The
T_{\mu}^{A}
functions depend on the routings, and their subtraction is
zero if shifts are possible; only their difference is a function of the
external momentum. This aspect is peculiar to this dimension; nonetheless,
the restrictions from low-energy implications are precisely mirrored in four
dimensions. Simultaneously satisfaction of RAGFs and translational
invariance in momentum space is prohibited by the low-energy behavior of
finite functions.

Chapter 6
Four-Dimensional Three-Point Functions

The analysis developed in the physical dimension focuses on
odd amplitudes that are rank-3 tensors, namely
AVV
,
VAV
,
VVA
, and
AAA
. Their mathematical structures follow the same features seen in two
dimensions. They depend on the trace involving six Dirac matrices plus the
chiral one, whose computation yields products between the Levi-Civita symbol
and metric tensor. After the integration, that generates expressions that
differ in their dependence on surface terms and finite parts. We want to
verify these prospects by evaluating the triangles’ basic versions
1
1

1

To this aim, we compute twenty-four triangles of rank-one. Twelve
parity-even triangles:
VPP
,
ASP
,
VSS
, and their permutations. Twelve
parity-odd tensors:
ASS
,
APP
,
VPS
, and their permutations. Besides, we
identify three standard tensors in a similar fashion for two dimensions.
.
Once these resources are clear, we study how symmetries, linearity of
integration, and uniqueness manifest.

From Eqs. (
2.9
) and (
2.11
), integrated three-point amplitudes are
denoted through capital letters
T^{\Gamma_{1}\Gamma_{2}\Gamma_{3}}
and
exhibit the integrand

t^{\Gamma_{1}\Gamma_{2}\Gamma_{3}}=\text{{tr}}\left[\Gamma_{1}S\left(1\right)\Gamma_{2}S\left(2\right)\Gamma_{3}S\left(3\right)\right].

(6.1)

Thus, after replacing vertex operators and disregarding vanishing traces,
3rd-order amplitudes assume the forms

\displaystyle t_{\mu_{123}}^{AVV}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[K_{123}^{\nu_{123}}\mathrm{tr}(\gamma_{\ast\mu_{1}\nu_{1}\mu_{2}\nu_{2}\mu_{3}\nu_{3}})+m^{2}\mathrm{tr}(\gamma_{\ast\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}\nu_{1}})(K_{1}^{\nu_{1}}-K_{2}^{\nu_{1}}+K_{3}^{\nu_{1}})]\frac{1}{D_{123}}

(6.2)

\displaystyle t_{\mu_{123}}^{VAV}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[K_{123}^{\nu_{123}}\mathrm{tr}(\gamma_{\ast\mu_{1}\nu_{1}\mu_{2}\nu_{2}\mu_{3}\nu_{3}})+m^{2}\mathrm{tr}(\gamma_{\ast\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}\nu_{1}})(K_{1}^{\nu_{1}}+K_{2}^{\nu_{1}}-K_{3}^{\nu_{1}})]\frac{1}{D_{123}}

(6.3)

\displaystyle t_{\mu_{123}}^{VVA}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[K_{123}^{\nu_{123}}\mathrm{tr}(\gamma_{\ast\mu_{1}\nu_{1}\mu_{2}\nu_{2}\mu_{3}\nu_{3}})-m^{2}\mathrm{tr}(\gamma_{\ast\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}\nu_{1}})(K_{1}^{\nu_{1}}-K_{2}^{\nu_{1}}-K_{3}^{\nu_{1}})]\frac{1}{D_{123}}

(6.4)

\displaystyle t_{\mu_{123}}^{AAA}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[K_{123}^{\nu_{123}}\mathrm{tr}(\gamma_{\ast\mu_{1}\nu_{1}\mu_{2}\nu_{2}\mu_{3}\nu_{3}})-m^{2}\mathrm{tr}(\gamma_{\ast\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}\nu_{1}})(K_{1}^{\nu_{1}}+K_{2}^{\nu_{1}}+K_{3}^{\nu_{1}})]\frac{1}{D_{123}},

(6.5)

where we recall the conventions
K_{123}^{\nu_{123}}=K_{1}^{\nu_{1}}K_{2}^{\nu_{2}}K_{3}^{\nu_{3}}
and
D_{123}=D_{1}D_{2}D_{3}
.

Although the trace involving four Dirac matrices plus the chiral one is
univocal, different expressions are attributed to the leading trace when
considering identities (
2.5
). Since Appendix (
A.1
)
shows that forms achieved through definition
\gamma_{\ast}=i\varepsilon_{\nu_{1234}}\gamma^{\nu_{1234}}/4!
are enough to compound any other,
our starting point is on their structure

\displaystyle\left(4i\right)^{-1}\mathrm{tr}(\gamma_{\ast abcdef})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle+g_{ab}\varepsilon_{cdef}+g_{ad}\varepsilon_{bcef}+g_{af}\varepsilon_{bcde}

(6.6)

\displaystyle+g_{bc}\varepsilon_{adef}+g_{cd}\varepsilon_{abef}+g_{cf}\varepsilon_{abde}

\displaystyle+g_{be}\varepsilon_{acdf}+g_{de}\varepsilon_{abcf}+g_{ef}\varepsilon_{abcd}

\displaystyle-g_{bd}\varepsilon_{acef}-g_{df}\varepsilon_{abce}-g_{bf}\varepsilon_{acde}

\displaystyle-g_{ac}\varepsilon_{bdef}-g_{ce}\varepsilon_{abdf}-g_{ae}\varepsilon_{bcdf}.

There are three basic versions, each corresponding to replacing the chiral
matrix near a specific vertex operator. We introduce a numeric label to
distinguish them:

[\mathrm{tr}(\gamma_{\ast\mu_{1}\nu_{1}\mu_{2}\nu_{2}\mu_{3}\nu_{3}})]_{1}=[\mathrm{tr}(\gamma_{\ast\mu_{2}\nu_{2}\mu_{3}\nu_{3}\mu_{1}\nu_{1}})]_{2}=[\mathrm{tr}(\gamma_{\ast\mu_{3}\nu_{3}\mu_{1}\nu_{1}\mu_{2}\nu_{2}})]_{3}.

(6.7)

They arise when setting the index configuration in the trace above (
6.6
), differing in the signs of terms. We cast their contraction with
K_{123}^{\nu_{123}}
in the sequence. Their integration leads to three not
(automatically) equivalent expressions for each triangle.

\displaystyle[K_{123}^{\nu_{123}}\mathrm{tr}(\gamma_{\ast\mu_{1}\nu_{1}\mu_{2}\nu_{2}\mu_{3}\nu_{3}})]_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-4i\varepsilon_{\mu_{23}\nu_{12}}[K_{1\mu_{1}}K_{23}^{\nu_{12}}-K_{2\mu_{1}}K_{13}^{\nu_{12}}+K_{3\mu_{1}}K_{12}^{\nu_{12}}]

(6.8)

\displaystyle-4i\varepsilon_{\mu_{13}\nu_{12}}[K_{1\mu_{2}}K_{23}^{\nu_{12}}+K_{2\mu_{2}}K_{13}^{\nu_{12}}-K_{3\mu_{2}}K_{12}^{\nu_{12}}]

\displaystyle+4i\varepsilon_{\mu_{12}\nu_{12}}[K_{1\mu_{3}}K_{23}^{\nu_{12}}-K_{2\mu_{3}}K_{13}^{\nu_{12}}-K_{3\mu_{3}}K_{12}^{\nu_{12}}]

\displaystyle-4i\varepsilon_{\mu_{123}\nu_{1}}[K_{1}^{\nu_{1}}(K_{2}\cdot K_{3})-K_{2}^{\nu_{1}}(K_{1}\cdot K_{3})+K_{3}^{\nu_{1}}(K_{1}\cdot K_{2})]

\displaystyle+4i[-g_{\mu_{12}}\varepsilon_{\mu_{3}\nu_{123}}-g_{\mu_{23}}\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\nu_{123}}+g_{\mu_{13}}\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}\nu_{123}}]K_{123}^{\nu_{123}}

\displaystyle[K_{123}^{\nu_{123}}\mathrm{tr}(\gamma_{\ast\mu_{2}\nu_{2}\mu_{3}\nu_{3}\mu_{1}\nu_{1}})]_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle+4i\varepsilon_{\mu_{13}\nu_{12}}[K_{1\mu_{2}}K_{23}^{\nu_{12}}-K_{2\mu_{2}}K_{13}^{\nu_{12}}+K_{3\mu_{2}}K_{12}^{\nu_{12}}]

(6.9)

\displaystyle-4i\varepsilon_{\mu_{12}\nu_{12}}[K_{1\mu_{3}}K_{23}^{\nu_{23}}+K_{2\mu_{3}}K_{13}^{\nu_{13}}+K_{3\mu_{3}}K_{12}^{\nu_{12}}]

\displaystyle-4i\varepsilon_{\mu_{23}\nu_{12}}[K_{1\mu_{1}}K_{23}^{\nu_{23}}+K_{2\mu_{1}}K_{13}^{\nu_{13}}-K_{3\mu_{1}}K_{12}^{\nu_{12}}]

\displaystyle-4i\varepsilon_{\mu_{123}\nu_{1}}[K_{1}^{\nu_{1}}(K_{2}\cdot K_{3})+K_{2}^{\nu_{1}}(K_{1}\cdot K_{3})-K_{3}^{\nu_{1}}(K_{1}\cdot K_{2})]

\displaystyle+4i[g_{\mu_{12}}\varepsilon_{\mu_{3}\nu_{123}}-g_{\mu_{13}}\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}\nu_{123}}-g_{\mu_{23}}\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\nu_{123}}]K_{123}^{\nu_{123}}

\displaystyle[K_{123}^{\nu_{123}}\mathrm{tr}(\gamma_{\ast\mu_{3}\nu_{3}\mu_{1}\nu_{1}\mu_{2}\nu_{2}})]_{3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-4i\varepsilon_{\mu_{12}\nu_{12}}[K_{1\mu_{3}}K_{23}^{\nu_{12}}-K_{2\mu_{3}}K_{13}^{\nu_{12}}+K_{3\mu_{3}}K_{12}^{\nu_{12}}]

(6.10)

\displaystyle-4i\varepsilon_{\mu_{23}\nu_{12}}[K_{1\mu_{1}}K_{23}^{\nu_{12}}-K_{2\mu_{1}}K_{13}^{\nu_{12}}-K_{3\mu_{1}}K_{12}^{\nu_{12}}]

\displaystyle-4i\varepsilon_{\mu_{13}\nu_{12}}[K_{1\mu_{2}}K_{23}^{\nu_{12}}+K_{2\mu_{2}}K_{13}^{\nu_{12}}+K_{3\mu_{2}}K_{12}^{\nu_{12}}]

\displaystyle+4i\varepsilon_{\mu_{123}\nu_{1}}[K_{1}^{\nu_{1}}(K_{2}\cdot K_{3})-K_{2}^{\nu_{1}}(K_{1}\cdot K_{3})-K_{3}^{\nu_{1}}(K_{1}\cdot K_{2})]

\displaystyle+4i[-g_{\mu_{12}}\varepsilon_{\mu_{3}\nu_{123}}-g_{\mu_{13}}\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}\nu_{123}}+g_{\mu_{23}}\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\nu_{123}}]K_{123}^{\nu_{123}}

Analogously to two-dimensional calculations, our next task consists of
organizing and integrating the complete expressions. As the three first rows
of the above equations are similar to the object (
4.24
), we define the
tensors

\varepsilon_{\mu_{ab}\nu_{12}}t_{\mu_{c}}^{\nu_{12}\left(s_{1}s_{2}\right)}=\varepsilon_{\mu_{ab}\nu_{12}}\left(K_{1\mu_{c}}K_{23}^{\nu_{12}}+s_{1}K_{2\mu_{c}}K_{13}^{\nu_{12}}+s_{2}K_{3\mu_{c}}K_{12}^{\nu_{12}}\right)\frac{1}{D_{123}}

(6.11)

where
s_{i}=\pm 1
. We rewrite this equation using
K_{i}=K_{j}+p_{ij}
and

\varepsilon_{\mu_{ab}\nu_{12}}K_{ij}^{\nu_{12}}=\varepsilon_{\mu_{ab}\nu_{12}}p_{ji}^{\nu_{2}}K_{i}^{\nu_{1}}
to achieve the structures
introduced in Section (
3.3
):

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\mu_{ab}\nu_{12}}t_{\mu_{c}}^{\nu_{12}\left(s_{1}s_{2}\right)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\mu_{ab}\nu_{12}}[(1+s_{1})p_{31}^{\nu_{2}}-(1-s_{2})p_{21}^{\nu_{2}}]K_{1}^{\nu_{1}}K_{1\mu_{c}}\frac{1}{D_{123}}

(6.12)

\displaystyle+\varepsilon_{\mu_{ab}\nu_{12}}[p_{21}^{\nu_{1}}p_{32}^{\nu_{2}}K_{1\mu_{c}}+(s_{1}p_{21\mu_{c}}p_{31}^{\nu_{2}}+s_{2}p_{31\mu_{c}}p_{21}^{\nu_{2}})K_{1}^{\nu_{1}}]\frac{1}{D_{123}}.

Hence, final expressions arise directly by replacing vector and tensor
Feynman integrals from Subsection (
3.3.2
). Although four sign
configurations are available, the expression taking
s_{1}=-1
and
s_{2}=1

cancels out. That is straightforward for the first row, but a closer look at
the composition of the following integral is necessary to analyze the
second:

\overline{J}_{3}^{\mu}=J_{3}^{\mu}=i\left(4\pi\right)^{-2}[-p_{21}^{\mu}Z_{10}^{\left(-1\right)}\left(p_{21},p_{31}\right)-p_{31}^{\mu}Z_{01}^{\left(-1\right)}\left(p_{21},p_{31}\right)].

(6.13)

Since it is proportional to external momenta, it leads to symmetric tensors
that vanish when contracted with Levi-Civita symbol. We cast all sign
configurations in the sequence:

\displaystyle 2\varepsilon_{\mu_{ab}\nu_{12}}T_{\mu_{c}}^{\nu_{12}\left(-+\right)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\varepsilon_{\mu_{ab}\nu_{12}}[p_{21}^{\nu_{1}}p_{32}^{\nu_{2}}J_{3\mu_{c}}+(-p_{21\mu_{c}}p_{31}^{\nu_{2}}+p_{31\mu_{c}}p_{21}^{\nu_{2}})J_{3}^{\nu_{1}}]\equiv 0,

(6.14)

\displaystyle 2\varepsilon_{\mu_{ab}\nu_{12}}T_{\mu_{c}}^{\nu_{12}(+-)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4\varepsilon_{\mu_{ab}\nu_{12}}[p_{31}^{\nu_{2}}(J_{3\mu_{c}}^{\nu_{1}}+p_{21\mu_{c}}J_{3}^{\nu_{1}})-p_{21}^{\nu_{2}}(J_{3\mu_{c}}^{\nu_{1}}+p_{31\mu_{c}}J_{3}^{\nu_{1}})]

(6.15)

\displaystyle+(\varepsilon_{\mu_{ab}\nu_{12}}p_{32}^{\nu_{2}}\Delta_{3\mu_{c}}^{\nu_{1}}+\varepsilon_{\mu_{abc}\nu_{1}}p_{32}^{\nu_{1}}I_{\log}),

\displaystyle 2\varepsilon_{\mu_{ab}\nu_{12}}T_{\mu_{c}}^{\nu_{12}\left(--\right)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-4\varepsilon_{\mu_{ab}\nu_{12}}p_{21}^{\nu_{2}}(J_{3\mu_{c}}^{\nu_{1}}+p_{31\mu_{c}}J_{3}^{\nu_{1}})

(6.16)

\displaystyle-(\varepsilon_{\mu_{ab}\nu_{12}}p_{21}^{\nu_{2}}\Delta_{3\mu_{c}}^{\nu_{1}}+\varepsilon_{\mu_{abc}\nu_{1}}p_{21}^{\nu_{1}}I_{\log}),

\displaystyle 2\varepsilon_{\mu_{ab}\nu_{12}}T_{\mu_{c}}^{\nu_{12}\left(++\right)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle+4\varepsilon_{\mu_{ab}\nu_{12}}p_{31}^{\nu_{2}}(J_{3\mu_{c}}^{\nu_{1}}+p_{21\mu_{c}}J_{3}^{\nu_{1}})

(6.17)

\displaystyle+(\varepsilon_{\mu_{ab}\nu_{12}}p_{31}^{\nu_{2}}\Delta_{3\mu_{c}}^{\nu_{1}}+\varepsilon_{\mu_{abc}\nu_{1}}p_{31}^{\nu_{1}}I_{\log}).

Different tensor contributions appear for each trace version from (
6.8
)-(
6.10
). Thus, after disregarding the vanishing contribution, we
identify the corresponding combinations

\displaystyle C_{1\mu_{123}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\varepsilon_{\mu_{13}\nu_{12}}T_{\mu_{2}}^{\nu_{12}\left(+-\right)}+\varepsilon_{\mu_{12}\nu_{12}}T_{\mu_{3}}^{\nu_{12}\left(--\right)}

(6.18)

\displaystyle C_{2\mu_{123}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\varepsilon_{\mu_{12}\nu_{12}}T_{\mu_{3}}^{\nu_{12}\left(++\right)}-\varepsilon_{\mu_{23}\nu_{12}}T_{\mu_{1}}^{\nu_{12}\left(+-\right)}

(6.19)

\displaystyle C_{3\mu_{123}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\varepsilon_{\mu_{23}\nu_{12}}T_{\mu_{1}}^{\nu_{12}\left(--\right)}-\varepsilon_{\mu_{13}\nu_{12}}T_{\mu_{2}}^{\nu_{12}\left(++\right)}.

(6.20)

The sampling of indexes reflects the absence of the index
\mu_{i}
of the
vertex
\Gamma_{i}
in the sign tensors of the
C_{i\mu_{123}}
, enabling
the anticipation of violations of either WIs or RAGFs. That occurs because
this specific index appears in the tensor
\varepsilon_{\mu_{ab}\nu_{12}}T_{\mu_{i}}^{\nu_{12}\left(-,+\right)}
, which is finite and
identically zero, present in each of the above expressions before
integration.

Let us return to the last row of Eqs. (
6.8
)-(
6.10
), which
corresponds to 1st-order odd triangles. The precise identifications among
the possibilities occur when replacing the vertex configurations in the
general integrand (
6.1
); however, all of them are proportional to
ASS

amplitude:

t_{\mu_{i}}^{ASS}=4i\varepsilon_{\mu_{i}\nu_{123}}K_{123}^{\nu_{123}}\frac{1}{D_{123}}=4i\varepsilon_{\mu_{i}\nu_{123}}p_{21}^{\nu_{2}}p_{31}^{\nu_{3}}K_{1}^{\nu_{1}}\frac{1}{D_{123}}.

(6.21)

We already performed some simplifications through the same resources from
the tensor discussion (beginning of the previous paragraph). After
integration, this function depends on the Feynman integral
J_{3}^{\nu_{1}}
. Since this object is a finite tensor proportional to external momenta
p_{ij}
, the contraction with the Levi-Civita symbol necessarily vanishes

T_{\mu_{i}}^{ASS}=4i\varepsilon_{\mu_{i}\nu_{123}}p_{21}^{\nu_{2}}p_{31}^{\nu_{3}}J_{3}^{\nu_{1}}=0.

(6.22)

For this reason, we omit this class of amplitudes from the final triangles.

We left the fourth line of (
6.8
)-(
6.10
) for last since bilinears
get summed with mass terms from the remaining trace. Each investigated case
leads to a subamplitude identified after comparing vertex arrangements in (
6.1
). This result is general: besides
C_{i\mu_{123}}
tensors,
different rank-1 even subamplitudes appear inside each version of rank-3 odd
amplitudes. Table
6.1
accounts for all of these possibilities,
while Appendix (
E
) presents explicit expressions for
subamplitudes. Let us consider the first version of
AVV
to illustrate.
After combining mass terms from Eq. (
6.2
) with bilinears from Eq. (
6.8
), we find the
VPP
subamplitude

\mathrm{sub}(t_{\mu_{123}}^{AVV})_{1}=i\varepsilon_{\mu_{123}\nu_{1}}(t^{VPP})^{\nu_{1}}.

(6.23)

The integrand of this correlator has the structure

(t^{VPP})^{\nu_{1}}=\text{{tr}}[\gamma^{\nu_{1}}S\left(1\right)\gamma_{\ast}S\left(2\right)\gamma_{\ast}S\left(3\right)]=4(-K_{1}^{\nu_{1}}S_{23}+K_{2}^{\nu_{1}}S_{13}-K_{3}^{\nu_{1}}S_{12})\frac{1}{D_{123}},

(6.24)

where the combination
S_{ij}=K_{i}\cdot K_{j}-m^{2}
comes from definition (
4.23
). After reducing the denominator, we perform the integration

\displaystyle(T^{VPP})^{\nu_{1}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2[P_{31}^{\nu_{2}}\Delta_{3\nu_{2}}^{\nu_{1}}+(p_{21}^{\nu_{1}}-p_{32}^{\nu_{1}})I_{\log}]-4\left(p_{21}\cdot p_{32}\right)J_{3}^{\nu_{1}}

(6.25)

\displaystyle+2[(p_{31}^{\nu_{1}}p_{21}^{2}-p_{21}^{\nu_{1}}p_{31}^{2})J_{3}+p_{21}^{\nu_{1}}J_{2}\left(p_{21}\right)-p_{32}^{\nu_{1}}J_{2}\left(p_{32}\right)].

Table 6.1:
Even sub-amplitudes related to each version of 3rd-order odd
amplitudes.

Version/Type

AVV

VAV

VVA

AAA

1

+VPP

+ASP

-APS

-VSS

2

-SAP

+PVP

+PAS

-SVS

3

+SPA

-PSA

+PPV

-SSV

Since all pieces are known, compounding triangle amplitudes is possible. For
instance, the
i
-th version of the
AVV
arises as a combination involving
the
C_{i}
-tensor and the corresponding vector subamplitude. Thus,
consulting Table
6.1
leads to the following associations

\displaystyle(T_{\mu_{123}}^{AVV})_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4iC_{1\mu_{123}}+i\varepsilon_{\mu_{123}\nu_{1}}(T^{VPP})^{\nu_{1}},

(6.26)

\displaystyle(T_{\mu_{123}}^{AVV})_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4iC_{2\mu_{123}}-i\varepsilon_{\mu_{123}\nu_{1}}(T^{SAP})^{\nu_{1}},

(6.27)

\displaystyle(T_{\mu_{123}}^{AVV})_{3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4iC_{3\mu_{123}}+i\varepsilon_{\mu_{123}\nu_{1}}(T^{SPA})^{\nu_{1}}.

(6.28)

The generalization for
VAV
,
VVA
, and
AAA
is straightforward:

(T_{\mu_{123}}^{\Gamma_{1}\Gamma_{2}\Gamma_{3}})_{i}=4iC_{i,\mu_{123}}\pm i\varepsilon_{\mu_{123}\nu_{1}}\left(\text{Corresponding
sub-amplitude}\right)^{\nu_{1}}.

(6.29)

We still want to detail some important points about these amplitudes. To
illustrate this subject, we use tools developed in this section to build up
the first version of
AVV,

\displaystyle(T_{\mu_{123}}^{AVV})_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle S_{1\mu_{123}}-8i\varepsilon_{\mu_{12}\nu_{12}}p_{21}^{\nu_{2}}(J_{3\mu_{3}}^{\nu_{1}}+p_{31\mu_{3}}J_{3}^{\nu_{1}})

(6.30)

\displaystyle-8i\varepsilon_{\mu_{13}\nu_{12}}[p_{31}^{\nu_{2}}(J_{3\mu_{2}}^{\nu_{1}}+p_{21\mu_{2}}J_{3}^{\nu_{1}})-p_{21}^{\nu_{2}}(J_{3\mu_{2}}^{\nu_{1}}+p_{31\mu_{2}}J_{3}^{\nu_{1}})]

\displaystyle-4i\varepsilon_{\mu_{123}\nu_{1}}(p_{21}\cdot p_{32})J_{3}^{\nu_{1}}+2i\varepsilon_{\mu_{123}\nu_{1}}[(p_{31}^{\nu_{1}}p_{21}^{2}-p_{21}^{\nu_{1}}p_{31}^{2})]J_{3}

\displaystyle+2i\varepsilon_{\mu_{123}\nu_{1}}[p_{21}^{\nu_{1}}J_{2}\left(p_{21}\right)-p_{32}^{\nu_{1}}J_{2}\left(p_{32}\right)].

The divergent part of the tensor (
6.18
) comes from Eqs. (
6.15
) and (
6.16
) as

4iC_{1\mu_{123}}=-2i[\varepsilon_{\mu_{13}\nu_{12}}p_{32}^{\nu_{2}}\Delta_{3\mu_{2}}^{\nu_{1}}+\varepsilon_{\mu_{12}\nu_{12}}p_{21}^{\nu_{2}}\Delta_{3\mu_{3}}^{\nu_{1}}+\varepsilon_{\mu_{123}\nu_{1}}(p_{21}^{\nu_{1}}-p_{32}^{\nu_{1}})I_{\log}].

When combined with the
VPP
subamplitude, we acknowledge the exact
cancellation of the object
I_{\log}
as it occurs for all investigated
versions. Thus, surface terms compound the whole structure of divergences

S_{1\mu_{123}}=-2i(\varepsilon_{\mu_{13}\nu_{12}}p_{32}^{\nu_{2}}\Delta_{3\mu_{2}}^{\nu_{1}}+\varepsilon_{\mu_{12}\nu_{12}}p_{21}^{\nu_{2}}\Delta_{3\mu_{3}}^{\nu_{1}})+2i\varepsilon_{\mu_{123}\nu_{1}}P_{31}^{\nu_{2}}\Delta_{3\nu_{2}}^{\nu_{1}}.

(6.31)

Moreover, contributions from vector subamplitudes exhibit arbitrary momenta
P_{ij}=k_{i}+k_{j}
as coefficients. We stress that the divergent content is
shared; the first version of amplitudes
AVV
,
VAV
,
VVA
, and
AAA

contains the same structure (
6.31
). That is a feature of the specific
version and not on the vertex content of the diagram. For later use, we
define the other sets of surface terms

\displaystyle S_{2\mu_{123}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2i(\varepsilon_{\mu_{12}\nu_{12}}p_{31}^{\nu_{2}}\Delta_{3\mu_{3}}^{\nu_{1}}+\varepsilon_{\mu_{23}\nu_{12}}p_{32}^{\nu_{2}}\Delta_{3\mu_{1}}^{\nu_{1}})+2i\varepsilon_{\mu_{123}\nu_{1}}P_{21}^{\nu_{2}}\Delta_{3\nu_{2}}^{\nu_{1}},

(6.32)

\displaystyle S_{3\mu_{123}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2i(\varepsilon_{\mu_{13}\nu_{12}}p_{31}^{\nu_{2}}\Delta_{3\mu_{2}}^{\nu_{1}}-\varepsilon_{\mu_{23}\nu_{12}}p_{21}^{\nu_{2}}\Delta_{3\mu_{1}}^{\nu_{1}})+2i\varepsilon_{\mu_{123}\nu_{1}}P_{32}^{\nu_{2}}\Delta_{3\nu_{2}}^{\nu_{1}}.

(6.33)

That concludes the preliminary discussion on rank-3 triangles, so
investigating RAGFs is possible. That is the subject of the following
sections.

6.1
Relations Among Green Functions and Uniqueness

The next step is to perform momenta contractions that lead to RAGFs
following the recipes in (
2.14
) and (
2.17
). Although they are
algebraic identities at the integrand level, their satisfaction is not
automatic after integration. In parallel to what we saw in the
two-dimensional case, possibilities for Dirac traces and values of surface
terms have important implications for this analysis.

\displaystyle p_{31}^{\mu_{1}}t_{\mu_{123}}^{AVV}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle t_{\mu_{32}}^{AV}\left(1,2\right)-t_{\mu_{23}}^{AV}\left(2,3\right)-2mt_{\mu_{23}}^{PVV}

(6.34)

\displaystyle p_{21}^{\mu_{2}}t_{\mu_{123}}^{AVV}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle t_{\mu_{13}}^{AV}\left(1,3\right)-t_{\mu_{13}}^{AV}\left(2,3\right)

\displaystyle p_{32}^{\mu_{3}}t_{\mu_{123}}^{AVV}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle t_{\mu_{12}}^{AV}\left(1,2\right)-t_{\mu_{12}}^{AV}\left(1,3\right)

\displaystyle p_{31}^{\mu_{1}}t_{\mu_{123}}^{VAV}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle t_{\mu_{23}}^{AV}\left(2,1\right)-t_{\mu_{23}}^{AV}\left(2,3\right)

(6.35)

\displaystyle p_{21}^{\mu_{2}}t_{\mu_{123}}^{VAV}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle t_{\mu_{31}}^{AV}\left(3,1\right)-t_{\mu_{13}}^{AV}\left(2,3\right)+2mt_{\mu_{13}}^{VPV}

\displaystyle p_{32}^{\mu_{3}}t_{\mu_{123}}^{VAV}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle t_{\mu_{21}}^{AV}\left(2,1\right)-t_{\mu_{21}}^{AV}\left(3,1\right)

\displaystyle p_{31}^{\mu_{1}}t_{\mu_{123}}^{VVA}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle t_{\mu_{32}}^{AV}\left(1,2\right)-t_{\mu_{32}}^{AV}\left(3,2\right)

(6.36)

\displaystyle p_{21}^{\mu_{2}}t_{\mu_{123}}^{VVA}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle t_{\mu_{31}}^{AV}\left(3,1\right)-t_{\mu_{31}}^{AV}\left(3,2\right)

\displaystyle p_{32}^{\mu_{3}}t_{\mu_{123}}^{VVA}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle t_{\mu_{12}}^{AV}\left(1,2\right)-t_{\mu_{21}}^{AV}\left(3,1\right)+2mt_{\mu_{12}}^{VVP}

\displaystyle p_{31}^{\mu_{1}}t_{\mu_{123}}^{AAA}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle t_{\mu_{23}}^{AV}\left(2,1\right)-t_{\mu_{32}}^{AV}\left(3,2\right)-2mt_{\mu_{23}}^{PAA}

(6.37)

\displaystyle p_{21}^{\mu_{2}}t_{\mu_{123}}^{AAA}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle t_{\mu_{13}}^{AV}\left(1,3\right)-t_{\mu_{31}}^{AV}\left(3,2\right)+2mt_{\mu_{13}}^{APA}

\displaystyle p_{32}^{\mu_{3}}t_{\mu_{123}}^{AAA}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle t_{\mu_{21}}^{AV}\left(2,1\right)-t_{\mu_{12}}^{AV}\left(1,3\right)+2mt_{\mu_{12}}^{AAP}

Let us introduce the structures that emerged within the relations above.
First, the RHS’s three-point functions are finite tensors external momenta
dependent. That is transparent due to their connection with finite Feynman
integrals introduced in Subsection (
3.2.2
), so we only remove the
overbar notation from corresponding tensors
\bar{J}_{3}^{\nu_{1}}=J_{3}^{\nu_{1}}
and
\bar{J}_{3}=J_{3}
. We have for single axial
triangles

\displaystyle-2mT_{\mu_{23}}^{PVV}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\mu_{23}\nu_{12}}p_{21}^{\nu_{1}}p_{32}^{\nu_{2}}(8im^{2}J_{3}),

(6.38)

\displaystyle 2mT_{\mu_{13}}^{VPV}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\mu_{13}\nu_{12}}p_{21}^{\nu_{1}}p_{32}^{\nu_{2}}(8im^{2}J_{3}),

(6.39)

\displaystyle 2mT_{\mu_{12}}^{VVP}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\mu_{12}\nu_{12}}p_{21}^{\nu_{1}}p_{32}^{\nu_{2}}(-8im^{2}J_{3}),

(6.40)

while momenta contractions for the triple axial triangle lead to

\displaystyle-2mT_{\mu_{23}}^{PAA}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\mu_{23}\nu_{12}}p_{31}^{\nu_{2}}[8im^{2}(2J_{3}^{\nu_{1}}+p_{21}^{\nu_{1}}J_{3})],

(6.41)

\displaystyle 2mT_{\mu_{13}}^{APA}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\mu_{13}\nu_{12}}p_{21}^{\nu_{2}}[-8im^{2}(2J_{3}^{\nu_{1}}+p_{31}^{\nu_{1}}J_{3})],

(6.42)

\displaystyle 2mT_{\mu_{12}}^{AAP}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\mu_{12}\nu_{12}}p_{32}^{\nu_{2}}[8im^{2}(2J_{3}^{\nu_{1}}+p_{21}^{\nu_{1}}J_{3})].

(6.43)

These amplitudes have a low-energy behavior that we aim to explore in
connection with RAGFs in Sections (
6.2
) and (
6.3
). Since
they depend on functions
Z_{n_{1}n_{2}}^{\left(-1\right)}
(
3.40
) through the scalar three-point integral
J_{3}=i(4\pi)^{-2}Z_{00}^{\left(-1\right)}
and the vector one (
6.13
). We use (
3.42
) to
determine the behavior of these tensors when all bilinears in their momenta
are zero:

\displaystyle\left.-2mT_{\mu_{23}}^{PVV}\right|_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}};\quad\left.2mT_{\mu_{13}}^{VPV}\right|_{0}=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}};\quad\left.2mT_{\mu_{12}}^{VVP}\right|_{0}=-\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}};

(6.44)

\displaystyle\left.-2mT_{\mu_{23}}^{PAA}\right|_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{3(2\pi)^{2}};\quad\left.2mT_{\mu_{13}}^{APA}\right|_{0}=\frac{1}{3(2\pi)^{2}};\quad\left.2mT_{\mu_{12}}^{AAP}\right|_{0}=-\frac{1}{3(2\pi)^{2}}.

(6.45)

Each term above is multiplied by the corresponding tensor
\varepsilon_{\mu_{kl}\nu_{12}}p_{21}^{\nu_{1}}p_{32}^{\nu_{2}}
with
k<l

Second, the other structures that appeared in the RAGFs are

As contributions (exclusively) on the external momentum cancel out in the

and
k_{b}
),
combinations seen in the RAGFs above arise:

\displaystyle T_{\mu_{32}}^{AV}\left(1,2\right)-T_{\mu_{23}}^{AV}\left(2,3\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2i\varepsilon_{\mu_{23}\nu_{12}}\left(p_{21}^{\nu_{2}}P_{12}^{\nu_{3}}+p_{32}^{\nu_{2}}P_{32}^{\nu_{3}}\right)\Delta_{3\nu_{3}}^{\nu_{1}}

\displaystyle-2i\varepsilon_{\mu_{13}\nu_{12}}\left(p_{32}^{\nu_{2}}P_{32}^{\nu_{3}}-p_{31}^{\nu_{2}}P_{31}^{\nu_{3}}\right)\Delta_{3\nu_{3}}^{\nu_{1}}

(6.48)

\displaystyle T_{\mu_{12}}^{AV}\left(1,2\right)-T_{\mu_{12}}^{AV}\left(1,3\right)

\displaystyle-2i\varepsilon_{\mu_{12}\nu_{12}}\left(p_{31}^{\nu_{2}}P_{31}^{\nu_{3}}-p_{21}^{\nu_{2}}P_{21}^{\nu_{3}}\right)\Delta_{3\nu_{3}}^{\nu_{1}}.

To verify RAGFs, we must contract external momenta with the explicit forms
of amplitudes. Observe the finite contributions displayed in the example (

) to clarify operations involving finite contributions.
These results use well-defined relations involving finite quantities. After

-tensors to identify

\displaystyle 2p_{21}^{\nu_{2}}J_{3\nu_{2}}^{\nu_{1}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-p_{21}^{2}J_{3}^{\nu_{1}}+J_{2}^{\nu_{1}}\left(p_{31}\right)+J_{2}^{\nu_{1}}\left(p_{32}\right)+p_{31}^{\nu_{1}}J_{2}\left(p_{32}\right),

(6.50)

\displaystyle 2p_{31}^{\nu_{2}}J_{3\nu_{2}}^{\nu_{1}}

\displaystyle-p_{31}^{2}J_{3}^{\nu_{1}}+J_{2}^{\nu_{1}}\left(p_{21}\right)+J_{2}^{\nu_{1}}\left(p_{32}\right)+p_{31}^{\nu_{1}}J_{2}\left(p_{32}\right),

(6.51)

\displaystyle 2J_{3\nu_{1}}^{\nu_{1}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2m^{2}J_{3}+2J_{2}\left(p_{32}\right)+i\left(4\pi\right)^{-2},

(6.52)

and vector integrals

\displaystyle 2p_{21\nu_{1}}J_{3}^{\nu_{1}}

\displaystyle-p_{21}^{2}J_{3}+J_{2}\left(p_{31}\right)-J_{2}\left(p_{32}\right),

(6.53)

\displaystyle 2p_{31\nu_{1}}J_{3}^{\nu_{1}}

\displaystyle-p_{31}^{2}J_{3}+J_{2}\left(p_{21}\right)-J_{2}\left(p_{32}\right).

(6.54)

This pattern repeats for the first version of the other amplitudes (
