Chapter 7
Gravitational Perturbative Amplitudes

The quantization of fermionic fields is according to the canonical rules of
Quantum Field Theory. To introduce these fields in a curved space, we
associate to space-time a Lorentz manifold, in which each point has a plane
space tangent to it. The connection between the two spaces is through
vielbein fields defined by

\displaystyle g_{\mu\nu}\left(x\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\eta_{ab}e_{\mu}^{a}\left(x\right)e_{\nu}^{b}\left(x\right)

(7.1)

\displaystyle\eta_{ab}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathrm{diag}\left(1,-1-1-1\right)

(7.2)

\displaystyle e_{a}^{\mu}e_{\mu}^{b}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta_{a}^{b};\qquad e_{a}^{\mu}e_{\nu}^{a}=\delta_{\nu}^{\mu}.

(7.3)

These fields work in such a way as to transform the coordinate basis into an
orthonormal basis. Through that basis, it is possible to introduce locally
the Clifford algebra whose representations the spinor field can be defined.
The algebra acquires a local character,

\displaystyle\gamma^{\mu}\left(x\right)

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle=e_{a}^{\mu}\left(x\right)\gamma^{a}

(7.4)

\displaystyle\left\{\gamma_{\mu}\left(x\right),\gamma_{\nu}\left(x\right)\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2g_{\mu\nu}\left(x\right)

(7.5)

\displaystyle\left\{\gamma^{a},\gamma^{b}\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\eta^{ab}

(7.6)

\displaystyle\gamma_{\left[ab\right]}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left[\gamma_{a},\gamma_{b}\right];

(7.7)

the last term
\gamma_{\left[ab\right]}/2
corresponds to the spinor
generator to the Lorentz group.

In this way, we will introduce a covariant generalization of the equations
formulated in flat spacetime to introduce fermions coupled to a spacetime
with arbitrary metrics. The action
S
must be invariant by Lorentz
transformations and general transformations of coordinates. We start by
considering the flat-space real Lagrangian

\mathcal{L}=\frac{1}{2}[i\bar{\psi}\gamma^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\psi-i\left(\partial_{\mu}\bar{\psi}\right)\gamma^{\mu}\psi]=\frac{1}{2}[i\bar{\psi}\gamma^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\psi+\left(i\bar{\psi}\gamma^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\psi\right)^{\dagger}],

(7.8)

and replace the covariant for the flat-spacetime metric in coordinate and
orthonormal frame
\partial_{\mu}
(cartesian one) by the spinor covariant
derivative in an arbitrary coordinate frame (but still flat geometry), we
have

\nabla_{\mu}\psi:=\partial_{\mu}\psi+\frac{1}{4}\omega_{\mu}^{\penalty\ \penalty\ ab}\gamma_{\left[ab\right]}\psi;\qquad\text{and\qquad}\nabla_{\mu}\bar{\psi}:=\partial_{\mu}\bar{\psi}-\frac{1}{4}\bar{\psi}\omega_{\mu}^{\penalty\ \penalty\ ab}\gamma_{\left[ab\right]}.

(7.9)

We used
\gamma_{0}\gamma_{\left[ab\right]}^{\dagger}\gamma_{0}=-\gamma_{\left[ab\right]}
in defining last equation;
\omega_{\mu}^{\penalty\ \penalty\ ab}\
are components of metric-compatible spin connection

\displaystyle\omega_{\hskip 8.19447pt\mu}^{ab}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\omega_{\hskip 8.19447ptc\mu}^{a}\eta^{cb}

(7.10)

\displaystyle\omega_{\hskip 8.19447ptc\mu}^{a}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e_{\hskip 8.19447pt\nu}^{a}\partial_{\mu}e_{c}^{\hskip 8.19447pt\nu}+e_{c}^{\hskip 8.19447pt\nu}e_{\hskip 8.19447pt\lambda}^{a}\Gamma_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda},

(7.11)

being
\Gamma_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}
the components of the connection in
the coordinate basis. Then, we allow the metric to correspond to a curved
background geometry, and thereby, the fermion propagation will be
classically given by

S=\int_{\mathcal{M}}\mathrm{d}^{2}xe\left(x\right)\frac{i}{2}\left[\bar{\psi}\gamma^{\mu}\nabla_{\mu}\psi-\left(\nabla_{\mu}\bar{\psi}\right)\gamma^{\mu}\psi\right],

(7.12)

where we introduced the scalar density
e\left(x\right)=\sqrt{\left|g\left(x\right)\right|}
in the volume 2-form d
V=\sqrt{\left|g\left(x\right)\right|}\mathrm{d}x_{1}\mathrm{d}x_{2}
,
g\left(x\right)=\det g_{\mu\nu}
, and modulus is due to the Lorentz
signature.

The extremization of action yields the motion’s equations:
\nabla_{\mu}\psi=0
and
\nabla_{\mu}\bar{\psi}=0.
Additionally, in 2D, the term
coupling to the spin-connection drops out from the action

\frac{1}{4}\omega_{\mu}^{\penalty\ \penalty\ ab}e_{c}^{\mu}\bar{\psi}\{\gamma^{c},\gamma_{\left[ab\right]}\}\psi=0,

(7.13)

due to the in this dimension
\gamma_{\left[ab\right]}=-i\gamma_{\ast}\varepsilon_{ab}
and
\left\{\gamma^{c},\gamma_{\ast}\right\}=0
.
Therefore, we adopt Weyl fermions henceforth, and the action simplifies to

S=\frac{i}{2}\int_{\mathcal{M}}\mathrm{d}^{2}xe\left(x\right)e_{a}^{\mu}[\bar{\psi}\gamma^{a}\overleftrightarrow{\partial}_{\mu}P_{\pm}\psi],

(7.14)

where the chiral projectors are given by
P_{\pm}=\left(1\pm\gamma_{\ast}\right)/2,
being that the chiral matrix (
2.3
) is
\gamma_{\ast}=\varepsilon_{ab}\gamma^{a}\gamma^{b}
and the ’flat’ Levi-Cevita
symbol is normalized by
\varepsilon^{01}=1
(it is a tensor density with
world indices).

The gravitational field appears only as a background field, without being
necessarily quantized and without associated dynamics. Then, we consider the
approximation expanding in powers of
h_{\mu\nu}
around the Minkowski
metric

\displaystyle g_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\eta_{\mu\nu}+\kappa h_{\mu\nu}

(7.15)

\displaystyle g^{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\eta^{\mu\nu}-\kappa h_{\mu\nu}+\mathcal{O}\left(\kappa^{2}\right).

(7.16)

\displaystyle e_{\mu}^{a}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta_{\mu}^{a}+\frac{1}{2}\kappa h_{\mu}^{a};\text{\qquad}e_{a}^{\mu}=\delta_{a}^{\mu}-\frac{1}{2}\kappa h_{a}^{\mu};\text{\qquad}e=1+\frac{1}{2}\kappa h_{\mu}^{\mu}.

(7.17)

We may expand in
e\left(x\right)
and inverse vielbein
e_{a}^{\mu}

independently; in this way, we would get

\frac{i}{2}e\left(x\right)e_{a}^{\mu}[\bar{\psi}\gamma^{a}\overleftrightarrow{\partial}_{\mu}\psi]=\frac{i}{2}\bar{\psi}\gamma^{\mu}\overleftrightarrow{\partial}_{\mu}\psi-\frac{1}{2}h^{\mu\nu}\left[\frac{i}{4}\bar{\psi}\gamma_{(\nu}\overleftrightarrow{\partial}_{\mu)}\psi-\frac{i}{2}\eta_{\mu\nu}\bar{\psi}\overleftrightarrow{\not{\partial}}\psi\right]+\mathcal{O}\left(h^{2}\right),

(7.18)

where
\overleftrightarrow{\not{\partial}}=\gamma^{\rho}\overleftrightarrow{\partial}_{\rho}
. The energy-momentum tensor, in this
linearized approximation, reads

T_{\mu\nu}^{\prime}=\frac{i}{4}(\bar{\psi}\gamma_{\nu}\overleftrightarrow{\partial}_{\mu}\psi+\bar{\psi}\gamma_{\mu}\overleftrightarrow{\partial}_{\nu}\psi)-\frac{i}{2}\eta_{\mu\nu}\bar{\psi}\overleftrightarrow{\not{\partial}}\psi.

(7.19)

Alternatively, we can absorb the
e=\sqrt{\left|g\right|}
into a
redefinition of
e^{1/2}\psi=\Psi
see Bonara et al. (
[
76
]
)
in Appendix B of that reference. Therefore, we have

S=\frac{i}{2}\int\mathrm{d}^{2}xe_{a}^{\mu}(\bar{\Psi}\gamma^{a}\overleftrightarrow{\partial}_{\mu}\Psi)=\frac{i}{2}\int\mathrm{d}^{2}x[\bar{\Psi}\gamma^{\mu}\overleftrightarrow{\partial}_{\mu}\Psi+\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{int}}\left(h,\Psi\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(h^{2}\right)].

(7.20)

In this way, the interaction Lagrangian
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{int}}
is
still defined as

\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{int}}\left(h,\Psi\right)=-\frac{1}{2}h^{\mu\nu}\left[\frac{i}{4}(\bar{\Psi}e_{a\mu}\gamma^{a}\overleftrightarrow{\partial}_{\nu}P_{\pm}\Psi+\bar{\Psi}e_{av}\gamma^{a}\overleftrightarrow{\partial}_{\mu}P_{\pm}\Psi)\right]=-\frac{1}{2}h^{\mu\nu}T_{\mu\nu}.

(7.21)

Then the linearized approximation of the energy-momentum tensor definition
follows as

T_{\mu\nu}=\frac{i}{4}\bar{\Psi}\gamma_{(\mu}\overleftrightarrow{\partial}_{\nu)}P_{\pm}\Psi.

(7.22)

From interaction Lagrangian follows the Feynman rules that will be used in
this work. The two-point gravitational amplitude is

T_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}^{G}\left(q\right)=i\int\mathrm{d}^{2}xe^{iq\cdot x}\left\langle 0\right|T\left[T_{\mu\nu}\left(x\right),T_{\alpha\beta}\left(0\right)\right]\left|0\right\rangle.

(7.23)

Moreover, the vertices of the perturbative amplitudes relative to the
interaction between the graviton and a fermion-antifermion pair are

\Gamma_{\mu\nu}^{G}=-\frac{i}{4}[\gamma_{\mu}\left(K_{1}+K_{2}\right)_{\nu}+\gamma_{\nu}\left(K_{1}+K_{2}\right)_{\mu}]P_{\pm}.

(7.24)

At the trace level, the gravitational amplitude of our interest is, see the
figure
7.1
,

t_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}^{G}=\mathrm{tr}[\Gamma_{\mu\nu}^{G}S\left(1\right)\Gamma_{\alpha\beta}^{G}S\left(2\right)].

(7.25)

Figure 7.1:
The diagram for two-point function of the the linearized energy
momentum tensor.

After integration, we will call
T_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}^{G}
. The total
amplitude with massive propagators is

\displaystyle\left(i64\right)T_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}^{G}\left(q\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{2}}\mathrm{tr}[(1\pm\gamma_{\ast})\gamma_{(\mu}(K_{1}+K_{2})_{\nu)}S\left(1\right)

(7.26)

\displaystyle\times(1\pm\gamma_{\ast})\gamma_{(\alpha}(K_{1}+K_{2})_{\beta)}S\left(2\right)].

We recall that the fermionic propagator is given by (
2.7
).

We will offer some layers of notations to devise an organizational scheme to
deal with this amplitude, as our approach presents multiple characteristics
and complexities. For the first one, let us break it down into four basic
permutations, given by

t_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}^{G}=-\frac{i}{64}\left(\hat{t}_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}^{G}+\hat{t}_{\mu\nu\beta\alpha}^{G}+\hat{t}_{\nu\mu\alpha\beta}^{G}+\hat{t}_{\nu\mu\beta\alpha}^{G}\right).

(7.27)

The structures presented above can be identified as

\hat{t}_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}^{G}=(K_{1\nu}+K_{2\nu})(K_{1\beta}+K_{2\beta})\mathrm{tr}[(1\pm\gamma_{\ast})\gamma_{\mu}S\left(1\right)(1\pm\gamma_{\ast})\gamma_{\alpha}S\left(2\right)].

(7.28)

The other three tensors come from the permutation
\mu\leftrightarrow\alpha,
followed by
\nu\leftrightarrow\beta
.

Any computational element developed to this permutation can be mirrored in
the others. Second step: expanding the products like
(1\pm\gamma_{\ast})\gamma_{\mu}
, we identify the integrand of typical fermionic amplitudes
as the one explored in the previous chapters. Explicitly

\hat{t}_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}^{G}=(K_{1\nu}+K_{2\nu})(K_{1\beta}+K_{2\beta})\left[t_{\mu\alpha}^{VV}+t_{\mu\alpha}^{AA}\pm t_{\mu\alpha}^{AV}\pm t_{\mu\alpha}^{VA}\right].

(7.29)

When integrated, we recognize another element in this decomposition layer,
allowing us to write the basic permutation for the structure below

\mathcal{T}_{\mu\alpha\nu\beta}^{\Gamma_{1}\Gamma_{2}}=\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{2}}\left(K_{1}+K_{2}\right)_{\nu}\left(K_{1}+K_{2}\right)_{\beta}[t_{\mu\alpha}^{\Gamma_{1}\Gamma_{2}}\left(k_{1},k_{2}\right)],

(7.30)

where the vertices are
\Gamma_{i}\in\{1,\gamma_{\ast},\gamma_{\mu},\gamma_{\ast}\gamma_{\mu}\}
, see (
2.2
). This last
equation will be constructed explicitly in the next chapter since it
comprises even more fundamental components. To cast these components, we
observe that
K_{2}-K_{1}=q\rightarrow K_{1}+K_{2}=2K_{1}+q.

Furthermore, expanding the Eq. (
7.30
) we write this combination

\mathcal{T}_{\mu\alpha\nu\beta}^{\Gamma_{12}}=4T_{\mu\alpha;\nu\beta}^{\Gamma_{12}}+2q_{\nu}T_{\mu\alpha;\beta}^{\Gamma_{12}}+2q_{\beta}T_{\mu\alpha;\nu}^{\Gamma_{12}}+q_{\nu}q_{\beta}T_{\mu\alpha}^{\Gamma_{12}}.

(7.31)

We must define what we mean by
T_{\mu\alpha;\nu\beta}^{\Gamma_{12}}
,
T_{\mu\alpha;\beta}^{\Gamma_{12}}
, and
T_{\mu\alpha;\nu}^{\Gamma_{12}}
, which we call derivative amplitudes for the sake of simplicity. As
an example, we have

T_{\mu\alpha;\nu\beta}^{VV}=\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{2}}t_{\mu\alpha;\nu\beta}^{VV}=\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{2}}K_{1\nu}K_{1\beta}\left(t_{\mu\alpha}^{VV}\right).

(7.32)

Derivative two-point amplitudes are defined even to
\Gamma_{i}
that do
not carry Lorentz indexes,

\displaystyle T_{\_;\alpha_{1}}^{\Gamma_{1}\Gamma_{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{2}}t_{\_;\alpha_{1}}^{\Gamma_{1}\Gamma_{2}}=\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{2}}K_{1\alpha_{1}}\mathrm{tr}[\Gamma_{1}S\left(1\right)\Gamma_{2}S\left(2\right)]

(7.33)

\displaystyle T_{\_;\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}^{\Gamma_{1}\Gamma_{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{2}}t_{\_;\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}^{\Gamma_{1}\Gamma_{2}}=\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{2}}K_{1\alpha_{1}}K_{1\alpha_{2}}\mathrm{tr}[\Gamma_{1}S\left(1\right)\Gamma_{2}S\left(2\right)].

(7.34)

When vertices to the matrix
\Gamma_{i}
have Lorentz indices, the notation
will carry such indices in the position we left a blank space. Indexes
\alpha_{i}
attached to factor
K_{1}
are derivative indexes. The
\mathcal{T}_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}^{\Gamma_{12}}
contain only a subset of
general amplitudes we have defined in our last layer. Typical amplitudes
associated with
T^{\Gamma_{1}\Gamma_{2}}
are the ones investigated in
Chapter (
4
). On the other hand, amplitudes as (
7.33
)-(
7.34
) carrying derivative indices are the new ingredients to comprise
two-point functions of the energy-momentum tensor.

To illustrate the notation, let us take a derivative amplitude that is not
part of the permutations
\mathcal{T}_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}^{\Gamma_{12}}
, by example selecting
\Gamma_{1}=S
and
\Gamma_{2}=V
, we have

t_{\mu;\nu}^{SV}=K_{1\nu}t_{\mu}^{SV}=K_{1\nu}\mathrm{tr}[S\left(1\right)\gamma_{\mu}\left(1\right)].

(7.35)

Note that the index
\nu
appearing after the semicolon is a derivative
index. It may happen that integration, through our technique, returns an
expression symmetric in the indices, being this amplitude an example
T_{\mu;\nu}^{SV}=

T_{\nu;\mu}^{SV}
as we will see. Besides these comments,
introducing these general definitions is crucial because they are all
related through RAGFs.

Relations relevant to this chapter arise from two types of momentum
contraction and traces, e.g.,
g^{\mu\alpha}t_{\mu\nu;\alpha\beta}^{VV}=mt_{\nu;\beta}^{SV}+t_{\nu;\beta}^{V}\left(k_{2}\right)
. The
one-point functions are part of the set:

\displaystyle t^{\Gamma_{1}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathrm{tr}\left[\Gamma_{1}S\left(k_{i}\right)\right];

(7.36)

\displaystyle t_{\_;\alpha_{1}}^{\Gamma_{1}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle K_{1\alpha_{1}}\mathrm{tr}\left[\Gamma_{1}S\left(k_{i}\right)\right];

(7.37)

\displaystyle t_{\_;\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}^{\Gamma_{1}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle K_{1\alpha_{1}}K_{1\alpha_{2}}\mathrm{tr}\left[\Gamma_{1}S\left(k_{i}\right)\right].

(7.38)

Amplitudes
t^{\Gamma_{1}}
and their integrals are the ones used for RAGF
investigations, fully developed in Chapter (
4
). When integrated,
they get a capital letter also.

To systematically analyze
\hat{t}_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}^{G}
, we split it
in even and odd tensors: amplitudes with two vector vertices, called
VV
,
and two axial vertices, called
AA
, are even, and amplitudes with composite
vertices,
AV
and
VA
, are odd. For this permutation of indices, we get

\hat{T}_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}^{G}=\hat{T}_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}^{V}+\hat{T}_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}^{A},

(7.39)

where each of the sectors above has the following combination of amplitudes,

\displaystyle\hat{T}_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}^{V}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\mu\alpha\nu\beta}^{VV}+\mathcal{T}_{\mu\alpha\nu\beta}^{AA}

(7.40)

\displaystyle\hat{T}_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}^{A}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\pm\left(\mathcal{T}_{\mu\alpha\nu\beta}^{AV}+\mathcal{T}_{\mu\alpha\nu\beta}^{VA}\right).

(7.41)

The disposition of indices can be a trick to avoid confusion. Observe the
indexes in
\hat{T}_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}^{V}
, we chose the sequence
\mu\nu\alpha\beta
since they come from
T^{G}
, however in
\mathcal{T}_{\mu\alpha;\nu\beta}^{VV}
the disposition emphasizes that the last two
indices correspond to derivative type, what is quite helpful in the
calculations. The basic permutations above (
\mathcal{T}_{\mu\alpha\nu\beta}^{\Gamma_{1}\Gamma_{2}}
) are shown here to make clear the
expansion in terms of derivatives structures

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\mu\alpha\nu\beta}^{VV}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\left(2T_{\mu\alpha;\nu\beta}^{VV}+q_{\nu}T_{\mu\alpha;\beta}^{VV}\right)+q_{\beta}\left(2T_{\mu\alpha;\nu}^{VV}+q_{\nu}T_{\mu\alpha}^{VV}\right)

(7.42)

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\mu\alpha\nu\beta}^{AA}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\left(2T_{\mu\alpha;\nu\beta}^{AA}+q_{\nu}T_{\mu\alpha;\beta}^{AA}\right)+q_{\beta}\left(2T_{\mu\alpha;\nu}^{AA}+q_{\nu}T_{\mu\alpha}^{AA}\right)

(7.43)

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\mu\alpha\nu\beta}^{AV}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\left(2T_{\mu\alpha;\nu\beta}^{AV}+q_{\nu}T_{\mu\alpha;\beta}^{AV}\right)+q_{\beta}\left(2T_{\mu\alpha;\nu}^{AV}+q_{\nu}T_{\mu\alpha}^{AV}\right)

(7.44)

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\mu\alpha\nu\beta}^{VA}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\left(2T_{\mu\alpha;\nu\beta}^{VA}+q_{\nu}T_{\mu\alpha;\beta}^{VA}\right)+q_{\beta}\left(2T_{\mu\alpha;\nu}^{VA}+q_{\nu}T_{\mu\alpha}^{VA}\right).

(7.45)

Summing the four permutations, we get

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}^{V}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\hat{T}_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}^{V}+\hat{T}_{\alpha\nu\mu\beta}^{V}+\hat{T}_{\mu\beta\alpha\nu}^{V}+\hat{T}_{\alpha\beta\mu\nu}^{V}

(7.46)

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}^{A}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\hat{T}_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}^{A}+\hat{T}_{\alpha\nu\mu\beta}^{A}+\hat{T}_{\mu\beta\alpha\nu}^{A}+\hat{T}_{\alpha\beta\mu\nu}^{A}.

(7.47)

Finally inserting in the definition it was given above (
7.27
), we have

T_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}^{G}=-\frac{i}{64}\{[\mathcal{T}_{\mu_{12}\sigma_{12}}^{\mathrm{V}}]+[\mathcal{T}_{\mu_{12}\sigma_{12}}^{\mathrm{A}}]\}.

(7.48)

From these elaborations, we can identify that we have already exposed the
amplitudes with two Lorentz indices
T_{\mu\nu}^{\Gamma_{1}\Gamma_{2}}

in the Chapter (
4
). So our task boils down to calculating only
typical fermionic amplitudes with three and four indices as the following
sequence.

Ward Identities:
The symmetries role is crucial for understanding a
QFT because we have an anomaly in quantum theory when there is a symmetry
violation of the action or the classical conservation law. However, in some
cases, we can avoid these anomalies by imposing severe restrictions on the
physical content of the approach. In this section, we will establish
symmetries and general restrictions that will guide the consistency of the
method and the interpretation of the presence of anomalies.

Classically, the energy-momentum tensor defined in (
7.21
) has
symmetry properties,
T_{\mu\nu}=T_{\nu\mu},
current conservation,
\nabla^{\mu}T_{\mu\nu}=0
, and null trace,
T_{\mu}^{\mu}=0,
see
[
61
]
. These would lead us to the identities for the green
function defined in (
7.23
)

\displaystyle T_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}^{G}\left(q\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle T_{\nu\mu\alpha\beta}^{G}\left(q\right);

(7.49)

\displaystyle q^{\mu}T_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}^{G}\left(q\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0;

(7.50)

\displaystyle g^{\mu\alpha}T_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}^{G}\left(q\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0.

(7.51)

However, the literature shows gravitation as a gauge theory. Therefore these
canonical identities are not necessarily satisfied. We will have an Einstein
anomaly in the violation of general coordinate transformations
(diffeomorphisms) and Lorentz anomalies that imply an antisymmetric part in
the first equation above. In the case of conformal transformations (Weyl
transformations) violations, we will have a Weyl anomaly.

In the context of Einstein and Weyl invariances, we obtain consistency tests
before the symmetry analysis. They arise when we perform
q^{\mu}T_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}^{G}
and
g^{\mu\nu}T_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}^{G}
to their
integrands and obtain relations (based on integration linearity) among the
set of structures defined above, i.e., through RAGFs. Since decomposition (
7.39
) can be done, writing a basic permutation of gravitational
amplitude, in terms of amplitudes with vertices analogous to those of vector
and axial currents, these can be studied individually, as they will present
well-defined relations among them. Their complete introduction and detailed
verification occur in the Section to even amplitudes (
7.3
) and
(
7.4
).

7.1
VV
-
AA
: Even Amplitudes

We aim to determine all components that integrate gravitational amplitudes
while assuming no choice in intermediate steps. In this way, it is possible
to systematize all odd amplitudes in terms of even amplitudes
VV
’s: their
divergent properties are functions of divergent parts from
VV
-amplitudes,
and their finite parts gain an additional term proportional to the mass
squared. So, we will focus on this amplitude, finding a set of definitions
that makes their discussion viable. Otherwise, it would be too long due to
the number of surface terms within the
IReg strategy
. From here on,
all the time, metric symbol
g_{\mu\nu}
means flat metric
g_{\mu\nu}=\eta_{\mu\nu}
.

As we saw in (
4.42
), the expression for the amplitude
VV
is given by

T_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}^{VV}=2\Delta_{2\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}+\theta_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}\left(4m^{2}J_{2}+i/\pi\right).

That also can be written in closed form by Feynman integrals basis, see
Section (
3.3
),

T_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}^{VV}=\mathcal{D}_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}^{VV}+4J_{2\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}+2q_{(\mu_{1}}J_{2\mu_{2})}+g_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}q^{2}J_{2}.

(7.52)

Finite parts come from definitions
J_{2},

J_{2\mu_{i},}
and
J_{2\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}
as combinations of
Z_{k}^{\left(n\right)}
. As for the
divergent part, we collect all divergent terms and combine them in the
definition

\mathcal{D}_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}^{VV}=2\Delta_{2\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}.

(7.53)

Amplitudes with additional factors
K_{1\alpha_{i}}
follow the operations
of those without derivative indices. The effect of this factor is to produce
an algebraic structure similar to
J
-integrals but with higher tensor
degrees. From previous definitions,

\displaystyle t_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2};\alpha_{1}}^{VV}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle K_{1\alpha_{1}}t_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}^{VV}

(7.54)

\displaystyle t_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2};\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}^{VV}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle K_{1\alpha_{1}}K_{1\alpha_{2}}t_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}^{VV}.

(7.55)

Therefore, amplitudes will have a greater degree of divergence, implying
that finite and divergent parts are more complex and lengthier. These are
expressed in Section (
3.3
).

Expressions appear as a standard tensor plus a PP amplitude; see (
4.31
),
thus

\displaystyle t_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{1}}^{VV}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2t_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2};\alpha_{1}}^{\left(+\right)}+g_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}t_{\alpha_{1}}^{PP}

(7.56)

\displaystyle t_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}^{VV}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2t_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2};\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}^{\left(+\right)}+g_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}t_{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}^{PP}.

(7.57)

Tensors
t_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2};\alpha_{1}}^{\left(+\right)}
and
t_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2};\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}^{\left(+\right)}
appearing above are
particular cases of general tensors:

\displaystyle t_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{1}}^{\left(s_{1}\right)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle K_{1\alpha_{1}}\left(K_{1\mu_{1}}K_{2\mu_{2}}+s_{1}K_{1\mu_{2}}K_{2\mu_{1}}\right)\frac{1}{D_{12}}

(7.58)

\displaystyle t_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{12}}^{\left(s_{1}\right)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle K_{1\alpha_{1}}t_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{1}}^{\left(s_{1}\right)};

(7.59)

where
s_{1}=

\pm
, see (
4.24
). For example, in the tensor of
3rd-order, two cases assume are

\displaystyle t_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{1}}^{\left(+\right)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\frac{K_{1\alpha_{1}}K_{1\mu_{1}}K_{1\mu_{2}}}{D_{12}}+\frac{q_{(\mu_{1}}K_{1\mu_{2})}K_{1\alpha_{1}}}{D_{12}}

(7.60)

\displaystyle t_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{1}}^{\left(-\right)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{q_{[\mu_{2}}K_{1\mu_{1}]}K_{1\alpha_{1}}}{D_{12}}.

(7.61)

Moreover, 4th-rank ones naturally get one more
K_{1\alpha_{2}}
factor. To

PP
amplitude (
4.21
), we add a
K_{1\alpha_{1}}
according to our
definitions

t_{\alpha_{1}}^{PP}=K_{1\alpha_{1}}\left(q^{2}\frac{1}{D_{12}}-\frac{1}{D_{1}}-\frac{1}{D_{2}}\right)=K_{1\alpha_{1}}t^{PP},

(7.62)

and with two indices
t_{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}^{PP}=K_{1\alpha_{2}}t_{\alpha_{1}}^{PP}
.

Integrating (
7.56
) using (
7.60
) and (
7.62
), derivative
VV

with three indices become

\displaystyle T_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{1}}^{VV}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4\bar{J}_{2\mu_{12}\alpha_{1}}+2q_{(\mu_{1}}\bar{J}_{2\mu_{2})\alpha_{1}}+g_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}q^{2}J_{2\alpha_{1}}

(7.63)

\displaystyle-g_{\mu_{12}}[\bar{J}_{1\alpha_{1}}\left(k_{2}\right)+\bar{J}_{1\alpha_{1}}\left(k_{1}\right)]+g_{\mu_{12}}q_{\alpha_{1}}\bar{J}_{1}\left(k_{2}\right).

For
VV
with four indices (
7.57
), we have when integrating the
tensor (
7.59
) and
K_{1\alpha_{2}}t_{\alpha_{1}}^{PP}
,

\displaystyle T_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{12}}^{VV}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4\bar{J}_{2\mu_{12}\alpha_{12}}+2q_{(\mu_{1}}\bar{J}_{2\mu_{2})\alpha_{12}}+g_{\mu_{12}}q^{2}\bar{J}_{2\alpha_{12}}

(7.64)

\displaystyle+g_{\mu_{12}}\left[\bar{J}_{1\alpha_{12}}\left(k_{2}\right)-\bar{J}_{1\alpha_{12}}\left(k_{1}\right)\right]-g_{\mu_{12}}\left[q_{(\alpha_{1}}\bar{J}_{1\alpha_{2})}\left(k_{2}\right)-q_{\alpha_{12}}\bar{J}_{1}\left(k_{2}\right)\right].

Additional terms in the
k_{2}
that appear in the
J
’s (with only one
propagator) come from the translations
K_{1}=K_{2}-q
used to define
functions in Section (
3.1
). Remember that barred
J
’s have
finite and divergent parts.

To simplify the exposition of finite and divergent parts from equations (
3.64
), (
3.66
), (
3.71
), and (
3.73
), it is
possible to write the results as

\displaystyle T_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{1}}^{VV}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4J_{2\mu_{12}\alpha_{1}}+2q_{(\mu_{1}}J_{2\mu_{2})\alpha_{1}}+g_{\mu_{12}}q^{2}J_{2\alpha_{1}}+\mathcal{D}_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{1}}^{VV}

(7.65)

\displaystyle T_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{12}}^{VV}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4J_{2\mu_{12}\alpha_{12}}+2q_{(\mu_{1}}J_{2\mu_{2})\alpha_{12}}+g_{\mu_{12}}q^{2}J_{2\alpha_{12}}+\mathcal{D}_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{12}}^{VV}.

(7.66)

In these cases, all divergent terms of integrals and define the 3rd-order
tensor

\mathcal{D}_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{1}}^{VV}=-P^{\nu_{1}}W_{3\mu_{12}\alpha_{1}\nu_{1}}+P_{(\mu_{1}}\Delta_{2\mu_{2}\alpha_{1})}+g_{\mu_{12}}P^{\nu_{1}}\Delta_{2\alpha_{1}\nu_{1}}-q_{\alpha_{1}}\Delta_{2\mu_{12}},

(7.67)

and the 4th-order tensor

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{12}}^{VV}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle+(W_{2\mu_{12}\alpha_{12}}-g_{\mu_{12}}\Delta_{1\alpha_{12}})+g_{\mu_{1}(\alpha_{1}}g_{\alpha_{2})\mu_{2}}I_{\text{{quad}}}+\frac{\Omega_{\mu_{12}\alpha_{12}}}{6q^{2}}I_{\text{{log}}}

(7.68)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{12}(3P^{\nu_{12}}+q^{\nu_{12}})W_{4\mu_{12}\alpha_{12}\nu_{12}}-\frac{1}{4}(P^{\nu_{12}}+q^{\nu_{12}})g_{\mu_{12}}W_{3\alpha_{12}\nu_{12}}

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}P^{\nu_{1}}\left(P_{\mu_{1}}W_{3\mu_{2}\alpha_{12}\nu_{1}}+P_{\mu_{2}}W_{3\mu_{1}\alpha_{12}\nu_{1}}\right)+\frac{1}{2}P^{\nu_{1}}g_{\mu_{12}}\left(P-q\right)_{(\alpha_{1}}\Delta_{2\alpha_{2})\nu_{1}}

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4}(P^{2}+q^{2})W_{3\mu_{12}\alpha_{12}}-\frac{1}{2}P^{\nu_{1}}\left(P-q\right)_{(\alpha_{1}}W_{3\alpha_{2})\mu_{12}\nu_{1}}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{4}[2(\theta_{\mu_{12}}+P_{\mu_{12}})+g_{\mu_{12}}(P^{2}+q^{2})]\Delta_{2\alpha_{12}}+\frac{1}{2}(P-q)_{\alpha_{1}}(P-q)_{\alpha_{2}}\Delta_{2\mu_{12}}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}P_{\mu_{2}}\left(P-q\right)_{(\alpha_{1}}\Delta_{2\alpha_{2})\mu_{1}}+\frac{1}{2}P_{\mu_{1}}(P-q)_{(\alpha_{1}}\Delta_{2\alpha_{2})\mu_{2}}.

We use definition of projectors
\theta_{\mu_{12}}
and
\Omega_{\mu_{12}\alpha_{12}}
as

\displaystyle\theta_{\mu_{12}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}q^{2}-q_{\mu_{1}}q_{\mu_{2}}

(7.69)

\displaystyle\Omega_{\mu_{12}\alpha_{12}}\left(q\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\theta_{\mu_{12}}\theta_{\alpha_{12}}-\left(\theta_{\mu_{1}\alpha_{1}}\theta_{\mu_{2}\alpha_{2}}+\theta_{\mu_{1}\alpha_{2}}\theta_{\mu_{2}\alpha_{1}}\right);

(7.70)

both are transverse; additionally,
\Omega
is traceless in all its
indices. Note that here the projector
\theta_{\mu\nu}

is not
dimensionless
as in Chapter (
4
); it has mass dimension two.

The finite part also can be expressed from explicit functions plus
\mathcal{D}
-tensor

T_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{1}}^{VV}=\frac{i}{2\pi}q_{\alpha_{1}}\theta_{\mu_{12}}(Z_{2}^{\left(-1\right)}-Z_{1}^{\left(-1\right)})+\mathcal{D}_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{1}}^{VV}.

(7.71)

The four-index amplitude is more complicated but can be written in the
projectors

\displaystyle T_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{12}}^{VV}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{i}{4\pi}\frac{1}{q^{2}}\left[-\Omega_{\mu_{12}\alpha_{12}}(2Z_{2}^{\left(0\right)}-Z_{1}^{\left(0\right)})+2\theta_{\mu_{12}}\theta_{\alpha_{12}}(3Z_{2}^{\left(0\right)}-2Z_{1}^{\left(0\right)})\right]

(7.72)

\displaystyle-\frac{i}{4\pi}q_{\alpha_{12}}\theta_{\mu_{12}}(Z_{2}^{\left(-1\right)}-Z_{1}^{\left(-1\right)})+\mathcal{D}_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{12}}^{VV}.

It is possible to maintain closed form in
J
’s, as we will see in RAGF,
through reductions as in Section (
3.2
). In this way, we find
leading amplitudes as a substructure of
T_{\mu_{12}}^{VV},

T_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{1}}^{VV}=-\frac{1}{2}q_{\alpha_{1}}T_{\mu_{12}}^{VV}+\mathcal{D}_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{1}}^{VV}+\frac{1}{2}q_{\alpha_{1}}\mathcal{D}_{\mu_{12}}^{VV}.

(7.73)

Moreover, the same is true for the 4th-order amplitude

\displaystyle T_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{12}}^{VV}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}q_{\alpha_{12}}T_{\mu_{12}}^{VV}+\mathcal{D}_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{12}}^{VV}-\frac{1}{4}q_{\alpha_{12}}\mathcal{D}_{\mu_{12}}^{VV}

(7.74)

\displaystyle+\frac{i}{4\pi}\frac{1}{q^{2}}\left[-\Omega_{\mu_{12}\alpha_{12}}(2Z_{2}^{\left(0\right)}-Z_{1}^{\left(0\right)})+2\theta_{\mu_{12}}\theta_{\alpha_{12}}(3Z_{2}^{\left(0\right)}-2Z_{1}^{\left(0\right)})\right].

The next amplitude to be calculated is the
AA
. Like
VV
, this amplitude
will contribute to the even sector of the gravitational amplitude in (
7.40
). From the chapter on equal masses, after traces, we have expressed
it exactly as (
4.32
). However, writing this result in terms of amplitude

t_{\mu_{12}}^{VV}
plus a scalar function proportional to the metric, is
feasible

t_{\mu_{12}}^{AA}=t_{\mu_{12}}^{VV}-4m^{2}g_{\mu_{12}}\frac{1}{D_{12}}.

(7.75)

This form allows us to write equations directly from definitions for
derivative amplitudes

\displaystyle t_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{1}}^{AA}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle t_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{1}}^{VV}-4m^{2}g_{\mu_{12}}\frac{K_{1\alpha_{1}}}{D_{12}}

(7.76)

\displaystyle t_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{12}}^{AA}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle t_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{12}}^{VV}-4m^{2}g_{\mu_{12}}\frac{K_{1\alpha_{1}}K_{1\alpha_{2}}}{D_{12}},

(7.77)

and their integrals

\displaystyle T_{\mu_{12}}^{AA}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle T_{\mu_{12}}^{VV}-4m^{2}g_{\mu_{12}}J_{2}

(7.78)

\displaystyle T_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{1}}^{AA}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle T_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{1}}^{VV}-4m^{2}g_{\mu_{12}}J_{2\alpha_{1}}

(7.79)

\displaystyle T_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{12}}^{AA}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle T_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{12}}^{VV}-4m^{2}g_{\mu_{12}}\bar{J}_{2\alpha_{12}}.

(7.80)

Additional contributions of massive terms present in this amplitude are
worth noting. For the divergent part, only the amplitude with four indices
has a non-zero term in
\bar{J}_{2\alpha_{12}}
, see (
3.66
).
Integrals appearing in the amplitudes of fewer indices contribute only to
the finite part. However, the 4th-rank amplitude has an additional
contribution as a surface term and
I_{\log}
. The final result is
identical to that obtained from the first form presented.

7.2
AV
-
VA
: Odd amplitudes

We will calculate all odd parts of gravitational amplitude. As seen in (
4.33
), we wrote two-index functions in terms of even ones using
general identity for
2D
,
\gamma_{\ast}\gamma_{\mu_{1}}=-\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}}\gamma^{\nu_{1}}
, present in (
4.15
). For
higher-rank amplitudes, traces operate in the same way but add indices to
the integrals:

(T_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{1}}^{AV})_{1}=-\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}}^{\hskip 8.19447pt\nu_{1}}T_{\nu_{1}\mu_{2};\alpha_{1}}^{VV};\qquad(T_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{1}}^{AV})_{2}=-\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}}^{\hskip 8.19447pt\nu_{1}}T_{\mu_{1}\nu_{1};\alpha_{1}}^{AA}

(7.81)

(T_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{12}}^{AV})_{1}=-\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}}^{\hskip 8.19447pt\nu_{1}}T_{\nu_{1}\mu_{2};\alpha_{12}}^{VV};\qquad(T_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{12}}^{AV})_{2}=-\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}}^{\hskip 8.19447pt\nu_{1}}T_{\mu_{1}\nu_{1};\alpha_{12}}^{AA}.

(7.82)

To complete odd amplitudes, we cast the analogous VA equations:

\displaystyle(T_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{1}}^{VA})_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}}^{\text{\hskip 7.97224pt}\nu_{1}}T_{\nu_{1}\mu_{2};\alpha_{1}}^{AA}\quad(T_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{1}}^{VA})_{2}=-\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}}^{\text{\hskip 7.97224pt}\nu_{1}}T_{\mu_{1}\nu_{1};\alpha_{1}}^{VV};

(7.83)

\displaystyle(T_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{12}}^{VA})_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}}^{\text{\hskip 7.97224pt}\nu_{1}}T_{\nu_{1}\mu_{2};\alpha_{12}}^{AA}\quad(T_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{12}}^{VA})_{2}=-\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}}^{\text{\hskip 7.97224pt}\nu_{1}}T_{\mu_{1}\nu_{1};\alpha_{12}}^{VV}.

(7.84)

The same considerations can be made when using the chiral matrix definition (
4.14
) directly in the Dirac traces. By considering expressions for
amplitudes with additional terms, as in (
4.27
) and (
4.28
),
for amplitudes with derivative vertices, we have

\displaystyle(t_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{1}}^{AV})_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}}^{\text{\hskip 7.97224pt}\nu_{1}}t_{\nu_{1}\mu_{2};\alpha_{1}}^{VV}+2\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}}^{\text{\hskip 7.97224pt}\nu_{1}}t_{\mu_{1}\nu_{1};\alpha_{1}}^{\left(-\right)}+g_{\mu_{12}}t_{\alpha_{1}}^{SP}

(7.85)

\displaystyle(t_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{1}}^{AV})_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}}^{\text{\hskip 7.97224pt}\nu_{1}}t_{\mu_{1}\nu_{1};\alpha_{1}}^{AA}+2\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}}^{\text{\hskip 7.97224pt}\nu_{1}}t_{\nu_{1}\mu_{2};\alpha_{1}}^{\left(-\right)}-g_{\mu_{12}}t_{\alpha_{1}}^{SP}

(7.86)

\displaystyle(t_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{12}}^{AV})_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}}^{\text{\hskip 7.97224pt}\nu_{1}}t_{\nu_{1}\mu_{2};\alpha_{12}}^{VV}+2\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}}^{\text{\hskip 7.97224pt}\nu_{1}}t_{\mu_{1}\nu_{1};\alpha_{12}}^{\left(-\right)}+g_{\mu_{12}}t_{\alpha_{12}}^{SP}

(7.87)

\displaystyle(t_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{12}}^{AV})_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}}^{\text{\hskip 7.97224pt}\nu_{1}}t_{\mu_{1}\nu_{1};\alpha_{12}}^{AA}+2\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}}^{\text{\hskip 7.97224pt}\nu_{1}}t_{\nu_{1}\mu_{2};\alpha_{12}}^{\left(-\right)}-g_{\mu_{12}}t_{\alpha_{12}}^{SP}.

(7.88)

Additional terms combine and cancel out when integrated, so the equations
above reduce to those given in (
7.81
)-(
7.82
). Let us demonstrate
this fact, using the definition (
7.58
) to
t^{\left(-\right)}
at the
beginning of the last section. Thus we have

2\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}}^{\text{\hskip 7.97224pt}\nu_{1}}T_{\mu_{1}\nu_{1};\alpha_{1}}^{\left(-\right)}+g_{\mu_{12}}T_{\alpha_{1}}^{SP}=2\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}\nu_{1}}q_{\mu_{1}}\bar{J}_{2\alpha_{1}}^{\nu_{1}}-2\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}\nu_{1}}q^{\nu_{1}}\bar{J}_{2\mu_{1}\alpha_{1}}+2g_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}\varepsilon_{\nu_{1}\nu_{2}}q^{\nu_{2}}\bar{J}_{2\alpha_{1}}^{\nu_{1}}.

(7.89)

We applied our definitions of
J_{2}
integrals, and employed the identity
below in the las term

\varepsilon_{\nu_{1}\nu_{2}}g_{\mu_{2}\mu_{1}}+\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}\nu_{1}}g_{\nu_{2}\mu_{1}}+\varepsilon_{\nu_{2}\mu_{2}}g_{\nu_{1}\mu_{1}}=0.

(7.90)

It is direct to observe the exact cancellation of the first two terms

2\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}}^{\text{\hskip 7.97224pt}\nu_{1}}T_{\mu_{1}\nu_{1};\alpha_{1}}^{\left(-\right)}+g_{\mu_{12}}T_{\alpha_{1}}^{SP}=0.

(7.91)

That occurs independently of divergent content of
\bar{J}_{2\mu\nu}
. It
is easy to see that the same happens to the analogous terms in the 4th-rank
amplitude’s version,

2\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}}^{\text{\hskip 7.97224pt}\nu_{1}}T_{\mu_{1}\nu_{1};\alpha_{12}}^{\left(-\right)}+g_{\mu_{12}}T_{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}^{SP}=0.

(7.92)

Definitions for the
VA
computed with the definition of the chiral matrix
were not present because the logic and result are the same. As for the
relation between
VV
and
AA
amplitudes, we write from the integrand level

\displaystyle(T_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{1}}^{VA})_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}}^{\text{\hskip 7.97224pt}\nu_{1}}T_{\nu_{1}\mu_{2};\alpha_{1}}^{AA}=-\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}}^{\text{\hskip 7.97224pt}\nu_{1}}(T_{\nu_{1}\mu_{2};\alpha_{1}}^{VV}-4m^{2}g_{\nu_{1}\mu_{2}}J_{2\alpha_{1}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(T_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2};\alpha_{1}}^{AV})_{1}+4m^{2}\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}J_{2\alpha_{1}}.

This relation is satisfied without any conditions. In general, we have

\displaystyle(T_{\mu_{12}}^{VA})_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(T_{\mu_{12}}^{AV})_{i}+4m^{2}\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}J_{2}

(7.93)

\displaystyle(T_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{1}}^{VA})_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(T_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{1}}^{AV})_{i}+4m^{2}\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}J_{2\alpha_{1}}

(7.94)

\displaystyle(T_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{12}}^{VA})_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(T_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{12}}^{AV})_{i}+4m^{2}\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}\bar{J}_{2\alpha_{12}},

(7.95)

where the index
i=1,2
is associated with versions, and the Eqs (
7.93
)-(
7.95
) will often be used to reduce manipulations
required for the gravitational anomaly.

On the other hand, basic and independent versions one and two are only
strictly equivalent with conditions. This fact was worked in Chapters (
4
) and (
5
), where a single mass and two masses in odd
amplitudes were handled. Let us retrieve the explicitly computed result to
establish general results to be used in the sequel

\displaystyle(T_{\mu_{12}}^{AV})_{1}-(T_{\mu_{12}}^{AV})_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2(\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\nu}\Delta_{2\mu_{2}}^{\nu}-\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}\nu}\Delta_{2\mu_{1}}^{\nu})

(7.96)

\displaystyle-(\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}}\theta_{\mu_{2}}^{\nu}-\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}\nu_{1}}\theta_{\mu_{1}}^{\nu})\frac{1}{q^{2}}\left(4m^{2}J_{2}+i/\pi\right)+4\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}m^{2}J_{2}.

We rearrange the finite part using
\varepsilon_{[\mu_{1}\nu}\theta_{\mu_{2}]}^{\nu}=0
and surface terms
\varepsilon_{[\mu_{1}\nu}\Delta_{2\mu_{2}]}^{\nu}=0
,

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\nu}\Delta_{2\mu_{2}}^{\nu}+\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}\mu_{1}}\Delta_{2\nu}^{\nu}+\varepsilon_{\nu\mu_{2}}\Delta_{2\mu_{1}}^{\nu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0=\varepsilon_{[\mu_{1}\nu}\Delta_{2\mu_{2}]}^{\nu},

(7.97)

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\nu}\theta_{\mu_{2}}^{\nu}+\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}\mu_{1}}\theta_{\nu}^{\nu}+\varepsilon_{\nu\mu_{2}}\theta_{\mu_{1}}^{\nu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0=\varepsilon_{[\mu_{1}\nu}\theta_{\mu_{2}]}^{\nu};

(7.98)

hence, the difference between the two versions reduces to

(T_{\mu_{12}}^{AV})_{1}-(T_{\mu_{12}}^{AV})_{2}=-\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}\left(2\Delta_{2\alpha}^{\alpha}+i/\pi\right).

(7.99)

Here we clarify how this result can be written systematically. It boils down
to using the definitions and caveat that each term present represents
complete amplitudes,

\displaystyle T_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}^{VV}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\Delta_{2\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}+\frac{\theta_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}}{q^{2}}\left(4m^{2}J_{2}+i/\pi\right),

(7.100)

\displaystyle T_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}^{AA}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\Delta_{2\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}+\frac{\theta_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}}{q^{2}}\left(4m^{2}J_{2}+i/\pi\right)-g_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}\left(4m^{2}J_{2}\right).

(7.101)

Using (
4.33
), the versions for
AV
-amplitudes arise

\displaystyle(T_{\mu_{12}}^{AV})_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}}^{\hskip 8.19447pt\nu}\Delta_{2\mu_{2}\nu}-\frac{\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\nu}\theta_{\mu_{2}}^{\nu}}{q^{2}}\left(4m^{2}J_{2}+i/\pi\right),

(7.102)

\displaystyle(T_{\mu_{12}}^{AV})_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}}^{\hskip 8.19447pt\nu}\Delta_{2\mu_{1}\nu}-\frac{\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}\nu}\theta_{\mu_{1}}^{\nu}}{q^{2}}\left(4m^{2}J_{2}+i/\pi\right)-\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}\left(4m^{2}J_{2}\right).

(7.103)

After writing the difference between them

(T_{\mu_{12}}^{AV})_{1}-(T_{\mu_{12}}^{AV})_{2}=-\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}}^{\hskip 8.19447pt\nu_{1}}T_{\nu_{1}\mu_{2}}^{VV}+\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}}^{\hskip 8.19447pt\nu_{1}}T_{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}}^{AA},

(7.104)

we take into account identity among
AA
and
VV
(
7.78
):

(T_{\mu_{12}}^{AV})_{1}-(T_{\mu_{12}}^{AV})_{2}=-\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}}^{\hskip 8.19447pt\nu_{1}}T_{\nu_{1}\mu_{2}}^{VV}+\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}}^{\hskip 8.19447pt\nu_{1}}T_{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}}^{VV}-4m^{2}\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}\mu_{1}}J_{2}.

(7.105)

Lastly, employ

\varepsilon_{[\mu_{1}\nu_{1}}\left(T^{VV}\right)_{\hskip 8.19447pt\mu_{2}]}^{\nu_{1}}=0\Leftrightarrow-\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}}^{\hskip 8.19447pt\nu_{1}}T_{\nu_{1}\mu_{2}}^{VV}+\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}}^{\hskip 8.19447pt\nu_{1}}T_{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}}^{VV}=\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}\mu_{1}}\left(g^{\nu_{12}}T_{\nu_{1}\nu_{2}}^{VV}\right),

to reach an expression equivalent to work term by term on the amplitude,

(T_{\mu_{12}}^{AV})_{1}-(T_{\mu_{12}}^{AV})_{2}=\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}\mu_{1}}\left(g^{\nu_{12}}T_{\nu_{1}\nu_{2}}^{VV}-4m^{2}J_{2}\right)=:\Upsilon.

(7.106)

With the help of explicit expression, follows

\Upsilon=2\Delta_{2\rho}^{\rho}+\left(4m^{2}J_{2}+i/\pi\right)-4m^{2}J_{2}=2\Delta_{2\rho}^{\rho}+i/\pi.

(7.107)

As it must be, this condition is equal to that deduced to the equivalence of
basic (
4.52
). For these amplitudes, the equality among independent
expressions is obtained through any possible way to employ the trace of four
gamma matrices and a chiral one.

It is a direct task to identify this condition to higher-rank amplitudes,
namely,

\displaystyle(T_{\mu_{12}}^{AV})_{1}-(T_{\mu_{12}}^{AV})_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}\mu_{1}}\Upsilon

(7.108)

\displaystyle(T_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{1}}^{AV})_{1}-(T_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{1}}^{AV})_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}\mu_{1}}\Upsilon_{\alpha_{1}}

(7.109)

\displaystyle(T_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{12}}^{AV})_{1}-(T_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{12}}^{AV})_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}\mu_{1}}\Upsilon_{\alpha_{12}}.

(7.110)

Due to the relevance of these terms, we present the following definition

\displaystyle\Upsilon

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(g^{\nu_{12}}T_{\nu_{1}\nu_{2}}^{VV}-4m^{2}J_{2}\right)

(7.111)

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{\alpha_{1}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(g^{\nu_{12}}T_{\nu_{1}\nu_{2};\alpha_{1}}^{VV}-4m^{2}J_{2\alpha_{1}}\right)

(7.112)

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{\alpha_{12}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(g^{\nu_{12}}T_{\nu_{1}\nu_{2};\alpha_{12}}^{VV}-4m^{2}J_{2\alpha_{12}}\right).

(7.113)

At the end of calculations, identities of this type must be used in surface
terms and finite parts of amplitudes. This approach simplifies the
conclusions that can be given by exposing hundreds of terms that build up
some of these amplitudes, making that path prohibitively long to be exposed.
The identities only express the vanishing of a complete antisymmetric tensor
of degree three in two dimensions.

The last section exposed detailed results for finite and divergent parts of
core component
VV
amplitudes that appear in RHS of (
7.112
) and (
7.113
). Thus, we take expressions (
7.65
) and (
7.66
) into account to
write

\displaystyle g^{\nu_{12}}T_{\nu_{12};\alpha_{1}}^{VV}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4g^{\nu_{12}}J_{2\nu_{12}\alpha_{1}}+2(2q^{\nu_{1}}J_{2\nu_{1}\alpha_{1}}+q^{2}J_{2\alpha_{1}})+g^{\nu_{12}}\mathcal{D}_{\nu_{12};\alpha_{1}}^{VV}

(7.114)

\displaystyle g^{\nu_{12}}T_{\nu_{12};\alpha_{12}}^{VV}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4g^{\nu_{12}}J_{2\nu_{12}\alpha_{12}}+2(2q^{\nu_{1}}J_{2\nu_{1}\alpha_{12}}+q^{2}J_{2\alpha_{12}})+g^{\nu_{12}}\mathcal{D}_{\nu_{12};\alpha_{12}}^{VV}.

(7.115)

Observe that
J
-functions comprise the entire finite part while
\mathcal{D}^{VV}
-tensor accounts for divergent terms. Therefore, these calculations
require the traces

\displaystyle 4g^{\nu_{12}}J_{2\nu_{12}\alpha_{1}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4m^{2}J_{2\alpha_{1}}-\frac{i}{2\pi}q_{\alpha_{1}}

(7.116)

\displaystyle 4g^{\nu_{12}}J_{2\nu_{12}\alpha_{12}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4m^{2}J_{2\alpha_{12}}-\frac{i}{12\pi}[\theta_{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}\left(q\right)-3q_{\alpha_{1}}q_{\alpha_{2}}],

(7.117)

and relations coming from momentum contractions

\displaystyle 2q^{\nu_{1}}J_{2\nu_{1}\alpha_{1}}+q^{2}J_{2\alpha_{1}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

(7.118)

\displaystyle 2q^{\nu_{1}}J_{2\nu_{1}\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}+q^{2}J_{2\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0;

(7.119)

results derived in Sections (
3.2
) and (
3.3
).
Substituting in (
7.114
) and (
7.115
) yields

\displaystyle g^{\nu_{12}}T_{\nu_{12};\alpha_{1}}^{VV}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4m^{2}J_{2\alpha_{1}}-\frac{i}{2\pi}q_{\alpha_{1}}+g^{\nu_{12}}\mathcal{D}_{\nu_{12};\alpha_{1}}^{VV}

(7.120)

\displaystyle g^{\nu_{12}}T_{\nu_{12};\alpha_{12}}^{VV}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4m^{2}J_{2\alpha_{12}}-\frac{i}{12\pi}(\theta_{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}-3q_{\alpha_{1}}q_{\alpha_{2}})+g^{\nu_{12}}\mathcal{D}_{\nu_{12};\alpha_{12}}^{VV}.

(7.121)

The trace of
\mathcal{D}^{VV}
-tensor, their explicit forms from (
7.67
) and (
7.68
). For the one derivative index, the divergent
terms have only logarithmic divergent surface terms

g^{\nu_{12}}\mathcal{D}_{\nu_{12};\alpha_{1}}^{VV}=-\frac{1}{2}P^{\nu_{1}}(2W_{3\rho\alpha_{1}\nu_{1}}^{\rho}-8\Delta_{2\alpha_{1}\nu_{1}})+P_{\alpha_{1}}\Delta_{2\rho}^{\rho}-q_{\alpha_{1}}\Delta_{2\rho}^{\rho}.

(7.122)

As for the trace of the two-derivative indices tensor, its divergent part is
more complex. It presents a relation involving the trace of quadratically
divergent objects, as seen in the first line of the following equation

\displaystyle g^{\nu_{12}}\mathcal{D}_{\nu_{12};\alpha_{12}}^{VV}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(W_{2\rho\alpha_{12}}^{\rho}-2\Delta_{1\alpha_{12}})+2g_{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}I_{\text{{quad}}}+\frac{1}{6p^{2}}g^{\mu_{12}}\Omega_{\mu_{12}\alpha_{12}}I_{\text{{log}}}

(7.123)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{36}(3P^{\nu_{12}}+q^{\nu_{12}})(3W_{4\rho\alpha_{12}\nu_{12}}^{\rho}-18W_{3\alpha_{12}\nu_{12}})

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4}P^{\nu_{1}}(P_{\alpha_{2}}-q_{\alpha_{2}})(2W_{3\rho\alpha_{1}\nu_{1}}^{\rho}-8\Delta_{2\alpha_{1}\nu_{1}})

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4}P^{\nu_{1}}(P_{\alpha_{1}}-q_{\alpha_{1}})(2W_{3\rho\alpha_{2}\nu_{1}}^{\rho}-8\Delta_{2\alpha_{2}\nu_{1}})

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{8}(P^{2}+q^{2})(2W_{3\rho\alpha_{12}}^{\rho}-8\Delta_{2\alpha_{12}})

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}(P_{\alpha_{1}}-q_{\alpha_{1}})(P_{\alpha_{2}}-q_{\alpha_{2}})\Delta_{2\rho}^{\rho}.

Identities involving
W_{4\rho\alpha_{12}\nu_{12}}^{\rho}
,
W_{3\rho\alpha_{2}\nu_{1}}^{\rho}
and
\Delta_{2\rho}^{\rho}
are a valuable
way to write the results. They arise from taking the trace of
W
’s and
applying combinatorial analysis in their definition as linear expansions of
surface terms, which was performed in Section (
3.1
), Eqs (
3.24
)-(
3.25
). They are

2W_{3\rho\mu_{12}}^{\rho}-8\Delta_{2\mu_{12}}=[2(\square_{3\rho\mu_{12}}^{\rho}-\Delta_{2\mu_{12}})-g_{\mu_{12}}\Delta_{2\rho}^{\rho}]+2g_{\mu_{12}}\Delta_{2\rho}^{\rho},

(7.124)

\displaystyle 3W_{4\rho\mu_{1234}}^{\rho}-18W_{3\mu_{1234}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[3\Sigma_{4\rho\mu_{1234}}^{\rho}-8\square_{3\mu_{1234}}-g_{(\mu_{12}}g_{\mu_{34})}\Delta_{2\rho}^{\rho}]

(7.125)

\displaystyle+g_{(\mu_{12}}[\square_{3\rho\mu_{34})}^{\rho}-\Delta_{2\mu_{34})}-\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu_{34})}\Delta_{2\rho}^{\rho}]+3g_{(\mu_{12}}g_{\mu_{34})}\Delta_{2\rho}^{\rho}.

The use of these relations will become apparent in the course of the
investigation.

To get an explicit expression for terms that make versions of amplitudes
distinct, see (
7.112
) and (
7.113
), we join the results
g^{\nu_{12}}\mathcal{D}_{\nu_{12};\alpha_{1}}^{VV}
and
g^{\nu_{12}}\mathcal{D}_{\nu_{12};\alpha_{12}}^{VV}
with finite part previously calculated, which
allow us to write:

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{\alpha_{1}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(g^{\nu_{12}}T_{\nu_{12};\alpha_{1}}^{VV}-4m^{2}J_{2\alpha_{1}}\right)

(7.126)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}P^{\nu_{1}}[2(\square_{3\rho\alpha_{1}\nu_{1}}^{\rho}-\Delta_{2\alpha_{1}\nu_{1}})-g_{\alpha_{1}\nu_{1}}\Delta_{2\rho}^{\rho}]-q_{\alpha_{1}}(\Delta_{2\rho}^{\rho}+i/2\pi)

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(g^{\nu_{12}}T_{\nu_{12};\alpha_{12}}^{VV}-4m^{2}\bar{J}_{2\alpha_{12}}\right)

(7.127)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{6}\left(\theta_{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}-3q_{\alpha_{1}}q_{\alpha_{2}}\right)(\Delta_{2\rho}^{\rho}+i/2\pi)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{36}(3P^{\nu_{12}}+q^{\nu_{12}})[3\Sigma_{4\rho\alpha_{12}\nu_{12}}^{\rho}-8\square_{3\alpha_{12}\nu_{12}}-g_{(\alpha_{12}}g_{\nu_{12})}\Delta_{2\rho}^{\rho}]

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{72}(3P^{\nu_{12}}+q^{\nu_{12}})g_{(\alpha_{12}}[2\square_{3\nu_{12})\rho}^{\rho}-2\Delta_{2\nu_{12})}-g_{\nu_{12})}\Delta_{2\rho}^{\rho}]

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4}P^{\nu_{1}}\left(P_{\alpha_{2}}-q_{\alpha_{2}}\right)[2(\square_{3\rho\alpha_{1}\nu_{1}}^{\rho}-\Delta_{2\alpha_{1}\nu_{1}})-g_{\alpha_{1}\nu_{1}}\Delta_{2\rho}^{\rho}]

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4}P^{\nu_{1}}\left(P_{\alpha_{1}}-q_{\alpha_{1}}\right)[2(\square_{3\rho\alpha_{2}\nu_{1}}^{\rho}-\Delta_{2\alpha_{2}\nu_{1}})-g_{\alpha_{2}\nu_{1}}\Delta_{2\rho}^{\rho}]

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{8}(P^{2}+q^{2})[2(\square_{3\rho\alpha_{12}}^{\rho}-\Delta_{2\alpha_{12}})-g_{\alpha_{12}}\Delta_{2\rho}^{\rho}]

\displaystyle+(W_{2\rho\alpha_{12}}^{\rho}-2\Delta_{1\alpha_{12}})+2g_{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}I_{\text{{quad}}}-2m^{2}\left(\Delta_{2\alpha_{12}}+g_{\alpha_{12}}I_{\log}\right).

For the last relation, we defined the complete two-point tensor integral

\bar{J}_{2\alpha_{12}}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\Delta_{2\alpha_{12}}+g_{\alpha_{12}}I_{\log}\right)+J_{2}.

We already have all expressions that make up gravitational amplitude.
However, we also need to know how they manifest in RAGFs, a subject we will
address next. In a second step, we will analyze its consequences for
symmetries of keeping these relations preserved and whether it is possible
to determine them independently of amplitudes context.

7.3
Even Amplitudes: RAGFs

Now, we will explore RAGFs for even amplitudes. In Chapters (
4
)
and (
5
), relations served as a bridge to establish how they
operate in odd amplitudes since contractions related to vertex indices
(called
internal indices
) are trivially satisfied. Beyond the
relation

q^{\mu_{1}}t_{\mu_{12}}^{VV}=t_{\mu_{2}}^{V}\left(k_{1}\right)-t_{\mu_{2}}^{V}\left(k_{2}\right)=t_{\left(-\right)\mu_{2}}^{V},

(7.128)

already verified in (
4.53
), we need relations for amplitudes
derivative:

\displaystyle q^{\mu_{1}}t_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{1}}^{VV}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle t_{\mu_{2};\alpha_{1}}^{V}\left(k_{1}\right)-t_{\mu_{2};\alpha_{1}}^{V}\left(k_{2}\right)=t_{\left(-\right)\mu_{2};\alpha_{1}}^{V}

(7.129)

\displaystyle q^{\mu_{1}}t_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{12}}^{VV}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle t_{\mu_{2};\alpha_{12}}^{V}\left(k_{1}\right)-t_{\mu_{2};\alpha_{12}}^{V}\left(k_{2}\right)=t_{\left(-\right)\mu_{2};\alpha_{12}}^{V},

(7.130)

where
t_{\left(-\right)\mu_{2};\alpha_{1}}^{V}
and
t_{\mu_{2};\alpha_{12}}^{V}\left(k_{1}\right)
denotes the difference of vectorial
one-point functions.

In addition to relations for internal indices, the contractions with
derivative indices momentum (called
external indices
) also produce
relations for the gravitational amplitudes. They are obtained using the
following identity inside the Dirac trace

2q^{\alpha_{1}}K_{1\alpha_{1}}=[S^{-1}\left(K_{2}\right)\not{q}+\not{q}S^{-1}\left(K_{1}\right)+2m\not{q}-q^{2}],

(7.131)

in two distinct positions: around the first or second vertex. For example,
we apply it in front of the first vertex and split terms in the sum as

\displaystyle q^{\alpha_{1}}t_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{1}}^{VV}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}q^{2}t_{\mu_{12}}^{VV}+m\mathrm{tr}\left[\not{q}\gamma_{\mu_{1}}S\left(K_{1}\right)\gamma_{\mu_{2}}S\left(K_{2}\right)\right]

(7.132)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}\left[\not{q}S^{-1}\left(K_{1}\right)\gamma_{\mu_{1}}S\left(K_{1}\right)\gamma_{\mu_{2}}S\left(K_{2}\right)\right]

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}\left[S^{-1}\left(K_{2}\right)\not{q}\gamma_{\mu_{1}}S\left(K_{1}\right)\gamma_{\mu_{2}}S\left(K_{2}\right)\right].

Substituting in the second line of the relation above

S^{-1}\left(K_{1}\right)\gamma_{\mu_{1}}S\left(K_{1}\right)=2K_{1\mu_{1}}S\left(K_{1}\right)-\gamma_{\mu_{1}}-2m\gamma_{\mu_{1}}S\left(K_{1}\right),

the mass term was canceled. Applying
\not{q}=[S^{-1}\left(K_{2}\right)-S^{-1}\left(K_{1}\right)]
leads to the difference of the one-point
functions with derivative indices,
t_{\left(-\right)\mu_{2};\mu_{1}}^{V}
. We use the (anti)-commutations among
\gamma_{\mu_{i}}
and
\not{q}
matrices,

\displaystyle 2\gamma_{\mu_{1}}\gamma_{\mu_{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{\gamma_{\mu_{1}},\gamma_{\mu_{2}}\}+[\gamma_{\mu_{1}},\gamma_{\mu_{2}}]

(7.133)

\displaystyle[\gamma_{\mu_{1}},\gamma_{\mu_{2}}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}\gamma_{\ast}

(7.134)

\displaystyle 2g_{\mu_{12}}\not{q}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\not{q}\gamma_{\mu_{2}}\gamma_{\mu_{1}}+\gamma_{\mu_{2}}\gamma_{\mu_{1}}\not{q}.

(7.135)

The systematic procedure gives back the identity given by

\mathrm{tr}\left[\gamma_{\mu_{2}}\not{q}\gamma_{\mu_{1}}S\left(K_{1}\right)\right]-\mathrm{tr}\left[\not{q}\gamma_{\mu_{1}}\gamma_{\mu_{2}}S\left(K_{2}\right)\right]=q_{\mu_{1}}t_{\left(-\right)\mu_{2}}^{V}+q_{\mu_{2}}t_{\left(+\right)\mu_{1}}^{V}-g_{\mu_{12}}q^{\nu}t_{\left(+\right)\nu}^{V}

(7.136)

where the notation
t_{\left(+\right)\mu_{2};\alpha_{1}}^{V},
is
associated with the sum of vectorial one-point functions

t_{\left(+\right)\mu_{2};\alpha_{1}}^{V}=t_{\mu_{2};\alpha_{1}}^{V}\left(k_{1}\right)+t_{\mu_{2};\alpha_{1}}^{V}\left(k_{2}\right),

(7.137)

similarly to
t_{\left(+\right)\mu_{1}}^{V}
. The operations described
above leads the relation for
q^{\alpha}
contraction,

q^{\alpha_{1}}t_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{1}}^{VV}=-\frac{1}{2}q^{2}t_{\mu_{12}}^{VV}+t_{\left(-\right)\mu_{2};\mu_{1}}^{V}+\frac{1}{2}q_{\mu_{1}}t_{\left(-\right)\mu_{2}}^{V}+\frac{1}{2}q_{\mu_{2}}t_{\left(+\right)\mu_{1}}^{V}-\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu_{12}}q^{\alpha_{1}}t_{\left(+\right)\alpha_{1}}^{V}.

(7.138)

Starting with the initial identity (
7.131
) close to the right of vertex
\gamma_{\mu_{2}},
the relation obtained is equal to the previous one
after interchanging
\mu_{1}\leftrightarrow\mu_{2}
on the RHS. The
relation is the same for four indices amplitude, just adding a derived index
on the amplitudes.

Amplitudes with derivative indices also account for trace identities, which
will later be necessary to characterize Weyl anomalies. The result emerges
directly by using
\not{K}_{1}=S^{-1}\left(k_{1}\right)+m
, so relations
for the two amplitudes are given by

\displaystyle g^{\mu_{1}\alpha_{1}}t_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{1}}^{VV}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle t_{\mu_{2}}^{V}\left(k_{2}\right)+mt_{\mu_{2}}^{SV}

(7.139)

\displaystyle g^{\mu_{1}\alpha_{1}}t_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{12}}^{VV}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle t_{\mu_{2};\alpha_{2}}^{V}\left(k_{2}\right)+mt_{\mu_{2};\alpha_{2}}^{SV}.

(7.140)

These relations are symmetric for
\mu_{1}\leftrightarrow\mu_{2}

exchanges.

To finalize the exposure of RAGFs for even amplitudes, we extend the
procedure adopted for the
VV
s to
AA
. For the momentum contraction
q^{\mu_{i}}
(internal contractions):

\displaystyle q^{\mu_{1}}t_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{1}}^{AA}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle t_{\mu_{2};\alpha_{1}}^{V}\left(k_{1}\right)-t_{\mu_{2};\alpha_{1}}^{V}\left(k_{2}\right)-2mt_{\mu_{2};\alpha_{1}}^{PA}=t_{\left(-\right)\mu_{2};\alpha_{1}}^{V}-2mt_{\mu_{2};\alpha_{1}}^{PA}

(7.141)

\displaystyle q^{\mu_{1}}t_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{12}}^{AA}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle t_{\mu_{2};\alpha_{12}}^{V}\left(k_{1}\right)-t_{\mu_{2};\alpha_{12}}^{V}\left(k_{2}\right)-2mt_{\mu_{2};\alpha_{12}}^{PA}=t_{\left(-\right)\mu_{2};\alpha_{12}}^{V}-2mt_{\mu_{2};\alpha_{12}}^{PA}.

(7.142)

For the momentum contraction
q^{\alpha_{i}}
(external contractions):

\displaystyle q^{\alpha_{1}}t_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{1}}^{AA}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}q^{2}t_{\mu_{12}}^{AA}+t_{\left(-\right)\mu_{2};\mu_{1}}^{V}+\frac{1}{2}q_{\mu_{1}}t_{\left(-\right)\mu_{2}}^{V}+\frac{1}{2}q_{\mu_{2}}t_{\left(+\right)\mu_{1}}^{V}-\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu_{12}}q^{\alpha_{1}}t_{\left(+\right)\alpha_{1}}^{V}

(7.143)

\displaystyle+mg_{\mu_{12}}[t^{S}\left(k_{2}\right)-t^{S}\left(k_{1}\right)]+m\varepsilon_{\mu_{12}}[t^{P}\left(k_{2}\right)+t^{P}\left(k_{1}\right)].

The last line has two additional terms compared to (
7.138
). These
terms do not contribute for three-index amplitudes; however, those with four
indices have
[t_{\alpha_{2}}^{S}\left(k_{2}\right)-t_{\alpha_{2}}^{S}\left(k_{1}\right)]\neq 0
when integrated. And for the trace
Contractions:

\displaystyle g^{\mu_{1}\alpha_{1}}t_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{1}}^{AA}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle t_{\mu_{2}}^{V}\left(k_{2}\right)+mt_{\mu_{2}}^{PA}

(7.144)

\displaystyle g^{\mu_{1}\alpha_{1}}t_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{12}}^{AA}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle t_{\mu_{2};\alpha_{2}}^{V}\left(k_{2}\right)+mt_{\mu_{2};\alpha_{2}}^{PA}.

(7.145)

The following subsections pursue links between the finite and divergent
parts that will guide us in studying even and odd parts of Einstein and Weyl
anomalies. Some passages are detailed to explain that all mathematical
operations carried out follow rigorously.

7.3.1
Internal contractions:
q^{\mu}T_{\mu\nu;\sigma}^{VV}
and
q^{\mu}T_{\mu\nu;\sigma\lambda}^{VV}

From detailed results for amplitudes, we can proceed to the verification of
RAGFs, starting with those involving vertex-index contractions (
7.129
) and (
7.130
). We expect them to remain valid to ensure the linearity
of integration operation:

\displaystyle q^{\mu_{1}}T_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{1}}^{VV}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle T_{\mu_{2};\alpha_{1}}^{V}\left(k_{1}\right)-T_{\mu_{2};\alpha_{1}}^{V}\left(k_{2}\right)=T_{\left(-\right)\mu_{2};\alpha_{1}}^{V}

(7.146)

\displaystyle q^{\mu_{1}}T_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{12}}^{VV}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle T_{\mu_{2};\alpha_{12}}^{V}\left(k_{1}\right)-T_{\mu_{2};\alpha_{12}}^{V}\left(k_{2}\right)=T_{\left(-\right)\mu_{2};\alpha_{12}}^{V}.

(7.147)

As they involve differences of vector one-point functions from (
7.37
)
and (
7.38
), we need to calculate these values
\{T_{\mu_{1};\alpha_{1}}^{V}\left(k_{i}\right);\quad T_{\mu_{1};\alpha_{12}}^{V}\left(k_{i}\right)\}.
These amplitudes are expressed in terms of one-point
integrals
\bar{J}_{1\mu_{1}}\left(k_{i}\right)
,
\bar{J}_{1\mu_{12}}\left(k_{i}\right)
and
\bar{J}_{1\mu_{123}}\left(k_{i}\right)

in Eq’s (
C.2
)-(
C.7
) in Appendix (
C
). For the
amplitudes with the label
k_{1}
as the reference momentum, expressions
follow directly from definitions used in the
J
-integrals, namely,

\displaystyle T_{\mu_{1;}\alpha_{1}}^{V}\left(k_{1}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\bar{J}_{1\mu_{1}\alpha_{1}}\left(k_{1}\right)

(7.148)

\displaystyle T_{\mu_{2};\alpha_{12}}^{V}\left(k_{1}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\bar{J}_{1\mu_{1}\alpha_{12}}\left(k_{1}\right).

(7.149)

The amplitudes with the label
k_{2}
momentum require the translation
K_{1}\rightarrow k+k_{2}-q
, just for convenience because
J
’s functions
were defined using this convention, so

\displaystyle T_{\mu_{1};\alpha_{1}}^{V}\left(k_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\bar{J}_{1\mu_{1}\alpha_{1}}\left(k_{2}\right)-2q_{\alpha_{1}}\bar{J}_{1\mu_{1}}\left(k_{2}\right)

(7.150)

\displaystyle T_{\mu_{2};\alpha_{12}}^{V}\left(k_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\bar{J}_{1\mu_{2}\alpha_{12}}\left(k_{2}\right)-2q_{(\alpha_{1}}\bar{J}_{1\alpha_{2})\mu_{2}}\left(k_{2}\right)+2q_{\alpha_{12}}\bar{J}_{1\mu_{2}}\left(k_{2}\right).

(7.151)

Differences of one-point vectorial functions with one and two derivative
indices are

\displaystyle T_{\left(-\right)\mu_{2};\alpha_{1}}^{V}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-q^{\nu_{2}}P^{\nu_{1}}W_{3\alpha_{1}\mu_{2}\nu_{12}}+(P_{\mu_{2}}q^{\nu_{1}}+P^{\nu_{1}}q_{\mu_{2}})\Delta_{3\alpha_{1}\nu_{1}}

(7.152)

\displaystyle+\left(P\cdot q\right)\Delta_{3\mu_{2}\alpha_{1}}+\left(P_{\alpha_{1}}-q_{\alpha_{1}}\right)q^{\nu_{1}}\Delta_{2\mu_{2}\nu_{1}},

\displaystyle T_{\left(-\right)\mu_{2};\alpha_{12}}^{V}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q^{\nu_{1}}W_{2\mu_{2}\alpha_{12}\nu_{1}}-q_{\mu_{2}}\Delta_{1\alpha_{12}}+q_{(\alpha_{1}}g_{\alpha_{2})\mu_{2}}I_{\text{{quad}}}

(7.153)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{12}[P^{(\nu_{12}}q^{\nu_{3})}+q^{\nu_{123}}]W_{4\mu_{2}\alpha_{12}\nu_{123}}

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4}[2q^{\nu_{1}}P^{\nu_{2}}P_{\mu_{2}}+(P^{\nu_{12}}+q^{\nu_{12}})q_{\mu_{2}}]W_{3\alpha_{12}\nu_{12}}

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4}\left[2\left(P\cdot q\right)P^{\nu_{1}}+q^{\nu_{1}}(P^{2}+q^{2})\right]W_{3\mu_{2}\alpha_{12}\nu_{1}}

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}q^{\nu_{2}}P^{\nu_{1}}(P-q)_{(\alpha_{1}}W_{3\alpha_{2})\mu_{2}\nu_{12}}]

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{4}\left[2(P\cdot q)P_{\mu_{2}}+(P^{2}+q^{2})q_{\mu_{2}}\right]\Delta_{2\alpha_{12}}+\frac{1}{2}(P\cdot q)(P-q)_{(\alpha_{1}}\Delta_{2\text{ }\alpha_{2})\mu_{2}}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}(P_{\mu}q^{\nu_{1}}+q_{\mu}P^{\nu_{1}})(P-q)_{(\alpha_{1}}\Delta_{2\text{ }\alpha_{2})\nu_{1}}+\frac{1}{2}(P-q)_{\alpha_{1}}(P-q)_{\alpha_{2}}q^{\nu_{1}}\Delta_{2\mu_{2}\nu_{1}}.

We will begin verifying relations obtained for even amplitudes. From the
relation for 2nd-order VV amplitude in (
4.53
), the verification for
derivative amplitudes follows the same procedure. We have for the 3rd and
4th-order
VV
amplitude

q^{\mu_{1}}T_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{1}}^{VV}=2(2q^{\mu_{1}}J_{2\mu_{12}\alpha_{1}}+q^{2}J_{2\mu_{2}\alpha_{1}})+q_{\mu_{2}}(2q^{\mu_{1}}J_{2\mu_{1}\alpha_{1}}+q^{2}J_{2\alpha_{1}})+q^{\mu_{1}}\mathcal{D}_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{1}}^{VV}

(7.154)

q^{\mu_{1}}T_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{12}}^{VV}=2(2q^{\mu_{1}}J_{2\mu_{12}\alpha_{12}}+q^{2}J_{2\mu_{2}\alpha_{12}})+q_{\mu_{2}}(2q^{\mu_{1}}J_{2\mu_{1}\alpha_{12}}+q^{2}J_{2\alpha_{12}})+q^{\mu_{1}}\mathcal{D}_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{12}}^{VV}.

(7.155)

from Section (
3.3
), which are of the same type used in
establishing constraints over odd amplitudes (by example
2q^{\mu_{1}}J_{2\mu_{12}\alpha_{1}}=-q^{2}J_{2\mu_{2}\alpha_{1}})
, we have
that finite part vanishes and divergent factors
q^{\mu_{1}}\mathcal{D}_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{1}}^{VV}
and
q^{\mu_{1}}\mathcal{D}_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{12}}^{VV}
satisfy identically

\displaystyle q^{\mu_{1}}\mathcal{D}_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{1}}^{VV}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle T_{\left(-\right)\mu_{2};\alpha_{1}}^{V}

(7.156)

\displaystyle q^{\mu_{1}}\mathcal{D}_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{12}}^{VV}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle T_{\left(-\right)\mu_{2};\alpha_{12}}^{V}.

(7.157)

Due to the definitions of tensors
W_{4\mu_{123456}}
and
W_{3\mu_{1234}}
, see Section (
3.1
) there are hundreds of surface terms in the
last relation. Although it seems complicated to verify such equality, its
satisfaction follows from the observation that each of the lines that we
arrange for tensor
\mathcal{D}_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{12}}^{VV}
in (
7.68
) will correspond to one of the lines expressed by difference
T_{\left(-\right)\mu_{2};\alpha_{12}}^{V}
in (
7.153
), when
contracting with momentum. We facilitate these identifications by
classifying surface terms, following criteria regarding the divergence
degree, tensor rank, and contraction type. For example, it is necessary to
note that index
\mu_{1}
becomes a contracted index,
3q^{\mu_{1}}P^{\nu_{12}}W_{4\mu_{12}\alpha_{12}\nu_{12}}=P^{(\nu_{12}}q^{\nu_{3})}W_{4\mu_{2}\alpha_{12}\nu_{123}}.
As the tensor
W_{4\mu_{123456}}
is fully
symmetric, terms are identical, and so on for all others. Expanding
W

combinations in primary surface terms is not necessary. In this way,
relations for amplitudes at the trace level incorporate integration
linearity established in (
7.147
) and are satisfied without restriction
on the divergent parts of expressions.

7.3.2
External Contractions:
q^{\sigma}T_{\mu\nu;\sigma}^{VV}
and
q^{\sigma}T_{\mu\nu;\sigma\lambda}^{VV}

We have one more momentum contraction to check regarding amplitudes
T_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{1}}^{VV}
and
T_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{12}}^{VV}
: they are
q^{\alpha_{1}}T_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{1}}^{VV}
and
q^{\alpha_{1}}T_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{12}}^{VV}
from (
7.138
). It can be made by contracting
the amplitude and identifying the function of the RHS. Nevertheless, we
proceed through an alternative route, using manipulations to reorganize
integrands of amplitudes. Effectively these indices exchange from Dirac
matrices
\mu_{i}
with indices from derivative factors
\alpha_{i}
. In
this way, if previously verified relations (
7.147
) are satisfied, they
will also be satisfied since they come from Dirac traces. We will detail
calculations for relations involving amplitude with a derivative index. At
the end of the operations, we expect to obtain (
7.138
) integrated. We
will extend this result to amplitude with two derivatives, drawing attention
to their differences. These two amplitudes with exchange indexes will be the
basis for calculating the relations for the other even and odd amplitudes.

From definition for the amplitude
t_{\mu_{12}}^{VV}
and
t_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{1}}^{VV},
see Eqs (
4.24
) and (
7.56
), we obtain

\displaystyle 2t_{\mu_{12}}^{\left(+\right)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle t_{\mu_{12}}^{VV}-g_{\mu_{12}}t^{PP}

(7.158)

\displaystyle 2t_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{1}}^{\left(+\right)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle t_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{1}}^{VV}-g_{\mu_{12}}t_{\alpha_{1}}^{PP}.

(7.159)

It can be noted that the role of indexes position in the second tensor is

\displaystyle 2t_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{1}}^{\left(+\right)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2K_{1\alpha_{1}}(K_{1\mu_{1}}K_{2\mu_{2}}+K_{2\mu_{1}}K_{1\mu_{2}})\frac{1}{D_{12}}

(7.160)

\displaystyle 2t_{\alpha_{1}\mu_{2};\mu_{1}}^{\left(+\right)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2K_{1\mu_{1}}\left(K_{1\alpha_{1}}K_{2\mu_{2}}+K_{2\alpha_{1}}K_{1\mu_{2}}\right)\frac{1}{D_{12}},

(7.161)

where the outside term in parentheses comes from derivative contribution.
Manipulating the expression for
t_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{1}}^{\left(+\right)}
using
K_{2}=K_{1}+q
relate both tensors, changing the role of indices
\mu_{1}\leftrightarrow\alpha_{1}
. We use the notation to represent the
antisymmetry of indices
[

\ ]
:

t_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{1}}^{\left(+\right)}=t_{\alpha_{1}\mu_{2};\mu_{1}}^{\left(+\right)}-q_{[\alpha_{1}}K_{1\mu_{1}]}K_{1\mu_{2}}\frac{1}{D_{12}}.

(7.162)

The tensors
t_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{1}}^{\left(+\right)}
and
t_{\alpha_{1}\mu_{2};\mu_{1}}^{\left(+\right)}
differ by an additional tensor
from translation of
K_{1}
momentum. Expressing
t^{\left(+\right)}

parts in terms of
VV
and
PP
amplitudes leads to

t_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{1}}^{VV}=t_{\alpha_{1}\mu_{2};\mu_{1}}^{VV}+g_{\mu_{12}}t_{\alpha_{1}}^{PP}-g_{\alpha_{1}\mu_{2}}t_{\mu_{1}}^{PP}-2q_{[\alpha_{1}}K_{1\mu_{1}]}K_{1\mu_{2}}\frac{1}{D_{12}}.

(7.163)

Furthermore, the last term with this equation also has a form in terms of
VV
-amplitude

2q_{[\alpha_{1}}K_{1\mu_{1}]}K_{1\mu_{2}}\frac{1}{D_{12}}=\frac{1}{2}(q_{\alpha_{1}}t_{\mu_{12}}^{VV}-q_{\mu_{1}}t_{\alpha_{1}\mu_{2}}^{VV})-\frac{1}{2}q_{[\alpha_{1}}g_{\mu_{1}]\mu_{2}}t^{PP}-q_{\mu_{2}}q_{[\alpha_{1}}K_{1\mu_{1}]}\frac{1}{D_{12}}.

(7.164)

Starting from the definition of amplitude
T_{\alpha_{1}}^{PP}
(
7.62
)
and using
K_{1}=K_{2}-q
, the sum of one-point vector functions appears
straightforwardly

t_{\alpha_{1}}^{PP}=q^{2}\frac{K_{1\alpha_{1}}}{D_{12}}+q_{\alpha_{1}}\frac{1}{D_{2}}-\frac{1}{2}[t_{\alpha_{1}}^{V}\left(k_{1}\right)+t_{\alpha_{1}}^{V}\left(k_{2}\right)].

(7.165)

These observations, we obtain an identity representing the exchanging of
indices that facilitate the study of this relation coming from contractions
involving derivatives indices

t_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{1}}^{VV}=-\frac{1}{2}q_{\alpha_{1}}t_{\mu_{12}}^{VV}+t_{\alpha_{1}\mu_{2};\mu_{1}}^{VV}+\frac{1}{2}q_{\mu_{1}}t_{\alpha_{1}\mu_{2}}^{VV}-\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu_{12}}t_{\left(+\right)\alpha_{1}}^{V}+\frac{1}{2}g_{\alpha_{1}\mu_{2}}t_{\left(+\right)\mu_{1}}^{V}+r_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{1}}.

(7.166)

Here,
r_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{1}}
is a residual term anissymetric in
\mu_{1}
and
\alpha_{1}

r_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{1}}=\frac{1}{2}q_{[\alpha_{1}}g_{\mu_{1}]\mu_{2}}\left(q^{2}\frac{1}{D_{12}}+\frac{1}{D_{2}}-\frac{1}{D_{1}}\right)-\theta_{\mu_{2}[\alpha_{1}}K_{1\mu_{1}]}\frac{1}{D_{12}},

(7.167)

whose integration yields

\displaystyle R_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{1}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}q_{[\alpha_{1}}g_{\mu_{1}]\mu_{2}}q^{2}J_{2}-q^{2}g_{\mu_{2}[\alpha_{1}}J_{2\mu_{1}]}+q_{\mu_{2}}q_{[\alpha_{1}}J_{2\mu_{1}]}

(7.168)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}q_{[\alpha_{1}}g_{\mu_{1}]\mu_{2}}q^{2}[\bar{J}_{1}(k_{2})-\bar{J}_{1}(k_{1})].

After substitutions of
J_{2\mu},

J_{2}
and
\bar{J}_{1}\left(k_{i}\right),
this term is null,
R_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{1}}=0.
It is
essential to mention that we carry out passive operations. Rearranging
amplitude terms does not represent any operations performed on the original
amplitude. The full expression is

T_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{1}}^{VV}=-\frac{1}{2}q_{\alpha_{1}}T_{\mu_{12}}^{VV}+T_{\alpha_{1}\mu_{2};\mu_{1}}^{VV}+\frac{1}{2}q_{\mu_{1}}T_{\alpha_{1}\mu_{2}}^{VV}-\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu_{12}}T_{\left(+\right)\alpha_{1}}^{V}+\frac{1}{2}g_{\alpha_{1}\mu_{2}}T_{\left(+\right)\mu_{1}}^{V}.

(7.169)

Let us analyze
q^{\alpha_{1}}
contractions. We have already verified that
RAGF is satisfied with matrix indices. Thus, we have automatic satisfaction
of contractions with
\alpha_{1}
index

q^{\alpha_{1}}T_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{1}}^{VV}=-\frac{1}{2}q^{2}T_{\mu_{12}}^{VV}+T_{\left(-\right)\mu_{2};\mu_{1}}^{V}+\frac{1}{2}q_{\mu_{1}}T_{\left(-\right)\mu_{2}}^{V}+\frac{1}{2}q_{\mu_{2}}T_{\left(+\right)\mu_{1}}^{V}-\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu_{12}}q^{\alpha_{1}}T_{\left(+\right)\alpha_{1}}^{V}.

(7.170)

Adding one more factor
K_{1\alpha_{2}}
in (
7.166
), the structure
is the same as the previous one,

\displaystyle t_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{12}}^{VV}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}q_{\alpha_{1}}t_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{2}}^{VV}+t_{\alpha_{1}\mu_{2};\mu_{1}\alpha_{2}}^{VV}+\frac{1}{2}q_{\mu_{1}}t_{\alpha_{1}\mu_{2};\alpha_{2}}^{VV}

(7.171)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu_{12}}t_{\left(+\right)\alpha_{1};\alpha_{2}}^{V}+\frac{1}{2}g_{\alpha_{1}\mu_{2}}t_{\left(+\right)\mu_{1};\alpha_{2}}^{V}+r_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{12}}.

However, we need to analyze the effect on the term
r_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{12}}=K_{1\alpha_{2}}r_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{1}}
from (
7.167
),

r_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{12}}=\frac{1}{2}q_{[\alpha_{1}}g_{\mu_{1}]\mu_{2}}K_{1\alpha_{2}}\left(q^{2}\frac{1}{D_{12}}+\frac{1}{D_{2}}-\frac{1}{D_{1}}\right)-\theta_{\mu_{2}[\alpha_{1}}K_{1\mu_{1}]}\frac{K_{1\alpha_{2}}}{D_{12}}.

(7.172)

After being integrated, the residual terms can be organized as

\displaystyle R_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{12}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}q_{[\alpha_{1}}g_{\mu_{1}]\mu_{2}}\left[q^{2}J_{2\alpha_{2}}-J_{1\alpha_{2}}\left(k_{1}\right)+J_{1\alpha_{2}}\left(k_{2}\right)-q_{\alpha_{2}}J_{1}\left(k_{2}\right)\right]-\theta_{\mu_{2}[\alpha_{1}}\bar{J}_{2\mu_{1}]\alpha_{2}}

(7.173)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}q_{[\alpha_{1}}g_{\mu_{1}]\mu_{2}}(q^{2}J_{2\alpha_{2}}-q^{\nu_{1}}\Delta_{2\alpha_{2}\nu_{1}}-q_{\alpha_{2}}I_{\log})-\theta_{\mu_{2}[\alpha_{1}}\bar{J}_{2\mu_{1}]\alpha_{2}}.

This term does not cancel itself when integrated; nonetheless, its
contractions do not contribute to the relations

2q^{\alpha_{1}}R_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{12}}=\theta_{\mu_{12}}\left(2q^{\alpha_{1}}\bar{J}_{2\alpha_{12}}+q^{2}J_{2\alpha_{2}}-q^{\nu_{1}}\Delta_{2\alpha_{2}\nu_{1}}-q_{\alpha_{2}}I_{\log}\right)=0.

(7.174)

Furthermore, we find the same outcome for the trace

g^{\mu_{1}\alpha_{1}}R_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{12}}=\theta_{\mu_{2}}^{\alpha_{1}}\bar{J}_{2\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}-\theta_{\mu_{2}}^{\alpha_{1}}\bar{J}_{2\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}=0.

(7.175)

Thus, it will not contribute to any of the contractions that remain to be
verified.

\displaystyle q^{\mu_{1}}R_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{12}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0\quad q^{\mu_{2}}R_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{12}}=0\text{\quad}q^{\alpha_{1}}R_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{12}}=0

(7.176)

\displaystyle g^{\mu_{2}\alpha_{2}}R_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{12}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0\quad g^{\mu_{1}\alpha_{1}}R_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{12}}=0\text{\quad}g^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}R_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{12}}=0.

(7.177)

The complete expression is given by

\displaystyle T_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{12}}^{VV}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}q_{\alpha_{1}}T_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{2}}^{VV}+T_{\alpha_{1}\mu_{2};\mu_{1}\alpha_{2}}^{VV}+\frac{1}{2}q_{\mu_{1}}T_{\alpha_{1}\mu_{2};\alpha_{2}}^{VV}

(7.178)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu_{12}}T_{\left(+\right)\alpha_{1};\alpha_{2}}^{V}+\frac{1}{2}g_{\alpha_{1}\mu_{2}}T_{\left(+\right)\mu_{1};\alpha_{2}}^{V}+R_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{12}}.

Divergent parts are not restricted to any values. Contracting the equation
and using (
7.174
), we have the relation (
7.138
) satisfied for this
amplitude.

7.3.3

g^{\mu\sigma}T_{\mu\nu;\sigma}^{VV}
and

Relations from metric contraction (

) can be

\displaystyle g^{\mu_{1}\alpha_{1}}t_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2};\alpha_{1}}^{VV}-t_{\mu_{2}}^{V}\left(k_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle mt_{\mu_{2}}^{SV}

(7.179)

\displaystyle g^{\mu_{1}\alpha_{1}}t_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2};\alpha_{12}}^{VV}-t_{\mu_{2};\alpha_{2}}^{V}\left(k_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle mt_{\mu_{2};\alpha_{2}}^{SV}.

(7.180)

They can be reformulated based on what was discussed for contractions
involving derivative indices—in this case, exchanging
\mu_{1}\leftrightarrow\alpha_{1}
to get the relation. That is also valid for
the permutation
\mu_{2}\leftrightarrow\alpha_{1}
since two matrix
indices
\mu
’s are symmetric, and for the second expression, the same is
valid for indexes
\alpha
’s. We have to the integrated (

)

2g^{\mu_{1}\alpha_{1}}T_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{1}}^{VV}=g^{\nu_{12}}[2T_{\nu_{12};\mu_{2}}^{VV}+q_{\mu_{2}}T_{\nu_{12}}^{VV}]+[T_{\left(+\right)\mu_{2}}^{V}-q^{\mu_{1}}T_{\mu_{12}}^{VV}].

(7.181)

The argument follows the previous case: if the relation (
4.53
) is
valid, then

2[g^{\mu_{1}\alpha_{1}}T_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{1}}^{VV}-T_{\mu_{2}}^{V}\left(k_{2}\right)]=g^{\nu_{12}}[2T_{\nu_{12};\mu_{2}}^{VV}+q_{\mu_{2}}T_{\nu_{12}}^{VV}].

(7.182)

Moreover, the contraction of (
7.178
) is condionated by
satisfaction of (
7.146
), therefore

2[g^{\mu_{1}\alpha_{1}}T_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{12}}^{VV}-T_{\mu_{2};\alpha_{2}}^{V}\left(k_{2}\right)]=g^{\nu_{12}}[2T_{\nu_{12};\mu_{2}\alpha_{2}}^{VV}+q_{\mu_{2}}T_{\nu_{12};\alpha_{2}}^{VV}].

(7.183)

If we compare these expressions with the integrated ones (
7.179
)
and (
7.180
), showing their equivalence is doable. In this way, the
RHS can be written as

\displaystyle g^{\nu_{12}}[2T_{\nu_{12};\mu_{2}}^{VV}+q_{\mu_{2}}T_{\nu_{12}}^{VV}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2mT_{\mu_{2}}^{SV}

(7.184)

\displaystyle g^{\nu_{12}}[2T_{\nu_{12};\mu_{2}\alpha_{2}}^{VV}+q_{\mu_{2}}T_{\nu_{12};\alpha_{2}}^{VV}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2mT_{\mu_{2};\alpha_{2}}^{SV}.

(7.185)

We need traces of the
VV
to verify if these relations are satisfied since
divergent terms will be contained in traces of
\mathcal{D}^{VV}
-parts.
Nonetheless, there is a path using exclusively
\Upsilon_{\mu_{2}}
and
\Upsilon_{\alpha_{2}\mu_{2}}
that emerged in constraint of equivalence
among odd amplitudes. Explicit forms of
SV
-amplitudes regard
J_{2}
’s
integrals; therefore, let us write the results

T_{\mu_{2}}^{SV}=2m(2J_{2\mu_{2}}+q_{\mu_{2}}J_{2})=0.

(7.186)

Even if it is identically zero due to relations among finite integrals of
equal masses, we will use its terms separately in the sequel. The other

T_{\mu_{2};\alpha_{2}}^{SV}=2m(2\bar{J}_{2\mu_{2}\alpha_{2}}+q_{\mu_{2}}J_{2\alpha_{2}})=2m(\Delta_{2\mu_{2}\alpha_{2}}+g_{\mu_{2}\alpha_{2}}I_{\log})+2m(2J_{2\mu_{2}\alpha_{2}}+q_{\mu_{2}}J_{2\alpha_{2}}).

(7.187)

Beginning with Eq. (
7.184
), we write

2(g^{\nu_{12}}T_{\nu_{12};\mu_{2}}^{VV}-4m^{2}J_{2\mu_{2}})+q_{\mu_{2}}(g^{\nu_{12}}T_{\nu_{12}}^{VV}-4m^{2}J_{2})=0.

(7.188)

It is a matter of recognizing
\Upsilon
-factors; consult their explicit
expressions in Eqs. (
7.107
) and (
7.126
) to write
2\Upsilon_{\mu_{2}}+q_{\mu_{2}}\Upsilon=0.
That is a condition for compliance with RAGF
derived through the metric contraction. Extending this construction to Eq. (
7.185
),

2(g^{\nu_{12}}T_{\nu_{12};\mu_{2}\alpha_{2}}^{VV}-4m^{2}\bar{J}_{2\mu_{2}\alpha_{2}})+q_{\mu_{2}}(g^{\nu_{12}}T_{\nu_{12};\alpha_{2}}^{VV}-4m^{2}J_{2\alpha_{2}})=0.

(7.189)

That means
2\Upsilon_{\mu_{2}\alpha_{2}}+q_{\mu_{2}}\Upsilon_{\alpha_{2}}=0
due to the definition already given, see (
7.127
) for the
explicit expression of
\Upsilon_{\mu_{2}\alpha_{2}}
. Hence, metric
RAGFs are not automatically satisfied also for even amplitudes. Owing
derivations until this point, we can lay down the equations:

\displaystyle g^{\nu_{12}}[2T_{\nu_{12};\mu_{2}}^{VV}+q_{\mu_{2}}T_{\nu_{12}}^{VV}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2mT_{\mu_{2}}^{SV}+2\Upsilon_{\mu_{2}}+q_{\mu_{2}}\Upsilon

(7.190)

\displaystyle g^{\nu_{12}}[2T_{\nu_{12};\mu_{2}\alpha_{2}}^{VV}+q_{\mu_{2}}T_{\nu_{12};\alpha_{2}}^{VV}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2mT_{\mu_{2};\alpha_{2}}^{SV}+2\Upsilon_{\mu_{2}\alpha_{2}}+q_{\mu_{2}}\Upsilon_{\alpha_{2}}.

(7.191)

Alternatively, we can express them in the way it was derived

\displaystyle 2g^{\mu_{1}\alpha_{1}}T_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{1}}^{VV}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2T_{\mu_{2}}^{V}\left(k_{2}\right)+2mT_{\mu_{2}}^{SV}+\left(2\Upsilon_{\mu_{2}}+q_{\mu_{2}}\Upsilon\right).

(7.192)

\displaystyle 2g^{\mu_{1}\alpha_{1}}T_{\mu_{12};\alpha_{12}}^{VV}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2T_{\mu_{2};\alpha_{2}}^{V}\left(k_{2}\right)+2mT_{\mu_{2};\alpha_{2}}^{SV}+(2\Upsilon_{\mu_{2}\alpha_{2}}+q_{\mu_{2}}\Upsilon_{\alpha_{2}}).

(7.193)

The vanishing of individual violating terms
\Upsilon
is enough to satisfy
these relations. This constraint preserves all RAGFs in all amplitudes;
however, in (
7.192
), combinations of violating terms can be made
zero without canceling each term. That is the only place this happens; they
always arise individually in other relations. Two-index combination requires
that terms cancel independently. Calling for the full results (
7.107
)
and (
7.126
), it is clear that violating terms in three-indices relation

2\Upsilon_{\mu_{2}}+q_{\mu_{2}}\Upsilon=-P^{\nu_{1}}[2(\square_{3\rho\mu_{2}\nu_{1}}^{\rho}-\Delta_{2\mu_{2}\nu_{1}})-g_{\mu_{2}\nu_{1}}\Delta_{2\rho}^{\rho}].

(7.194)

It can be restricted to zero without each component being zero
independently. As a last comment, violating factors come from suitably
complex functions of momenta, physical
q
, or ambiguous
P
. Nonetheless,
they are local polynomials in these variables, which can be asserted from
their expressions. The remaining appears in (
7.127
).

Discussing if violating terms are null and the consequences of this property
is a crucial point of this investigation and what perspective we can
establish from conditions for RAGF satisfaction in odd amplitudes context.

These relations differ from those associated with vector amplitudes by an

-amplitudes,

\displaystyle T_{\mu_{2}}^{PA}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2mq_{\mu_{2}}J_{2}

(7.195)

\displaystyle T_{\mu_{2};\alpha_{1}}^{PA}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2mq_{\mu_{2}}J_{2\alpha_{1}}

(7.196)

\displaystyle T_{\mu_{2};\alpha_{12}}^{PA}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2mq_{\mu_{2}}\bar{J}_{2\alpha_{12}}.

(7.197)

As they exactly match the additional terms through connection with the

\displaystyle q^{\mu_{1}}T_{\mu_{12}}^{AA}
