q^{\mu_{i}}T_{\mu_{12}}^{\left(2D\right)AV}=\varepsilon_{\mu_{a}\nu}q^{\nu}V_{i}\rightarrow\left.\left(V_{1}+V_{2}\right)\right|_{0}=0.

(9.7)

The kinematical theorem is incompatible with the low-energy limit of finite
functions

\Omega_{1}^{PV}\left(0\right)+\Omega_{1}^{AS}\left(0\right)=-i/\pi.

(9.8)

Hence, the
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
functions are inevitably of the form
V_{i}=\Omega_{i}+\mathcal{A}_{i}
, with
\mathcal{A}_{1}+\mathcal{A}_{2}=i/\pi
.

Moreover, considering the surface terms for the expression to the general
tensor, an analogous condition is derived through the constraint of
algebraic property encoded by the RAGFs, viz.,

2\Delta_{2\alpha}^{\left(2\right)\alpha}=-i/\pi=\Omega^{PV}\left(0\right)+\Omega^{AS}\left(0\right).

(9.9)

This constraint also makes the amplitudes unique concerning the Dirac traces
used. To four dimensions, this turns the amplitudes quantities subject to
routing choices. In contrast, to two dimensions, satisfying RAGFs leads to
Dirac-trace independent expressions that only depend on the physical
momentum.

The feature of Dirac traces appearing in all the treated amplitudes and the
analogous ones for
2n
dimensions arises for the trace of
2n+2
Dirac
matrices and an odd number of the chiral matrices. An assortment of
expressions is available when one writes the tensor representing that trace,
differing by the number of monomials and their signs, plus what subset of
its Lorentz indexes appear. Those expressions are equivalent under the
condition that surface terms have a value corresponding to the low-energy
limit of finite-functions combination (
9.5
) in
4D
or (
9.8
)
in
2D
.

Adopting the
zero value
follows a set of expressions to each
amplitude that may keep at most two RAGFs in
4D
or one in
2D
. These
expressions can be obtained either applying the definition of
\gamma_{\ast}
, in some position along the trace or using the identity below in the
adjacent position of matrix
\gamma_{\mu_{i}},

\displaystyle\left(2n\right)

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle\gamma_{\ast}\gamma_{\mu_{i}}=\frac{i^{n+1}}{\left(2n-1\right)!}\varepsilon_{\mu_{i}\nu_{2}\cdots\nu_{2n}}\gamma^{\nu_{2}\cdots\nu_{2n}}

(9.10)

\displaystyle\left(2D\right)

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle\gamma_{\ast}\gamma_{\mu_{i}}=-\varepsilon_{\mu_{i}\nu_{1}}\gamma^{\nu_{1}}\text{\quad and\quad}\left(4D\right):\gamma_{\ast}\gamma_{\mu_{i}}=\varepsilon_{\mu_{i}\nu_{123}}\gamma^{\nu_{123}}/6.

(9.11)

Thus, the tensors calculated for the amplitudes will correspond to the
versions defined as the main ingredients of the investigation. They violate
the RAGF for the vertex corresponding to
\gamma_{\mu_{i}}
, and the WI
gets violated in the same vertex. Two aspects must be noticed: (i) To have
all the indices present, or to use the definition of the chiral matrix, is
not exceptional since identities (above ones) yield fewer terms and deliver
the same integrated expressions. (ii) The specialty of these identities is
that they furnish the maximum number of RAGF automatically satisfied; hence
the last RAGF can not be met because we would be violating a low-energy
implication (
9.6
) in
4D
and (
9.9
) in
2D
.

To sum up, adopting null surface terms makes the amplitudes depend on the
traces used. The Schouten identity inside the integral that connects the
integrands ceases to make it in the final integrated results. Ultimately,
this breaks the linearity of integration and violates the RAGFs. Different
formulae for the traces do not deliver identical tensors. The main elements
involved in the versions were that they correspond to the same integrand;
for instance, in
2D

(t_{\mu_{12}}^{AV})_{1}=(t_{\mu_{12}}^{AV})_{2}
.
However, after being integrated separately, we find their subtraction as

(T_{\mu_{12}}^{\left(2D\right)AV})_{1}-(T_{\mu_{12}}^{\left(2D\right)AV})_{2}=2\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}\mu_{1}}(2\Delta_{2\rho}^{\left(2\right)\rho}+i/\pi).

(9.12)

Following the same argument, we build up the combination

(t_{\mu_{12}}^{\left(2D\right)AV})_{1}=(t_{\mu_{12}}^{\left(2D\right)AV})_{2}=\frac{1}{r_{1}+r_{2}}[r_{1}(t_{\mu_{12}}^{\left(2D\right)AV})_{1}+r_{2}(t_{\mu_{12}}^{\left(2D\right)AV})_{2}],

(9.13)

with
r_{1}+r_{2}\not=0
and otherwise arbitrary numbers; thus, after
integration and adoption of
\Delta_{2\mu\nu}^{\left(2\right)}=0
we
may write any other expression, in particular, the version
(T_{\mu_{12}}^{\left(2D\right)AV})_{3}
discussed in Chapter (
4
)
which is the linear combination above with
r_{1}=r_{2}=1
. In that chapter,
it was used one of the identities satisfied by the antisymmetric products of
Dirac matrices, viz.,
\gamma_{\ast}\gamma_{\left[\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\right]}=-\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}
. In general, not only 2D, all
expressions obtainable utilizing those identities are a linear combination
of the basic versions. Once more because they satisfy the most RAGFs as
possible. With this algorithm in mind, we can build, if desired, the content
one needs, by example,

(T_{\mu_{123}}^{AAA})_{\{1,1,1\}}=\frac{1}{3}[(T_{\mu_{123}}^{AAA})_{1}+(T_{\mu_{123}}^{AAA})_{2}+(T_{\mu_{123}}^{AAA})_{3}]

(9.14)

has one-third of the anomaly in
(T_{\mu_{123}}^{AVV})_{1}
, for each
vertex.

About uniqueness, some definition is necessary. A criterion that makes the
amplitudes unique in a universal sense is impossible since they are
divergent quantities. After renormalization, they become dependent on an
arbitrary mass scale. We employed the definition: One expression coming from
the Feynman rules is unique if, for all intrinsic arbitrariness in
intermediary algebraic manipulations, as Dirac traces and arbitrary
routings, the final result is the same. This concept definition is well
defined in the odd and non-derivative amplitudes studied in
2D
because we
got an expression depending on the external momentum and independent from
Dirac traces. To the amplitudes investigated in
4D
, the ’unique’ answer is
a function of the routings taken as independent variables. Meaning one does
not have a unique amplitude of the external momenta.

As for rules, it makes the surface terms zero as done in even amplitudes and
by an intelligent choice of Dirac trace to obtain the symmetry content.
Notwithstanding, if RAGFs are respected, turning amplitudes unique functions
of their routings, this enables one to recover the symmetry content by
choice of the remaining ambiguities for the momenta labels
k_{i}
, except
2D; this can be done in all even dimensions to the tensors like
T_{\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{n+1}}^{\left(2n\right)A^{2r+1}V^{n-2r}};r\leq\left[n/2\right].

Gravitation
: The situation changes drastically when the power
counting is higher than linear. For quadratic divergent gravitational
amplitude, by preserving the RAGFs, we have the finiteness of the relevant
surface terms as the constraint; see (
8.132
,
8.133
and
8.134
). Thus, it follows a unique form independent of manipulations in
the Dirac algebra but ambiguous in what refers to the routing of the
diagram. The results, in this scenario, for the Weyl anomaly is

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle=g^{\mu_{12}}T_{\mu_{12}\alpha_{12}}^{G}=-\frac{1}{96\pi}q^{\nu}\left(\varepsilon_{\alpha_{1}\nu}q_{\alpha_{2}}+\varepsilon_{\alpha_{2}\nu}q_{\alpha_{1}}\right)-\frac{1}{24\pi}\theta_{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}-\frac{1}{48\pi}q_{\alpha_{2}}q_{\alpha_{1}}

(9.15)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{192\pi}\left(3\chi^{2}-5\right)\left[q^{\nu}\left(\varepsilon_{\alpha_{1}\nu}q_{\alpha_{2}}+\varepsilon_{\alpha_{2}\nu}q_{\alpha_{1}}\right)+\left(\theta_{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}-q_{\alpha_{2}}q_{\alpha_{1}}\right)\right].

Furthermore, for the Einstein anomaly, we have the expression above

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\mu_{2}\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle=q^{\mu_{1}}T_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}^{G}=-\frac{1}{96\pi}\varepsilon_{\alpha_{1}\nu}q^{\nu}\theta_{\alpha_{2}\mu_{2}}+

(9.16)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{192\pi}(q_{\alpha_{1}}\theta_{\alpha_{2}\mu_{2}}+q_{\alpha_{2}}\theta_{\alpha_{1}\mu_{2}}-2q_{\mu_{2}}\theta_{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}-2q_{\alpha_{1}}q_{\alpha_{2}}q_{\mu_{2}})

\displaystyle+\frac{\left(3\chi^{2}-5\right)}{384\pi}\{2q_{\alpha_{1}}q_{\alpha_{2}}q_{\mu_{2}}+\varepsilon_{\alpha_{1}}^{\penalty\ \penalty\ \nu}\left(q_{\mu_{2}}\theta_{\nu\alpha_{2}}+q_{\alpha_{2}}\theta_{\nu\mu_{2}}-q_{\nu}\theta_{\alpha_{2}\mu_{2}}-q_{\alpha_{2}}q_{\mu_{2}}q_{\nu}\right)\}.

The first terms of each expression correspond to the ones in Bertlmann and
Kohlprath
[
61
,
62
]
. The result shows that apart
from the question of the origin of the additional terms as trivial anomalies
and which actions generate them. They are the product of preserving
algebraic operations determined without resorting to a specific evaluation
of divergent integrals, even though the representation of surface terms
appears in this fashion.

Distinctly from the chiral anomalies, and in a certain sense similar to the
vacuum polarization tensor of 4D quantum electrodynamics, the symmetry
content (or violation thereof) can not be recovered by choice of the
arbitrary internal momenta
k_{1}+k_{2}=\chi q
, at least for the even part
(we restrict ourselves to covariant choices). The odd part allows this for
the parameter
\chi^{2}=5/3
, namely

\displaystyle\left.\mathcal{W}_{\alpha_{12}}\right|_{\chi^{2}=5/3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{96\pi}[q^{\nu}\left(\varepsilon_{\alpha_{1}\nu}q_{\alpha_{2}}+\varepsilon_{\alpha_{2}\nu}q_{\alpha_{1}}\right)+4\theta_{\alpha_{12}}+2q_{\alpha_{12}})]

(9.17)

\displaystyle\left.\mathcal{E}_{\mu_{2}\alpha_{12}}\right|_{\chi^{2}=5/3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{192\pi}[2\varepsilon_{\alpha_{1}\nu}q^{\nu}\theta_{\mu_{2}\alpha_{2}}-q_{(\alpha_{1}}\theta_{\alpha_{2})\mu_{2}}+2q_{\mu_{2}}\theta_{\alpha_{12}}+2q_{\mu_{2}}q_{\alpha_{12}})].

(9.18)

There is no choice of
\chi
which eliminates the vector part of the
Einstein anomaly for finite surface terms, nor the vector part of the Weyl
one. The only possibility to eliminate the even part of Einstein’s anomaly
is to spoil the linearity of integration and turn off the surface terms.
This attitude brings a complex set of possibilities in the axial sector to
be discussed in the sequel. The axial part of the Weyl anomaly can be
eliminated by adopting
\chi^{2}=1
. However, we did not explore the
aspect, which is interesting since adding the Bardeen-Zumino polynomial in
the stress tensor to change the consistent anomaly in the covariant one, the
odd part disappears; see the book of Bertlmann
[
11
]
, pg. 541
or the paper cited previously.

Turning to the scenario where surface terms vanish and thus freeing the even
part of the Einstein anomaly, the odd part, constituted of multiple terms,
allows the exploration of the traces in each component. It is a choice
available once the algebraic properties of the amplitudes are broken. In
this thesis, we restricted to simplifications where the expressions to each
of the four permutations (
\mu_{1}\leftrightarrow\mu_{2}
)
\leftrightarrow
(
\alpha_{1}\leftrightarrow\alpha_{2}
) in the expansion
that follows have the same version for each term.

\mathcal{T}_{\mu_{1}\alpha_{1}\mu_{2}\alpha_{2}}^{AV}=4T_{\mu_{1}\alpha_{1};\mu_{2}\alpha_{2}}^{AV}+2q_{\mu_{2}}T_{\mu_{1}\alpha_{1};\alpha_{2}}^{AV}+2q_{\alpha_{2}}T_{\mu_{1}\alpha_{1};\mu_{2}}^{AV}+q_{\alpha_{2}}q_{\mu_{2}}T_{\mu_{1}\alpha_{1}}^{AV}.

(9.19)

We allowed other trace choices only for the partner
\mathcal{T}^{VA}
,
uniformly in its terms. We do not impose a priori symmetries in the indices,
exploiting just the freedom of the versions. Those symmetries are preserved
once the RAGFs are so, e.g.,
T_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}^{G}=T_{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}^{G}
. In making the
selections stated, we arrive at a phenomenon already observed in the chiral
counterparts: the anomalies can migrate from contraction to contraction. The
compact formula for the Einstein anomalies becomes

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\alpha_{r}}^{ij}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{96\pi}\left(\delta_{i,2}+\delta_{j,2}\right)\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\nu}q^{\nu}\theta_{\mu_{2}\alpha_{r}}

(9.20)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\mu_{r}\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}^{ij}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{96\pi}\left(2-\delta_{i,2}-\delta_{j,2}\right)\varepsilon_{\alpha_{1}\nu}q^{\nu}\theta_{\alpha_{2}\mu_{r}}.

(9.21)

They come from the contraction with
q^{\alpha_{1};\alpha_{2}}
and
q^{\mu_{1};\mu_{2}}
, being that upper-indices in
\mathcal{E}^{ij}

assumes
1
or
2
values. The Weyl ones are

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}^{ij}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{24\pi}\theta_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}-\frac{1}{96\pi}(2-\delta_{i,2}-\delta_{j,2})q^{\nu}(\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\nu}q_{\mu_{2}}+\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}\nu}q_{\mu_{1}})

(9.22)

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}^{ij}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{24\pi}\theta_{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}-\frac{1}{96\pi}\left(\delta_{i,2}+\delta_{j,2}\right)q^{\nu}(\varepsilon_{\alpha_{1}\nu}q_{\alpha_{2}}+\varepsilon_{\alpha_{2}\nu}q_{\alpha_{1}}).

(9.23)

Notice that when the Einstein anomaly (odd part) drops out in one group of
indices, the Weyl anomaly does so in the complementary set, occurring when
i=j
. In the combinations
ij=12
or
ij=21
, none are zero and equal to
half of the results for the non-vanishing parts of
ij=11
or
ij=22
. The
mixed versions have coefficients equal to the ones in Bertlmann
[
61
]
, which is one particular result of our analysis.

Ultimately, the expression (
9.19
) above admits independent choices
for each term. As a consequence, the factor
\Upsilon,\Upsilon_{\alpha},\Upsilon_{\alpha\mu}
(
7.107
-
7.126
,
7.127
) do not combine
into the
U_{\alpha\mu}
-factor, and the other projector aside
\theta_{\mu\alpha}
(
\omega_{\mu\alpha}=q_{\mu}q_{\alpha}
) would arise
with a proliferation of coefficients. This scenario is allowed for once the
surface terms are interpreted as quantities that vanish. This element leads
to expressions that exhibit Lorentz anomaly. We deviated from this anomaly
once the same version was used when summing the basic permutations. Another
interesting point is to study a low-energy theorem in the gravitational
setting, as done for the chiral anomalies. Research along these lines is
underway.

As a final comment, the possibility of final and compact expressions that
preserve all the features of the computation is mainly due to the use of a
definition of the surface terms of rank four
\square_{3\mu\nu\alpha\rho}
, and six
\Sigma_{4\mu\nu\alpha\rho\sigma\lambda}
, which are
explicitly total symmetric in the Lorentz indices. In addition, their
compilation into terms that may break the algebraic RAGFs, the objects
\{\Upsilon,\Upsilon_{\alpha},\Upsilon_{\alpha\mu}\}
. In particular,
we call attention to the scalar one,
\Upsilon=2\Delta_{2\rho}^{\rho}+i/\pi
, which in the last instance, determines the satisfaction or not of
all RAGFs for the energy-momentum two-point function. It is precisely the
same one that appears in the 2D chiral anomaly. The extension of these
protocols to four dimensions facilitates the investigations underway
associated with trace anomalies closely related to the recent publications
in Bonora
[
20
]
and
[
77
]
. The RAGFs will become
exceedingly complicated; as an example, we have

\displaystyle 2p_{31}^{\alpha}T_{\mu_{123};\alpha}^{AVV}+p_{31}^{2}T_{\mu_{123}}^{AVV}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2m[T_{\mu_{31}\mu_{2}}^{\tilde{T}V}\left(1,2\right)+T_{\mu_{12}\mu_{3}}^{\tilde{T}V}\left(2,3\right)]

\displaystyle+i[\varepsilon_{\mu_{12}}^{\hskip 10.0pt\nu_{12}}p_{31\nu_{2}}T_{\nu_{1}\mu_{3}}^{VV}\left(2,3\right)]-i[\varepsilon_{\mu_{13}}^{\hskip 10.0pt\nu_{12}}p_{31\nu_{2}}T_{\nu_{1}\mu_{2}}^{VV}\left(1,2\right)]

\displaystyle+2[T_{\mu_{32};\mu_{1}}^{AV}\left(1,2\right)-T_{\mu_{23};\mu_{1}}^{AV}\left(2,3\right)]

\displaystyle-p_{31}^{\nu_{1}}[g_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}T_{\nu_{1}\mu_{3}}^{AV}\left(2,3\right)+g_{\mu_{1}\mu_{3}}T_{\nu_{1}\mu_{2}}^{AV}\left(1,2\right)]

\displaystyle+[p_{31\mu_{1}}T_{\mu_{32}}^{AV}\left(1,2\right)+p_{31\mu_{3}}T_{\mu_{12}}^{AV}\left(1,2\right)-p_{31\mu_{1}}T_{\mu_{23}}^{AV}\left(2,3\right)+p_{31\mu_{2}}T_{\mu_{13}}^{AV}\left(2,3\right)],

where even arises a pseudo-tensor vertex
\tilde{T}=\gamma_{\ast}\gamma_{\left[\mu\nu\right]}
. Nonetheless, by the systematization developed in
this thesis such task becomes feasible as well.

Appendix A
Dirac Matrices and Traces

Lets us introduce the Clifford algebra representation in terms of matrices
\left\{\gamma_{\mu_{1}},\gamma_{\mu_{2}}\right\}=2g_{\mu_{12}}\mathbf{1,}
the dimension of irreducible representations are
\mathrm{\dim}\left(\gamma\right)=2^{\left[d/2\right]}\times 2^{\left[d/2\right]}
, and
the basic traces are

\displaystyle\mathrm{tr}\left(\gamma^{\mu}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

(A.1)

\displaystyle\mathrm{tr}\left\{\gamma_{\alpha},\gamma_{\beta}\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2g_{\alpha\beta}\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathbf{1}_{2^{n}\times 2^{n}}\right).

(A.2)

For the two dimensional representation, we have:

\displaystyle\gamma_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sigma_{2};\gamma_{1}=i\sigma_{1};\gamma_{3}=\sigma_{3}

(A.3)

\displaystyle\gamma_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sigma_{1};\gamma_{1}=i\sigma_{2};\gamma_{3}=-\sigma_{3}.

For even dimensions,
d=2n
, there is a matrix given by

\gamma_{\ast}:=i^{n-1}\gamma_{0}\gamma_{1}\cdots\gamma_{2n-1}=\frac{i^{n-1}}{\left(2n\right)!}\varepsilon_{\nu_{1}\cdots\nu_{2n}}\gamma^{\nu_{1}\cdots\nu_{2n}}

(A.4)

that obeys
\left\{\gamma_{\ast},\gamma_{\mu}\right\}=0,
with
\varepsilon_{012\cdots d-1}=-1
. For four matrices, we have the trace

\mathrm{tr}\left(\gamma^{\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{4}}\right)=\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathbf{1}_{2^{n}\times 2^{n}}\right)\left(g^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}g^{\mu_{3}\mu_{4}}-g^{\mu_{1}\mu_{3}}g^{\mu_{2}\mu_{4}}+g^{\mu_{1}\mu_{4}}g^{\mu_{2}\mu_{3}}\right),

(A.5)

the general formula is

\mathrm{tr}\left(\gamma_{\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{2n}}\right)=\sum_{i=2}^{2n}\left(-1\right)^{i}g_{\mu_{1}\mu_{i}}\mathrm{tr}\left(\gamma_{\mu_{1}\cdots\hat{\mu}_{i}\cdots\mu_{2n}}\right).

(A.6)

The first non-zero trace with the chiral matrix in any even dimension is
given by

\mathrm{tr}\left(\gamma_{\ast}\gamma_{\mu_{1}}\gamma_{\mu_{2}}\cdots\gamma_{\mu_{2n}}\right)=2^{n}i^{n-1}\left(-1\right)^{n}\varepsilon_{\mu_{12\cdots}\mu_{2n}},

(A.7)

for
d=2n
to the string of
2n+2
gamma matrices plus
\gamma_{\ast}

using its definition follows the formula

\mathrm{tr}(\gamma_{\ast}\gamma_{a_{1}a_{2}\cdots a_{2n+1}a_{2n+2}})=2^{n}i^{3n-1}\sum_{k=1}^{2n+1}\sum_{j=k+1}^{2n+2}\left(-1\right)^{j+k+1}g_{a_{k}a_{j}}\varepsilon_{a_{1}\cdots\hat{a}_{k}\cdots\hat{a}_{j}\cdots(2n+2)},

(A.8)

where we have used the abbreviation
\gamma_{a_{1}a_{2}\cdots a_{2n+1}a_{2n+2}}=\prod_{j=1}^{2b+2}\gamma_{a_{j}}.
The Latin index ought
to be substituted to whatever configuration of Lorentz indices is
scrutinized.

A.1
Traces of a String of Six Gamma and the Chiral Matrix

One uses the following identities to insert the Levi-Civita tensor in traces
with the chiral matrix

\gamma_{\ast}\gamma_{\left[\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{r}\right]}=\frac{i^{n-1+r\left(r+1\right)}}{\left(2n-r\right)!}\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{r}\nu_{r+1}\cdots\nu_{2n}}\gamma^{\left[\nu_{r+1}\cdots\nu_{2n}\right]},

where the notation
\gamma_{\left[\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{r}\right]}

indicates antisymmetrized products of gammas and the investigated dimension
is
2n=4
. This appendix uses this resource to achieve different trace
expressions and explore their relations.

Trace using the definition
\gamma_{\ast}=i\varepsilon_{\nu_{1}\nu_{2}\nu_{3}\nu_{4}}\gamma^{\nu_{1}\nu_{2}\nu_{3}\nu_{4}}/4!
-
The three leading positions to substitute the definition are
around vertices
\Gamma_{1}
,
\Gamma_{2}
, and
\Gamma_{3}
. Even if
that brings six options, the same integrated expressions arise regardless of
replacing at the left or right. Thus, we cast the possibilities in the
sequence

\displaystyle t_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathrm{tr}(\gamma_{\ast}\gamma_{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}\mu_{2}\nu_{2}\mu_{3}\nu_{3}})=i\varepsilon^{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\alpha_{3}\alpha_{4}}\mathrm{tr}(\gamma_{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\alpha_{3}\alpha_{4}}\gamma_{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}\mu_{2}\nu_{2}\mu_{3}\nu_{3}})/4!

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle+g_{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}}\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}\nu_{2}\mu_{3}\nu_{3}}-g_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}\varepsilon_{\nu_{1}\nu_{2}\mu_{3}\nu_{3}}+g_{\mu_{1}\nu_{2}}\varepsilon_{\nu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}\nu_{3}}-g_{\mu_{1}\mu_{3}}\varepsilon_{\nu_{1}\mu_{2}\nu_{2}\nu_{3}}+g_{\mu_{1}\nu_{3}}\varepsilon_{\nu_{1}\mu_{2}\nu_{2}\mu_{3}}

\displaystyle+g_{\nu_{1}\mu_{2}}\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\nu_{2}\mu_{3}\nu_{3}}-g_{\nu_{1}\nu_{2}}\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}\nu_{3}}+g_{\nu_{1}\mu_{3}}\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\nu_{2}\nu_{3}}-g_{\nu_{1}\nu_{3}}\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\nu_{2}\mu_{3}}+g_{\mu_{2}\nu_{2}}\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}\mu_{3}\nu_{3}}

\displaystyle-g_{\mu_{2}\mu_{3}}\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}\nu_{2}\nu_{3}}+g_{\mu_{2}\nu_{3}}\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}\nu_{2}\mu_{3}}+g_{\nu_{2}\mu_{3}}\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}\mu_{2}\nu_{3}}-g_{\nu_{2}\nu_{3}}\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}}+g_{\mu_{3}\nu_{3}}\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}\mu_{2}\nu_{2}},

\displaystyle t_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathrm{tr}(\gamma_{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}}\gamma_{\ast}\gamma_{\mu_{2}\nu_{2}\mu_{3}\nu_{3}})=i\varepsilon^{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\alpha_{3}\alpha_{4}}\mathrm{tr}(\gamma_{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}}\gamma_{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\alpha_{3}\alpha_{4}}\gamma_{\mu_{2}\nu_{2}\mu_{3}\nu_{3}})/4!

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle+g_{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}}\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}\nu_{2}\mu_{3}\nu_{3}}+g_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}\varepsilon_{\nu_{1}\nu_{2}\mu_{3}\nu_{3}}-g_{\mu_{1}\nu_{2}}\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}\nu_{2}\mu_{3}\nu_{3}}+g_{\mu_{1}\mu_{3}}\varepsilon_{\nu_{1}\mu_{2}\nu_{2}\nu_{3}}-g_{\mu_{1}\nu_{3}}\varepsilon_{\nu_{1}\mu_{2}\nu_{2}\mu_{3}}

\displaystyle-g_{\nu_{1}\mu_{2}}\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\nu_{2}\mu_{3}\nu_{3}}+g_{\nu_{1}\nu_{2}}\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}\nu_{3}}-g_{\nu_{1}\mu_{3}}\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\nu_{2}\nu_{3}}+g_{\nu_{1}\nu_{3}}\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\nu_{2}\mu_{3}}+g_{\mu_{2}\nu_{2}}\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}\mu_{3}\nu_{3}}

\displaystyle-g_{\mu_{2}\mu_{3}}\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}\nu_{2}\nu_{3}}+g_{\mu_{2}\nu_{3}}\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}\nu_{2}\mu_{3}}+g_{\nu_{2}\mu_{3}}\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}\mu_{2}\nu_{3}}-g_{\nu_{2}\nu_{3}}\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}}+g_{\mu_{3}\nu_{3}}\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}\mu_{2}\nu_{2}},

\displaystyle t_{3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathrm{tr}(\gamma_{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}\mu_{2}\nu_{2}}\gamma_{\ast}\gamma_{\mu_{3}\nu_{3}})=i\varepsilon^{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\alpha_{3}\alpha_{4}}\mathrm{tr}(\gamma_{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}\mu_{2}\nu_{2}}\gamma_{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\alpha_{3}\alpha_{4}}\gamma_{\mu_{3}\nu_{3}})/4!

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle+g_{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}}\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}\nu_{2}\mu_{3}\nu_{3}}-g_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}\varepsilon_{\nu_{1}\nu_{2}\mu_{3}\nu_{3}}+g_{\mu_{1}\nu_{2}}\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}\nu_{2}\mu_{3}\nu_{3}}+g_{\mu_{1}\mu_{3}}\varepsilon_{\nu_{1}\mu_{2}\nu_{2}\nu_{3}}-g_{\mu_{1}\nu_{3}}\varepsilon_{\nu_{1}\mu_{2}\nu_{2}\mu_{3}}

\displaystyle+g_{\nu_{1}\mu_{2}}\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\nu_{2}\mu_{3}\nu_{3}}-g_{\nu_{1}\nu_{2}}\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}\nu_{3}}-g_{\nu_{1}\mu_{3}}\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\nu_{2}\nu_{3}}+g_{\nu_{1}\nu_{3}}\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\nu_{2}\mu_{3}}+g_{\mu_{2}\nu_{2}}\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}\mu_{3}\nu_{3}}

\displaystyle+g_{\mu_{2}\mu_{3}}\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}\nu_{2}\nu_{3}}-g_{\mu_{2}\nu_{3}}\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}\nu_{2}\mu_{3}}-g_{\nu_{2}\mu_{3}}\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}\mu_{2}\nu_{3}}+g_{\nu_{2}\nu_{3}}\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}}+g_{\mu_{3}\nu_{3}}\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}\mu_{2}\nu_{2}},

where we omit the global factor
4i
. Since each expression contains fifteen
monomials featuring all index configurations, different signs are the unique
distinguishing factor among them. That is also the reason why references
often name them symmetric or democratic
[
28
,
73
,
50
]
.

These (main) versions play fundamental roles in this investigation as they
are enough to obtain any other result. If we use any other identity
constructed with the equations involving the antisymmetric products the
trace expressions relate directly to them or their combinations
t_{ij}=\left(t_{i}+t_{j}\right)/2
only using sums and no other operation.
Consequently, any expression attributed to the investigated triangles is a
linear combination of those detailed in the main body of this work. All of
them produce the mentioned relations, so we cast some at the end of this
appendix.

\displaystyle t_{12}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-g_{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}}\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}\mu_{3}\nu_{2}\nu_{3}}-g_{\mu_{2}\nu_{2}}\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\mu_{3}\nu_{1}\nu_{3}}+g_{\mu_{2}\nu_{3}}\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\mu_{3}\nu_{1}\nu_{2}}

\displaystyle-g_{\nu_{2}\mu_{3}}\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\nu_{1}\nu_{3}}-g_{\mu_{3}\nu_{3}}\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\nu_{1}\nu_{2}}-g_{\mu_{2}\mu_{3}}\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}\nu_{2}\nu_{3}}-g_{\nu_{2}\nu_{3}}\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}\nu_{1}},

\displaystyle t_{13}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-g_{\mu_{3}\nu_{3}}\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\nu_{1}\nu_{2}}-g_{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}}\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}\mu_{3}\nu_{2}\nu_{3}}+g_{\mu_{1}\nu_{2}}\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}\mu_{3}\nu_{1}\nu_{3}}

\displaystyle-g_{\nu_{1}\mu_{2}}\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\mu_{3}\nu_{2}\nu_{3}}-g_{\mu_{2}\nu_{2}}\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\mu_{3}\nu_{1}\nu_{3}}-g_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}\varepsilon_{\mu_{3}\nu_{1}\nu_{2}\nu_{3}}-g_{\nu_{1}\nu_{2}}\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}\nu_{3}},

\displaystyle t_{23}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-g_{\mu_{2}\nu_{2}}\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\mu_{3}\nu_{1}\nu_{3}}-g_{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}}\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}\mu_{3}\nu_{2}\nu_{3}}+g_{\mu_{1}\nu_{3}}\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}\mu_{3}\nu_{1}\nu_{2}}

\displaystyle-g_{\nu_{1}\mu_{3}}\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\nu_{2}\nu_{3}}-g_{\mu_{3}\nu_{3}}\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\nu_{1}\nu_{2}}-g_{\mu_{1}\mu_{3}}\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}\nu_{1}\nu_{2}\nu_{3}}-g_{\nu_{1}\nu_{3}}\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}\nu_{2}},

Trace using

\gamma_{\ast}\gamma_{a}=-i\varepsilon_{a\nu_{1}\nu_{2}\nu_{3}}\gamma^{\nu_{1}\nu_{2}\nu_{3}}/3!
- After using
this identity for the chiral matrix and the first gamma, we write this trace
through ten monomials.

\eta_{1}\left(a\right)=\mathrm{tr}\left(\gamma_{\ast}\gamma_{abcdef}\right)=-i\varepsilon_{a}^{\hskip 8.19447pt\nu_{1}\nu_{2}\nu_{3}}\mathrm{tr}\left(\gamma_{\nu_{1}\nu_{2}\nu_{3}}\gamma_{bcdef}\right)/6

\displaystyle\eta_{1}\left(a\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g_{\text{$b$}c}\varepsilon_{\text{$a$}def}-g_{\text{$b$}d}\varepsilon_{\text{$a$}cef}+g_{\text{$b$}e}\varepsilon_{\text{$a$}cdf}-g_{\text{$b$}f}\varepsilon_{\text{$a$}cde}+g_{\text{$c$}d}\varepsilon_{\text{$a$}bef}

\displaystyle-g_{\text{$c$}e}\varepsilon_{\text{$a$}bdf}+g_{\text{$c$}f}\varepsilon_{\text{$a$}bde}+g_{de}\varepsilon_{abcf}+g_{ef}\varepsilon_{abcd}-g_{df}\varepsilon_{abce}

Trace using

\gamma_{\ast}\gamma_{\left[ab\right]}=-i\varepsilon_{ab\nu_{1}\nu_{2}}\gamma^{\nu_{1}\nu_{2}}/2!
- This
case requires expressing the ordinary product in terms of the
antisymmetrized one. We find seven monomials after taking the traces.

\gamma_{\ast}\gamma_{ab}=-\frac{1}{2}i\varepsilon_{ab\nu_{1}\nu_{2}}\gamma^{\nu_{1}\nu_{1}}+g_{ab}\gamma_{\ast}

\displaystyle\eta_{2}\left(ab\right)=\mathrm{tr}\left(\gamma_{\ast}\gamma_{abcdef}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g_{ab}\varepsilon_{cdef}+g_{cd}\varepsilon_{abef}-g_{ce}\varepsilon_{abdf}+g_{cf}\varepsilon_{abde}

\displaystyle+g_{d\text{$e$}}\varepsilon_{abcf}-g_{df}\varepsilon_{abce}+g_{ef}\varepsilon_{abcd}

Trace using

\gamma_{\ast}\gamma_{\left[abc\right]}=i\varepsilon_{abc\nu}\gamma^{\nu}
- Following a similar procedure we
find six monomials.

\gamma_{\ast}\gamma_{abc}=i\varepsilon_{abc\nu}\gamma^{\nu}+\gamma_{\ast}\left(g_{bc}\gamma_{a}-g_{ac}\gamma_{b}+g_{ab}\gamma_{c}\right)

\eta_{3}\left(abc\right)=\mathrm{tr}\left(\gamma_{\ast}\gamma_{abcdef}\right)=g_{ab}\varepsilon_{cdef}-g_{ac}\varepsilon_{bdef}+g_{bc}\varepsilon_{adef}+g_{de}\varepsilon_{abcf}-g_{df}\varepsilon_{abce}+g_{ef}\varepsilon_{abcd}

Trace using

\gamma_{\ast}\gamma_{\left[abcd\right]}=i\varepsilon_{abcd}
- This case also generates seven monomials.

\displaystyle\gamma_{\ast}\gamma_{abcd}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle i\varepsilon_{abcd}\mathbf{1}+g_{ab}\gamma_{\ast}\gamma_{\left[cd\right]}-g_{ac}\gamma_{\ast}\gamma_{\left[bd\right]}+g_{ad}\gamma_{\ast}\gamma_{\left[bc\right]}

\displaystyle+g_{bc}\gamma_{\ast}\gamma_{\left[ad\right]}-g_{bd}\gamma_{\ast}\gamma_{\left[ac\right]}+g_{cd}\gamma_{\ast}\gamma_{\left[ab\right]}+\left(g_{ab}g_{cd}-g_{ac}g_{bd}+g_{ad}g_{bc}\right)\gamma_{\ast}

\displaystyle\eta_{4}\left(abcd\right)=\mathrm{tr}\left(\gamma_{\ast}\gamma_{abcdef}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g_{ab}\varepsilon_{cdef}-g_{ac}\varepsilon_{bdef}+g_{ad}\varepsilon_{bcef}+g_{bc}\varepsilon_{adef}

\displaystyle-g_{bd}\varepsilon_{acef}+g_{cd}\varepsilon_{abef}+g_{ef}\varepsilon_{abcd}

Interconnection among formulas:
When computing the difference
between two integrated versions of the same amplitude, we acknowledge two
situations. First, it cancels out identically as their integrands are
precisely equal, for example:

\left[t_{12}-\eta_{2}\left(\mu_{1}\nu_{1}\right)\right]=0,\qquad\left[t_{23}-\eta_{4}\left(\mu_{3}\nu_{3}\mu_{1}\nu_{1}\right)\right]=0.

Second, it vanishes in the integration because the explicit computation
corresponds to finite null integrals embodied into the
t^{\left(-+\right)}
tensor (
6.14
) and the
ASS
amplitude (
6.22
). Some examples
are:

\displaystyle\left[t_{1}-\eta_{1}\left(\mu_{1}\right)\right]\frac{K_{123}^{\nu_{123}}}{D_{123}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}\mu_{3}\nu_{1}\nu_{2}}t_{\mu_{1}}^{\left(-+\right)\nu_{12}}-g_{\mu_{1}\mu_{3}}t_{\mu_{2}}^{ASS}+g_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}t_{\mu_{3}}^{ASS},

\displaystyle\left[t_{12}+\eta_{2}\left(\nu_{1}\mu_{2}\right)\right]\frac{K_{123}^{\nu_{123}}}{D_{123}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}\mu_{3}\nu_{1}\nu_{2}}t_{\mu_{1}}^{\left(-+\right)\nu_{12}}+\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\mu_{3}\nu_{1}\nu_{2}}t_{\mu_{2}}^{\left(-+\right)\nu_{12}}-g_{\mu_{2}\mu_{3}}t_{\mu_{1}}^{ASS}+g_{\mu_{1}\mu_{3}}t_{\mu_{2}}^{ASS},

\displaystyle\left[t_{13}+\eta_{4}\left(\nu_{1}\mu_{2}\nu_{2}\mu_{3}\right)\right]\frac{K_{123}^{\nu_{123}}}{D_{123}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\varepsilon_{\mu_{2}\mu_{3}\nu_{1}\nu_{2}}t_{\mu_{1}}^{\left(-+\right)\nu_{12}}-\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\nu_{1}\nu_{2}}t_{\mu_{3}}^{\left(-+\right)\nu_{12}}+g_{\mu_{2}\mu_{3}}t_{\mu_{1}}^{ASS}-g_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}t_{\mu_{3}}^{ASS}.

\displaystyle\left[t_{12}-\eta_{3}\left(\mu_{1}\nu_{1}\mu_{2}\right)\right]\frac{K_{123}^{\nu_{123}}}{D_{123}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-g_{\mu_{2}\mu_{3}}t_{\mu_{1}}^{ASS}+\varepsilon_{\mu_{13}\nu_{12}}t_{\mu_{2}}^{\left(-+\right)\nu_{12}}+g_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}t_{\mu_{3}}^{ASS},

\displaystyle\left[t_{23}-\eta_{3}\left(\mu_{2}\nu_{2}\mu_{3}\right)\right]\frac{K_{123}^{\nu_{123}}}{D_{123}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-g_{\mu_{3}\mu_{1}}t_{\mu_{2}}^{ASS}-\varepsilon_{\mu_{12}\nu_{12}}t_{\mu_{3}}^{\left(-+\right)\nu_{12}}+g_{\mu_{2}\mu_{3}}t_{\mu_{1}}^{ASS}

\displaystyle\left[t_{31}-\eta_{3}\left(\mu_{3}\nu_{3}\mu_{1}\right)\right]\frac{K_{123}^{\nu_{123}}}{D_{123}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-g_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}t_{\mu_{3}}^{ASS}-\varepsilon_{\mu_{23}\nu_{12}}t_{\mu_{1}}^{\left(-+\right)\nu_{12}}+g_{\mu_{3}\mu_{1}}t_{\mu_{2}}^{ASS}

We showed the forms that identically correspond here, not that all
differences are finite and vanishing. For example, the form obtained from
t_{12}
is not identical without conditions to any
t_{i}
.

Appendix B
Feynman Integrals

B.1
Feynman’s parametrization

Any integral that is explicitly evaluated in this work is well defined. To
operate, we combine the denominators that appear using Feynman
parametrization. The functions that occur after they have been split through
the formula (
3.4
) share the form

\frac{1}{D_{\lambda}^{N}D_{1}...D_{n}}.

(B.1)

They can be combined as

\frac{1}{D_{\lambda}^{N}D_{1}...D_{n}}=\left(N\right)_{n}\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}x_{1}\cdots\int_{0}^{1-x_{1}-...-x_{n-1}}\mathrm{d}x_{n}\frac{\left(1-x_{1}-\cdots x_{N}\right)^{N-1}}{\left[\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(D_{i}-D_{\lambda}\right)x_{i}+D_{\lambda}\right]^{n+N}},

(B.2)

where
\left(N\right)_{n}
is the Pochhammer symbol
\left(N\right)_{n}=\Gamma\left(N+n\right)/\Gamma\left(N\right).
It is a direct task
by induction to show that

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(D_{i}-D_{\lambda}\right)x_{i}+D_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle k^{2}-\lambda^{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(2k\cdot k_{i}+k_{i}^{2}\right)x_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\lambda^{2}-m_{i}^{2}\right)x_{i}

(B.3)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(k+\sum_{i=1}^{n}k_{i}x_{i}\right)^{2}+Q\left(\left\{k_{i},m_{i}^{2}\right\};\lambda^{2}\right),

where we define the
Q
polynomial

Q\left(\left\{k_{i},m_{i}^{2}\right\};\lambda^{2}\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}k_{i}^{2}x_{i}\left(1-x_{i}\right)-2\sum_{j>i}^{n}\left(k_{i}\cdot k_{j}\right)x_{i}x_{j}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\lambda^{2}-m_{i}^{2}\right)x_{i}-\lambda^{2}.

(B.4)

After integrating into the momentum
k
, we have a function of
Q
whose
integral over adequate parameter delivers the integrals used in work. As of
the finite functions, they appear as

\frac{1}{D_{1}...D_{n}}=\Gamma\left(n\right)\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}x_{1}\cdots\int_{0}^{1-x_{1}-...-x_{n-2}}\mathrm{d}x_{n-1}\frac{1}{\left[\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\left(D_{i}-D_{1}\right)x_{i}+D_{1}\right]^{n}}.

(B.5)

An example to illustrate this is the finite integral

I_{2}=\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{2}}\frac{1}{D_{12}}

(B.6)

the explicit
D_{i}
are

\displaystyle D_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(k+k_{1}\right)^{2}-m_{1}^{2}

(B.7)

\displaystyle D_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(k+k_{2}\right)^{2}-m_{2}^{2}

(B.8)

thus we identify

\displaystyle\left(D_{2}-D_{1}\right)x+D_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle k^{2}+2k\cdot\left[\left(k_{2}-k_{1}\right)x+k_{1}\right]+\left(k_{2}^{2}-k_{1}^{2}\right)x+k_{1}^{2}+\left(m_{1}^{2}-m_{2}^{2}\right)x-m_{1}^{2}

(B.9)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[k+\left(k_{2}-k_{1}\right)x+k_{1}\right]^{2}+\left(k_{2}-k_{1}\right)^{2}x\left(1-x\right)+\left(m_{1}^{2}-m_{2}^{2}\right)x-m_{1}^{2}

(B.10)

and with
q=k_{2}-k_{1}
the
Q
polynomial

Q=q^{2}x\left(1-x\right)+\left(m_{1}^{2}-m_{2}^{2}\right)x-m_{1}^{2}.

(B.11)

When integrating the translation in the k variable

k\rightarrow k-\left[\left(k_{2}-k_{1}\right)x+k_{1}\right]

(B.12)

allows us to write the integral as

J_{2}=\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}z\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{2}}\frac{1}{\left(k^{2}+Q\right)^{2}}.

(B.13)

The next step is integration in the momentum, where the next section derives
the necessary formulae.

B.2
The
J_{2\mu\nu}^{\left(2\right)}
Integral

For non-negative power counting integrals, we must split them using the
identity (
3.4
). Let us illustrate the type of operations needed to
integrate such integrals using as an example the fundamental tensor integral
with arbitrary masses in two dimensions

\bar{J}_{2}^{\left(2\right)\mu\nu}=\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{2}}\frac{K_{1}^{\mu}K_{1}^{\nu}}{D_{12}}.

(B.14)

Its integrand is decomposed in the form

\frac{K_{1}^{\mu}K_{1}^{\nu}}{D_{12}}=\frac{K_{1}^{\mu}K_{1}^{\nu}}{D_{\lambda}^{2}}-\frac{K_{1}^{\mu}K_{1}^{\nu}A_{2}}{D_{\lambda}^{2}D_{2}}-\frac{K_{1}^{\mu}K_{1}^{\nu}A_{1}}{D_{\lambda}D_{12}}.

(B.15)

Then, the following integrals are required to perform

\displaystyle\bar{J}_{2}^{\left(2\right)\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{2}}\frac{K_{1}^{\mu}K_{1}^{\nu}}{D_{12}}=\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{2}}\left\{\frac{K_{1}^{\mu}K_{1}^{\nu}}{D_{\lambda}^{2}}-\frac{K_{1}^{\mu}K_{1}^{\nu}A_{2}}{D_{\lambda}^{2}D_{2}}-\frac{K_{1}^{\mu}K_{1}^{\nu}A_{1}}{D_{\lambda}D_{12}}\right\}

(B.16)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{2}}\frac{K_{1}^{\mu}K_{1}^{\nu}}{D_{\lambda}^{2}}-F_{b}^{\mu\nu}-F_{a}^{\mu\nu}.

(B.17)

The final answer will be expressed as functional in
Q=q^{2}x\left(1-x\right)+\left(m_{1}^{2}-m_{2}^{2}\right)x-m_{1}^{2}.

To start with, we combine the denominators with Feynman parametrization for
F_{a}^{\mu\nu}

\frac{K_{1}^{\mu}K_{1}^{\nu}A_{1}}{D_{\lambda}D_{12}}=2\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}x_{1}\int_{0}^{1-x_{1}}\mathrm{d}x_{2}\frac{1}{\left[\left(D_{2}-D_{\lambda}\right)x_{1}+\left(D_{1}-D_{\lambda}\right)x_{2}+D_{\lambda}\right]^{3}}.

(B.18)

Integrating into the loop momentum and making the shift
k\rightarrow k-\left(k_{2}x_{1}+k_{1}x_{2}\right)
, we reach to

F_{a}^{\mu\nu}=\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{2}}\frac{K_{1}^{\mu}K_{1}^{\nu}A_{1}}{D_{\lambda}D_{12}}=2\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}x_{1}\int_{0}^{1-x_{1}}\mathrm{d}x_{2}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{2}}\frac{\left[K_{1}^{\mu}K_{1}^{\nu}A_{1}\right]_{k-\left(k_{2}x_{1}+k_{1}x_{2}\right)}}{\left(k^{2}+Q\right)^{3}},

(B.19)

where the
Q
polynomial is given by

\displaystyle Q\left(k_{2},k_{1},x_{1},x_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle k_{2}^{2}x_{1}\left(1-x_{1}\right)+k_{1}^{2}x_{2}\left(1-x_{2}\right)-2\left(k_{2}\cdot k_{1}\right)x_{1}x_{2}

(B.20)

\displaystyle+\left(\lambda^{2}-m_{2}^{2}\right)x_{1}+\left(\lambda^{2}-m_{1}^{2}\right)x_{2}-\lambda^{2}.

The integration limits satisfies

\displaystyle Q\left(x_{1},1-x_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q^{2}x_{1}\left(1-x_{1}\right)+\left(m_{1}^{2}-m_{2}^{2}\right)x_{1}-m_{1}^{2}

(B.21)

\displaystyle Q\left(x_{1},0\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle k_{2}^{2}x_{1}\left(1-x_{1}\right)+\left(\lambda^{2}-m_{2}^{2}\right)x_{1}-\lambda^{2}.

(B.22)

Recovering definition of
A_{i}=2k\cdot k_{i}+k_{i}^{2}+\lambda^{2}-m_{i}^{2}.
After shifting, it assumes the form

\left(A_{1}\right)_{k-\left(k_{2}x_{1}+k_{1}x_{2}\right)}=\left(2k\cdot k_{1}\right)+\frac{\partial Q}{\partial x_{2}}.

(B.23)

This feature will always happen to some
A_{i}
, which means one factor
becomes a sum of a bilinear and a derivative about the last integration
parameter. The next stage is to make partial integrations until all
derivatives are consumed.

For the vector
K_{1}
that we used as reference (although any other could
be chosen) in definitions of the integral, under shifting, it turns into
\left(K_{1}\right)_{k-\left(k_{2}x_{1}+k_{1}x_{2}\right)}=k-\left(k_{2}x_{1}+k_{1}x_{2}-k_{1}\right).
Moreover, in order to simplify and
organize, we define

\displaystyle L

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(k_{2}x_{1}+k_{1}x_{2}-k_{1}\right)

(B.24)

\displaystyle L\left(x_{1},1-x_{1}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(k_{2}-k_{1}\right)x_{1}=qx

(B.25)

\displaystyle L\left(x_{1},0\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle L_{0}=\left(k_{2}x_{1}-k_{1}\right)

(B.26)

Gathering all the elements, we are left with this expression to integrate

F_{a}^{\mu\nu}=2\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}x_{1}\int_{0}^{1-x_{1}}\mathrm{d}x_{2}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{2}}\left\{\left[2k\cdot k_{1}+\frac{\partial Q}{\partial x_{2}}\right]\frac{\left(k-L\right)^{\mu}\left(k-L\right)^{\nu}}{\left(k^{2}+Q\right)^{3}}\right\}.

(B.27)

At this point, we use the results that are elaborated in the sequel, namely

\displaystyle\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{2}}\frac{1}{\left(k^{2}+Q\right)^{3}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{i}{4\pi}\frac{1}{2Q^{2}}

(B.28)

\displaystyle\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{2}}\frac{k^{\mu}k^{\nu}}{\left(k^{2}+Q\right)^{3}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{i}{4\pi}\frac{1}{2}g^{\mu\nu}\frac{1}{2Q},

(B.29)

odd integrals drop from the expression, and we get

F_{a}^{\mu\nu}=\frac{i}{4\pi}\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}x_{1}\int_{0}^{1-x_{1}}\mathrm{d}x_{2}\left[-\left(k_{1}^{\mu}L^{\nu}+k_{1}^{\nu}L^{\mu}\right)\frac{1}{Q}+\frac{1}{2}g^{\mu\nu}\frac{\partial Q}{\partial x_{2}}\frac{1}{Q}+L^{\mu}L^{\nu}\frac{\partial Q}{\partial x_{2}}\frac{1}{Q^{2}}\right].

(B.30)

Integrating by parts, we find a total derivative

F_{a}^{\mu\nu}=\frac{i}{4\pi}\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}x_{1}\int_{0}^{1-x_{1}}\mathrm{d}x_{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{2}}\left[\frac{1}{2}g^{\mu\nu}\log\frac{Q}{-\lambda^{2}}-L^{\mu}L^{\nu}\frac{1}{Q}\right]

(B.31)

that gives us

\displaystyle F_{a}^{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{i}{4\pi}\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}x_{1}\left[\frac{1}{2}g^{\mu\nu}\log\frac{Q\left(x_{1},1-x_{1}\right)}{-\lambda^{2}}-q^{\mu}q^{\nu}\frac{x^{2}}{Q\left(x_{1},1-x_{1}\right)}\right]

(B.32)

\displaystyle-\frac{i}{4\pi}\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}x_{1}\left[\frac{1}{2}g^{\mu\nu}\log\frac{Q\left(x_{1},0\right)}{-\lambda^{2}}-L_{0}^{\mu}L_{0}^{\nu}\frac{1}{Q\left(x_{1},0\right)}\right]

recalling that

\displaystyle Q\left(x_{1},1-x_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q^{2}x_{1}\left(1-x_{1}\right)+\left(m_{1}^{2}-m_{2}^{2}\right)x_{1}-m_{1}^{2}

(B.33)

\displaystyle Q\left(x_{1},0\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle k_{2}^{2}x_{1}\left(1-x_{1}\right)+\left(\lambda^{2}-m_{2}^{2}\right)x_{1}-\lambda^{2}.

(B.34)

The other integral is easily expressed in the form

F_{b}^{\mu\nu}=\frac{i}{4\pi}\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}x_{1}\left(1-x_{1}\right)\left[-\left(k_{2}^{\mu}L_{0}^{\nu}+k_{2}^{\nu}L_{0}^{\mu}\right)\frac{1}{Q}+\frac{1}{2}g^{\mu\nu}\frac{1}{Q}\frac{\partial Q}{\partial x_{1}}+L_{0}^{\mu}L_{0}^{\nu}\frac{1}{Q^{2}}\frac{\partial Q}{\partial x_{1}}\right].

(B.35)

Here the argument of polynomial is
Q\left(x_{1},0\right)
. Thus, partial
integration follows

F_{b}^{\mu\nu}=\frac{i}{4\pi}\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}x_{1}\left[\frac{1}{2}g^{\mu\nu}\log\frac{Q\left(x_{1},0\right)}{-\lambda^{2}}-\frac{L_{0}^{\mu}L_{0}^{\nu}}{Q\left(x_{1},0\right)}\right]+\frac{i}{4\pi}\frac{k_{1}^{\mu}k_{1}^{\nu}}{\left(-\lambda^{2}\right)},

(B.36)

again taking into account that
L\left(x_{1},0\right)=L_{0}=\left(k_{2}x_{1}-k_{1}\right)
.

Finally, summing both contributions
F_{a}^{\mu\nu}
and
F_{b}^{\mu\nu}
, plus a external-momentum independent finite piece

\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{2}}\frac{k_{1}^{\mu}k_{1}^{\nu}}{D_{\lambda}^{2}}=\frac{i}{4\pi}\frac{k_{1}^{\mu}k_{1}^{\nu}}{\left(-\lambda^{2}\right)},

(B.37)

follows the complete integration of finite parts. The organization of tensor
integral for general masses give us the result

\bar{J}_{2}^{\mu\nu}=\frac{1}{2}\left[\Delta_{2}^{\mu\nu}+g^{\mu\nu}I_{\log}\left(\lambda^{2}\right)\right]+\frac{i}{4\pi}\left[-\frac{1}{2}g^{\mu\nu}Z_{0}^{\left(0\right)}+q^{\mu}q^{\nu}Z_{2}^{\left(-1\right)}\right].

(B.38)

Any other integral in this thesis can be obtained with the computational
elements illustrated here.

B.3
Integration in the loop momentum

After Feynman parametrization, all integrals assume the form of the rational
functions

\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{n}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{n}}\frac{\left(1,k_{\mu},k_{\mu\nu},k_{\mu\nu\rho},...\right)}{\left(k^{2}+Q\right)^{\alpha}}.

(B.39)

To solve the integral, we start with the form

I\left(k,M,n\right)=\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{n}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{n}}\frac{1}{\left(k^{2}-M^{2}\right)^{\alpha}}=\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{n}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{n}}\frac{1}{\left(k^{2}-2k\cdot q+Q\right)^{\alpha}},

(B.40)

where
2\alpha>n
and
M^{2}=q^{2}-Q.
The auxiliary variable
q
helps to
develop the tensor integrals. The integration measure
\mathrm{d}^{n}k=\mathrm{d}^{n-1}k\mathrm{d}k_{0}
. The square the momentum loop
k^{2}=k_{0}^{2}-\mathbf{k}^{2};
and
\mathbf{k}^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}k_{i}^{2}.
The integral (
B.40
) only

\displaystyle I\left(Q,n\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{n}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{n}}\frac{1}{\left(k^{2}-M^{2}\right)^{\alpha}}=\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{n-1}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{n}}\left[\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\mathrm{d}k_{0}f\left(k_{0}\right)\right]

(B.41)

\displaystyle f\left(k_{0}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[k_{0}^{2}-(\sqrt{\mathbf{k}^{2}+M^{2}}-i\varepsilon)^{2}\right]^{-\alpha},

(B.42)

\displaystyle f\left(k_{0}\right)

\displaystyle\sim

\displaystyle\frac{1}{k_{0}^{2\alpha}},\text{ as }k_{0}^{2}\rightarrow\infty.

(B.43)

The poles and prescription coming from Feynman propagators

\displaystyle k_{0}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{\mathbf{k}^{2}+M^{2}}-i\varepsilon

(B.44)

\displaystyle k_{0}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\sqrt{\mathbf{k}^{2}+M^{2}}+i\varepsilon.

(B.45)

To compute the integral, we extend the integration for
k_{0}\in\mathbb{C}

and consider the following contour
C=C_{1}+C_{2}+C_{3}+C_{4}
in the figure
below

Figure B.1:
Contour of integration

Then take the integral over that contour

F_{C}(\mathbf{k}^{2},M^{2})=\int_{C}\mathrm{d}k_{0}f\left(k_{0}\right)=\left(\int_{C_{1}}+\int_{C_{2}}+\int_{C_{3}}+\int_{C_{4}}\right)\mathrm{d}k_{0}f\left(k_{0}\right)=0

(B.46)

since there are no poles inside the closed path of integration. We write the
integral as
F_{C}=F_{1}+F_{2}+F_{3}+F_{4}
; the semi-circle contributions
vanish in the limit
\lim_{R\rightarrow\infty}\left(F_{C_{3}}+F_{C_{4}}\right)=0.
The reminder contribution gives the desired
relation

\lim_{R\rightarrow\infty}F_{C_{1}}=-\lim_{R\rightarrow\infty}F_{C_{2}}\rightarrow\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}k_{0}f\left(k_{0}\right)=-\int_{+i\infty}^{-i\infty}\mathrm{d}k_{0}f\left(k_{0}\right).

(B.47)

Changing the integration variable in the last integral over the imaginary
axis by adopting
k_{0}=ik_{0}^{\prime},
we may write

I\left(Q,n\right)=\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{n-1}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{n-1}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}k_{0}\frac{1}{\left(k_{0}^{2}-\mathbf{k}^{2}-M^{2}\right)^{\alpha}}=\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{n-1}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{n-1}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}k_{0}^{\prime}\frac{i}{\left(-k_{0}^{\prime 2}-\mathbf{k}^{2}-M^{2}\right)^{\alpha}}

(B.48)

and effectively we have an euclidean signature
(k^{\prime 2}:=k_{0}^{\prime 2}+\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}k_{i}^{2})
to perform the integral

I\left(Q,n\right)=i\left(-1\right)^{\alpha}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{n}k^{\prime}}{\left(2\pi\right)^{n}}\frac{1}{\left(k^{\prime 2}+M^{2}\right)^{\alpha}}.

(B.49)

Now we introduce spherical coordinates to these variables and split the
radius and solid angle integrations

I\left(Q,n\right)=\frac{i\left(-1\right)^{\alpha}}{\left(2\pi\right)^{n}}\int_{S^{n-1}}\mathrm{d}\Omega\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}rr^{n-1}\frac{1}{\left(r^{2}+M^{2}\right)^{\alpha}}.

(B.50)

The solid angle furnish

\frac{1}{\left(2\pi\right)^{n}}\int_{S^{n-1}}\mathrm{d}\Omega=\frac{2}{\left(4\pi\right)^{n/2}\Gamma\left(\frac{n}{2}\right)}

(B.51)

and simple manipulations bring the form

I\left(Q,n\right)=\frac{2i\left(-1\right)^{\alpha}}{\left(4\pi\right)^{n/2}\Gamma\left(\frac{n}{2}\right)}\frac{1}{2M^{2\left(\alpha-n/2\right)}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}\left(r^{\prime 2}\right)\left(r^{\prime 2}\right)^{\left(n-2\right)/2}\left(r^{\prime 2}+1\right)^{-\alpha}.

(B.52)

Another variables change
r^{\prime 2}=\left(1-y\right)/y\rightarrow\mathrm{d}\left(r^{\prime 2}\right)=-\mathrm{d}y1/y^{2},
the Beta
function is

B\left(\alpha-\frac{n}{2},\frac{n}{2}\right)=\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}yy^{\left(\alpha-n/2\right)-1}\left(1-y\right)^{n/2-1}.

We have

\displaystyle I\left(Q,n\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{i\left(-1\right)^{\alpha}}{\left(4\pi\right)^{n/2}\Gamma\left(\frac{n}{2}\right)M^{2\left(\alpha-n/2\right)}}\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}yy^{\left(\alpha-n/2\right)-1}\left(1-y\right)^{n/2-1}

(B.53)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{i\left(-1\right)^{\alpha}}{\left(4\pi\right)^{n/2}}\frac{\Gamma\left(\alpha-\frac{n}{2}\right)}{\Gamma\left(\alpha\right)M^{2\left(\alpha-n/2\right)}},

(B.54)

thus, from
M^{2}=q^{2}-Q
follows

I\left(Q,n\right)=\frac{i\left(-1\right)^{\alpha}}{\left(4\pi\right)^{n/2}}\frac{\Gamma\left(\alpha-\frac{n}{2}\right)}{\Gamma\left(\alpha\right)\left(q^{2}-Q\right)^{\alpha-n/2}}.

(B.55)

Now taking derivatives concerning the variable q on both sides and shifting
the parameters
\alpha\rightarrow\alpha-1
in the form

I\left(Q,n\right)=\frac{i}{\left(-4\pi\right)^{n/2}}\frac{\Gamma\left(\alpha-n/2\right)}{\Gamma\left(\alpha\right)\left(Q-q^{2}\right)^{\left(\alpha-n/2\right)}},

(B.56)

the explicit derivative is

\frac{\partial I}{\partial q^{\mu_{1}}}=\frac{i}{\left(-4\pi\right)^{n/2}}\frac{2q_{\mu_{1}}\Gamma\left(\alpha-n/2+1\right)}{\Gamma\left(\alpha\right)\left(Q-q^{2}\right)^{\alpha+1-n/2}}=\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{n}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{n}}\frac{2\alpha k_{\mu_{1}}}{\left(k^{2}-2k\cdot q+Q\right)^{\alpha+1}},

(B.57)

follows the relation

\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{n}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{n}}\frac{k_{\mu_{1}}}{\left(k^{2}-2k\cdot q+Q\right)^{\alpha}}=\frac{i}{\left(-4\pi\right)^{n/2}}q_{\mu_{1}}\frac{\Gamma\left(\alpha-n/2\right)}{\Gamma\left(\alpha\right)\left(Q-q^{2}\right)^{\alpha-n/2}}.

(B.58)

Recursively

\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{n}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{n}}\frac{k_{\mu_{2}}k_{\mu_{1}}}{\left(k^{2}+Q\right)^{\alpha}}=\frac{i}{\left(-4\pi\right)^{n/2}}\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu_{12}}\frac{\Gamma\left(\alpha-n/2-1\right)}{\Gamma\left(\alpha\right)Q^{\alpha-n/2-1}}.

(B.59)

From the formulae presented, it is possible to obtain a general result,
adopting
n=2\omega
, which reads

\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2\omega}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{2\omega}}\frac{k_{\mu_{1}}\cdots k_{\mu_{2l+1}}}{\left(k^{2}+Q\right)^{\alpha}}=0

(B.60)

\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2\omega}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{2\omega}}\frac{k_{\mu_{1}}\cdots k_{\mu_{2l}}}{\left(k^{2}+Q\right)^{\alpha}}=\frac{i}{\left(4\pi\right)^{\omega}}\frac{1}{2^{l}}g_{(\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}\cdots g_{\mu_{2l-1},\mu_{2l})}\frac{\Gamma\left(\alpha-\omega-l\right)}{\Gamma\left(\alpha\right)Q^{\alpha-\omega-l}}

(B.61)

It is interesting to note that these results imply in the properties:

\displaystyle\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2\omega}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{2\omega}}f\left(k^{2}\right)k_{\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

(B.62)

\displaystyle\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2\omega}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{2\omega}}k_{\mu}k_{\nu}f\left(k^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{g_{\mu\nu}}{2\omega}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2\omega}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{2\omega}}k^{2}f\left(k^{2}\right)

(B.63)

\displaystyle\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2\omega}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{2\omega}}k_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}f\left(k^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\left(g_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}+g_{\mu\alpha\nu\beta}+g_{\mu\beta\nu\alpha}\right)}{4\left(\omega+1\right)}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2\omega}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{2\omega}}k^{4}f\left(k^{2}\right).

(B.64)

Appendix C
The One point Integrals in Two Dimensions

After performing the Dirac traces present in the definitions we established
for the perturbative amplitudes in (
7.33
) and (
7.34
), their
integrals naturally decompose in Feynman integrals that we define in the
equations (
3.52
) and (
3.53
). The calculations follow the IReg
method by applying the separation identity (
3.4
) on the divergent
integrals. The finite part is integrated and projected in definitions (
3.27
) and (
3.28
). The residual divergent part is projected onto
divergent objects of the set, expressed in (
3.5
) and their
relations in the session (
3.1
).

We start with integrals that have only one propagator. These have only
divergent structures. The finite parts after separating the labels, cancel
out when they are integrated.

Integral
J_{1}:
by power-counting this integral has a superficial
degree of divergence is logarithmic

\bar{J}_{1}\left(k_{i}\right)=I_{\log}

(C.1)

From the next integral, it is necessary to specify the
k_{1}
and
k_{2}

labels of the integral.

Integral
J_{1\mu_{1}}:
superficial degree of divergence is linear

\displaystyle 2\bar{J}_{1\mu_{1}}\left(k_{1}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\left(P-q\right)^{\nu_{1}}\Delta_{2\mu_{1}\nu_{1}}

(C.2)

\displaystyle 2\bar{J}_{1\mu_{1}}\left(k_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\left(P+q\right)^{\nu_{1}}\Delta_{2\mu_{1}\nu_{1}}

(C.3)

Integral
J_{1\mu_{12}}:
superficial degree of divergence is
quadratic

\displaystyle\bar{J}_{1\mu_{12}}\left(k_{1}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left(\Delta_{1\mu_{12}}+g_{\mu_{12}}I_{\mathrm{quad}}\right)-\frac{1}{8}\left(P-q\right)^{2}\Delta_{2\mu_{12}}

(C.4)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{8}\left(P-q\right)^{\nu_{1}}\left[\left(P-q\right)^{\nu_{2}}W_{3\mu_{12}\nu_{12}}-2\left(P-q\right)_{(\mu_{1}}\Delta_{2\mu_{2})\nu_{1}}\right]

\displaystyle\bar{J}_{1\mu_{12}}\left(k_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left(\Delta_{1\mu_{12}}+g_{\mu_{12}}I_{\mathrm{quad}}\right)-\frac{1}{8}\left(P+q\right)^{2}\Delta_{2\mu_{12}}

(C.5)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{8}\left(P+q\right)^{\nu_{1}}\left[\left(P+q\right)^{\nu_{2}}W_{3\mu_{12}\nu_{12}}-2\left(P+q\right)_{(\mu_{1}}\Delta_{2\mu_{2})\nu_{1}}\right]

Integral
J_{1\mu_{123}}:
superficial degree of divergence is
cubic and are the integrals with the highest power-counting

\displaystyle J_{1\mu_{123}}\left(k_{1}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4}\left(P-q\right)^{\nu_{1}}W_{2\mu_{123}\nu_{1}}+\frac{1}{4}\left(P-q\right)_{(\mu_{1}}\Delta_{1\mu_{23})}

(C.6)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{48}\left(P-q\right)^{\nu_{1}}\left(P-q\right)^{\nu_{2}}\left(P-q\right)^{\nu_{3}}W_{4\mu_{123}\nu_{123}}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{16}\left(P-q\right)^{\nu_{1}}\left(P-q\right)^{\nu_{2}}\left(P-q\right)_{(\mu_{1}}W_{3\mu_{23})\nu_{12}}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{16}\left(P-q\right)^{2}\left[\left(P+p\right)^{\nu_{1}}W_{3\mu_{123}\nu_{1}}-\left(P-q\right)_{(\mu_{1}}\Delta_{2\mu_{23})}\right]

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{8}\left(P-q\right)^{\nu_{1}}\left(P-q\right)_{(\mu_{1}}\left(P-q\right)_{\mu_{2}}\Delta_{2\mu_{3})\nu_{1}}

\displaystyle J_{1\mu_{123}}\left(k_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4}\left(P+q\right)^{\nu_{1}}W_{2\mu_{123}\nu_{1}}+\frac{1}{4}\left(P+q\right)_{(\mu_{1}}\Delta_{1\mu_{23})}

(C.7)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{48}\left(P+q\right)^{\nu_{1}}\left(P+q\right)^{\nu_{2}}\left(P+q\right)^{\nu_{3}}W_{4\mu_{123}\nu_{123}}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{16}\left(P+q\right)^{\nu_{1}}\left(P+q\right)^{\nu_{2}}\left(P+q\right)_{(\mu_{1}}W_{3\mu_{23})\nu_{12}}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{16}\left(P+q\right)^{2}\left[\left(P+q\right)^{\nu_{1}}W_{3\mu_{123}\nu_{1}}-\left(P+q\right)_{(\mu_{1}}\Delta_{2\mu_{23})}\right]

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{8}\left(P+q\right)^{\nu_{1}}\left(P+q\right)_{(\mu_{1}}\left(P+q\right)_{\mu_{2}}\Delta_{2\mu_{3})\nu_{1}}.

For instance, we calculated the
J_{1}^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}\left(k_{i}\right)
. The complete expression:

\displaystyle J_{1}^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}\left(k_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{2}}\frac{k^{\mu_{1}}k^{\mu_{2}}}{D_{i}}

(C.8)

\displaystyle+\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{2}}\left(k_{i}^{\mu_{1}}k^{\mu_{2}}+k_{i}^{\mu_{2}}k^{\mu_{1}}\right)\frac{1}{D_{i}}

\displaystyle+k_{i}^{\mu_{1}}k_{i}^{\mu_{2}}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{2}}\frac{1}{D_{i}}.

Using the expansions for two first integral above, we have

\displaystyle\left[\frac{k^{\mu_{1}}k^{\mu_{2}}}{D_{i}}\right]_{\mathrm{even}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{k^{\mu_{1}}k^{\mu_{2}}}{D_{\lambda}}-k_{i}^{2}\frac{k^{\mu_{1}}k^{\mu_{2}}}{D_{\lambda}^{2}}+4k_{i\nu_{12}}\frac{k^{\mu_{1}}k^{\mu_{2}}k^{\nu_{1}}k^{\nu_{2}}}{D_{\lambda}^{3}}

(C.9)

\displaystyle\left[\frac{k^{\mu_{1}}}{D_{i}}\right]_{\mathrm{even}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2k_{i\nu_{1}}\frac{k^{\mu_{1}}k^{\nu_{1}}}{D_{\lambda}^{2}}.

(C.10)

So the expanded integral is given by

\displaystyle J_{1}^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}\left(k_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{2}}\left(\frac{k^{\mu_{1}}k^{\mu_{2}}}{D_{\lambda}}-k_{i}^{2}\frac{k^{\mu_{1}}k^{\mu_{2}}}{D_{\lambda}^{2}}+4k_{i\nu_{1}}k_{i\nu_{2}}\frac{k^{\mu_{1}}k^{\mu_{2}}k^{\nu_{1}}k^{\nu_{2}}}{D_{\lambda}^{3}}\right)

(C.11)

\displaystyle-2k_{i\nu_{1}}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{2}}\left(k_{i}^{\mu_{1}}\frac{k^{\mu_{2}}k^{\nu_{1}}}{D_{\lambda}^{2}}+k_{i}^{\mu_{2}}\frac{k^{\mu_{1}}k^{\nu_{1}}}{D_{\lambda}^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle+k_{i}^{\mu_{1}}k_{i}^{\mu_{2}}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{2}}\frac{1}{D_{i}}.

Identifying the divergent objects in Section (
3.1
)

\displaystyle\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{2}}\frac{8k^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\nu_{1}\nu_{2}}}{D_{\lambda}^{3}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle W_{3}^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\nu_{1}\nu_{2}}+g^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\nu_{1}\nu_{2}}I_{\text{{log}}}

\displaystyle W_{3}^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\nu_{1}\nu_{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\square_{3}^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\nu_{1}\nu_{2}}+\frac{1}{2}g^{(\mu_{1}\nu_{1}}\Delta_{2}^{\mu_{2}\nu_{2})}

\displaystyle\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{2}}\frac{2k^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}}{D_{\lambda}^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Delta_{2}^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}+g^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}I_{\text{{log}}}

\displaystyle\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{2}}\frac{2k^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}}{D_{\lambda}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Delta_{1}^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}+g^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}I_{\text{{quad}}}.

Substituting in (
C.11
), we can see the scalars
I_{\log}
cancel
and remains the final expression

\displaystyle J_{1}^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}\left(k_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left[\Delta_{1}^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}+g^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}I_{\mathrm{quad}}\right]+\frac{1}{2}k_{i\nu_{12}}W_{3}^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\nu_{1}\nu_{2}}

(C.12)

\displaystyle-k_{i}^{\mu_{1}}k_{i\nu_{1}}\Delta_{2}^{\mu_{2}\nu_{1}}-k_{i}^{\mu_{2}}k_{i\nu_{1}}\Delta_{2}^{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}}-\frac{1}{2}k_{i}^{2}\Delta_{2}^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}.

The expression above can be written as (
C.4
) and
C.5
replacing
the routing
k_{i}
by
2k_{1}=\left(P-q\right)
or
2k_{2}=\left(P+q\right).

Appendix D
Function
Z_{k}^{\left(-1\right)}\left(q^{2},m_{1}^{2},m_{2}^{2}\right)

As we saw throughout the text, it is sometimes interesting to consider
explicit forms of these functions due to their importance in discussing some
important aspects of amplitudes. So we consider the following function

Z_{k}^{\left(-1\right)}\left(q^{2},m_{1}^{2},m_{2}^{2}\right)\equiv\int_{0}^{1}d\mathrm{z}\frac{z^{k}}{Q\left(q^{2},m_{1}^{2},m_{2}^{2}\right)},

where
Q=q^{2}z\left(1-z\right)+\left(m_{1}^{2}-m_{2}^{2}\right)z-m_{1}^{2}
is the polynomial form of denominator. Since all the functions
Z_{k}^{\left(-1\right)}
can be put in terms of the functions
Z_{0}^{\left(-1\right)},
we will consider in this appendix the
calculation explicitly only of the function, defined by

Z_{0}^{\left(-1\right)}\left(q^{2},m_{1}^{2},m_{2}^{2}\right)=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{1}{Q}.

(D.1)

One way to integrate is to write the polynomial present in the denominator
through its roots. We do

Q=-q^{2}\left[z^{2}-\frac{1}{q^{2}}\left(q^{2}+m_{1}^{2}-m_{2}^{2}\right)z+\frac{m_{1}^{2}}{q^{2}}\right]=-q^{2}\left(z-\alpha\right)\left(z-\beta\right).

(D.2)

Where the roots of the polynomial are
\alpha
and
\beta
given by

\displaystyle\alpha

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\left(q^{2}+m_{1}^{2}-m_{2}^{2}\right)+\sqrt{\left(q^{2}+m_{1}^{2}-m_{2}^{2}\right)^{2}-4m_{1}^{2}q^{2}}}{2q^{2}};

(D.3)

\displaystyle\beta

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{q^{2}+m_{1}^{2}-m_{2}^{2}-\sqrt{\left(q^{2}+m_{1}^{2}-m_{2}^{2}\right)^{2}-4m_{1}^{2}q^{2}}}{2q^{2}},

(D.4)

where
\alpha
and
\beta
satisfy the following relations:

\displaystyle\alpha+\beta

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\left(q^{2}+m_{1}^{2}-m_{2}^{2}\right)}{q^{2}};\qquad\alpha\beta=\frac{m_{1}^{2}}{q^{2}}

(D.5)

\displaystyle\alpha-\beta

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{\left(q^{2}+m_{1}^{2}-m_{2}^{2}\right)^{2}-4m_{1}^{2}q^{2}}}{q^{2}}.

(D.6)

Rewriting Eq. (
D.1
) as

Z_{0}^{\left(-1\right)}=-\frac{1}{q^{2}}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{1}{\left(z-\alpha\right)\left(z-\beta\right)}=-\frac{1}{q^{2}}\frac{1}{\alpha-\beta}\int_{0}^{1}d\mathrm{z}\left[\frac{1}{\left(z-\alpha\right)}-\frac{1}{\left(z-\beta\right)}\right].

(D.7)

Using the passage

\int_{0}^{1}d\mathrm{z}\frac{1}{\left(z-\alpha\right)}=\ln\left(1-\alpha\right)-\ln\left(-\alpha\right)=\ln\left(\frac{\alpha-1}{\alpha}\right),

(D.8)

we will have

Z_{0}^{\left(-1\right)}=-\frac{1}{q^{2}}\frac{1}{\alpha-\beta}\left\{\ln\left(\frac{\alpha-1}{\alpha}\right)-\ln\left(\frac{\beta-1}{\beta}\right)\right\}=\frac{1}{q^{2}}\frac{1}{\alpha-\beta}\left[\ln\left(\frac{\alpha-1}{\alpha}\right)\left(\frac{\beta}{\beta-1}\right)\right].

(D.9)

From that, we can write the explicit form for the function
Z_{0}^{\left(-1\right)},

Z_{0}^{\left(-1\right)}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\left(q^{2}+m_{1}^{2}-m_{2}^{2}\right)^{2}-4m_{1}^{2}q^{2}}}\ln\left[\frac{\left(m_{1}^{2}+m_{2}^{2}-q^{2}\right)+\sqrt{\left(q^{2}+m_{1}^{2}-m_{2}^{2}\right)^{2}-4m_{1}^{2}q^{2}}}{\left(m_{1}^{2}+m_{2}^{2}-q^{2}\right)-\sqrt{\left(q^{2}+m_{1}^{2}-m_{2}^{2}\right)^{2}-4m_{1}^{2}q^{2}}}\right]

(D.10)

In the kinematical limit, where
q^{2}\ll 1,
we have the result

Z_{0}^{\left(-1\right)}=\frac{1}{\left(m_{1}^{2}-m_{2}^{2}\right)}\ln\left[\frac{\left(m_{1}^{2}-m_{2}^{2}\right)+\left(m_{1}^{2}-m_{2}^{2}\right)}{\left(m_{1}^{2}-m_{2}^{2}\right)-\left(m_{1}^{2}-m_{2}^{2}\right)}\right].

(D.11)

and
